# About the Authors

**Aristides Villarreal Bravo** lives in Panama, is a Java Developer, member of NetBeans Dream Teams since 2007, Jug Leaders. He is currently working on developing Java applications and with greater emphasis on technologies such as Java Enterprise Edition, Jakarta EE, Microprofile, and NoSQL databases.

He has developed several plugins for Apache NetBeans IDE and is working on his Jmoordb project, a Java API for NoSQL.

He has participated in many national and international conferences and has been a reviewer of several books on Java and related technologies. Member of JEspañol, winner of the Duke’s Choice Award in 2017.

Write many articles on your personal site **[http://avbravo.blogspot.com/](http://avbravo.blogspot.com/)**

Certified on Vaadin 14 framework, professional and developer. Leader of the Barranquilla-Colombia Java User Group ([www.jugbaq.org](http://www.jugbaq.org)), and has more than 12 years of experience in software development.

He has done lectures in international Java conferences using the latest practices and frameworks in the industry. He also writes in his blog about JVM related topics.

Your Blog links: **[https://geovanny0401.blogspot.com.co](https://geovanny0401.blogspot.com.co)**

He has experience mainly in persistence polyglot and high-performance applications in finances, social media, and e-commerce. Otavio is a member of both Expert Groups and Expert Leader in several JSRs and JCP executive committee. He is working on several Apache and Eclipse Foundation projects such as Apache Tamaya, Eclipse JNoSQL, Eclipse MicroProfile, JakartaEE. A JUG leader and global speaker at JavaOne and Devoxx conferences. # Preface

Web applications have evolved over the years. Java has been linked to business development since its inception. To achieve standardization and facilitate the development of applications of this type led to the birth of Java Enterprise Edition. This book introduces you to the main specifications that facilitate the creation of solutions for use in high-performance environments.

As the years passed, a movement was born for the implementation of new technology for databases that broke the traditional model, and these databases were grouped into what is known as NoSQL. Most of the chapters are oriented to their implementation using Jakarta NoSQL and Jmoordb that facilitate the use with Java.

Microservices are essential in the modern era for creating scalable applications. Microprofile.io is the reference in Java for their creation.

Some frameworks help us to create user interfaces. Among them we will learn about Java Server Faces, Vaadin, and Eclipse Krazo as important alternatives for application development.

We will end with topics such as application tracking and container testing, to give an overview of the main tools to consider when developing enterprise applications with Java.

We have developed ten chapters where you can find the following topics:

**[Chapter 1:](#c01.xhtml)** An Introduction to the Jakarta EE Platform. You will know the specifications with practical examples.

**[Chapter 2:](#c02.xhtml)** We explore the fundamentals of NoSQL databases and their advantages.

**[Chapter 3:](#c03.xhtml)** Jakarta NoSQL is a Jakarta EE specification that streamlines the integration of Java applications with NoSQL databases

**[Chapter 4:](#c04.xhtml)** Describes the Jmoordb API interacting with MongoDB a NoSQL databases.

**[Chapter 5:](#c05.xhtml)** The goal of the Eclipse MicroProfile initiative is to optimize Enterprise Java for the microservices architecture. It’s based on a subset of Jakarta EE APIs, so we can build MicroProfile applications the same way we build Jakarta EE ones.

**[Chapter 6:](#c06.xhtml)** Explore Java Server Faces and its integration with microservices and MongoDB.

**[Chapter 7:](#c07.xhtml)** Presents the Vaadin framework, which is designed to develop web applications in Java or JavaScript using Vaadin web components, allowing you to build a powerful user interface with just a few lines of code.

**[Chapter 8:](#c08.xhtml)** Describes how to develop a Java web application from scratch, designing user interface code to run securely on the server, right next to your business logic that you can access with the native Java API. Integrating the Vaadin framework with non-relational databases such as MongoDB.

**[Chapter 9:](#c09.xhtml)** It is an introduction to the new MVC Eclipse Krazo framework, and we set security to applications with Java security API and show how to use JWT simply.

**[Chapter 10:](#c10.xhtml)** Explains the use of containers, testing, continuous integration, DevOps as a fundamental part of the creation of robust and secure applications.

# [CHAPTER 1](#toc.xhtml_c01)

# [Jakarta EE Platform](#toc.xhtml_c01)

Today the Java EE/Jakarta EE platform has proven to be one of the leading platforms for developing cloud applications.

This chapter is an introduction to The Jakarta EE platform. It will present a feedback of its history of the platform, a set of specifications and simple examples that will help you to use the potential of the platform for building enterprise applications ready for use in the cloud.

Numbered APIs and frameworks will be used to demonstrate our goal: Jakarta NoSQL, Jmoordb, Vaadin, and Java Server Faces.

# [Structure](#toc.xhtml_s1a)

*   Jakarta EE platform
*   Context and Dependency Injection (CDI) 2.0 (JSR 365)
*   Enterprise Java Beans 3.2 (JSR-345)
*   Java Servlet 4.0 (JSR-369)
*   Java API for RESTful Web Services JAX-RS 2.1 (JSR-370)
*   Java EE Security API 1.0 (JSR -375)
*   The Java API for JSON Binding JSON-B 1.0 (JSR-367)
*   JavaServer Faces JSF 2.3 (JSR-372)
*   Java Persistence API 2.2 (JSR-338)

# [Objective](#toc.xhtml_s2a)

This chapter is an introduction to the APIs that are part of the Java Enterprise Edition platform and Jakarta EE.

# [Jakarta EE platform](#toc.xhtml_s3a)

Jakarta EE is a platform that offers a set of components and APIs for the development of Java business applications. It orients towards the support of HTML5, Enterprise Java Beans, Web Services, JSON, Databases, Microservices, WebSocket among others.

These components are based on the combination of specifications (JSR) coming from the ecosystem of JavaEE platform led by Java Community Process **([http://jcp.org](http://jcp.org))** but rebranded under Eclipse Jakarta EE and Eclipse Microprofile standards (**[https://microprofile.io/](https://microprofile.io/));** formed by companies, user groups, communities, which establish consensus on them, to be adaptable to the new requirements of the industry.

The APIs that are part of the specifications are known as **Java Specification Request (JSR).**

# [History](#toc.xhtml_s4a)

The Java EE platform has incorporated major changes in recent years, allowing a simpler development, performance improvements through reusable components resulting in lighter and scalable applications adapting to environments such as cloud, and Microservices.

We will briefly review the evolution of the platform over the last few years, showing each version along with the corresponding specifications.

The following table shows the different versions of the platform:

| **Version** | **Description** | **Date** |
| JPE Project | First version | May 1998 |
| J2EE 1.2 | Servlet, JSP,EJB,JMS,RMI,IIOP | Dec 12, 1999 |
| J2EE 1.3 | CMP, Connector Architecture | Sept 24, 2001 |
| J2EE 1.4 | Web Services, Mgmt, Deployment, Async Connector | Nov, 11, 2003 |
| Java EE 5 | Easy of Development Annotations, EJB 3.0, JPA, JSF, JAXB, JAX-WS, Stax, SAAJ | May 11, 2006. |
| Java EE 6 | Extensibility Profiles, Ease of Dev, EJB 3.1 Lite, RESTFull, CDI, JAX-RS | Dec 20, 2009 |
| Java EE 7 | JMS 2.0,Batch, Web Socket, JSON | May 28, 2013 |
| Java EE 8 | CDI 2.0, JSON-B 1.0, Java Servlet 4.0,JAX-RS 2.1, JSF 2.3,JSON-P 1.1, Java EE Security API 1.0, Bean Validation 2.0,Common Annotations 1.3, Java Persistence 2.2, Java API for WebSocket 1.1, Java Mail 1.6 | Sep 21, 2017 |

**Table 1.1**

# [From JavaEE to Jakarta EE](#toc.xhtml_s5a)

Over time companies and developer communities began to show concern for the evolution of the platform. Faced with the slowness in the evolution of specifications two important initiatives emerged in recent years; on the one hand, Microprofile (**[http://microprofile.io](http://microprofile.io)**) and on another one Java EE Guardian (**[https://javaee-guardians.io/](https://javaee-guardians.io/)).**

Microprofile brings an innovative standardization path to produce new APIs based on a consensus of large companies intending to optimize Java business applications to support Microservices. In 2017, *Reza Rahman* and *Dr. James Gosling,* created the Java EE Guardian formed by enthusiasts, to achieve a more open and agile process to implement new advances in the Java EE platform.

These efforts led Oracle to relinquish control of the Java EE platform to the Eclipse foundation. In what has been called Jakarta EE (**[https://jakarta.ee](https://jakarta.ee))**, whose purpose is focused on the active participation of the community to achieve progress more quickly and adapt to changing market needs.

Throughout this book we will develop a series of examples, so we specify some requirements that you must have installed on your computer.

We assume that the reader has prior knowledge of Java and the process of installing the development tools.

We will begin by briefly describing some of the JSR with code segments that will help you understand these APIs. If you already have experience with the Java EE platform, you will realize that they are very basic examples and concepts as they are aimed at new developers who want to know and develop with the Java EE/Jakarta EE platform.

You will see many definitions of the specification to guide the reader through the rules established through the JSR.

Above all let us mention some APIs grouped around the usage: web application, enterprise application, Web service and security management, microservices application.

# [Java EE/Jakarta EE application development](#toc.xhtml_s6a)

In this part, we will show you how to develop an application based Jakarta EE. Below a list of requirements we defined to develop our samples:

*   Java Development Kit
    *   JDK 8 or higher
*   Build Tools
    *   Maven or Gradle
*   Integrated Development Environment
    *   Apache NetBeans
        *   Plugin for Payara Micro
    *   Eclipse
    *   IntelliJ IDEA
*   NoSQL Databases
    *   MongoDB
*   Application Server
    *   Payara or any Jakarta EE certified application server
*   Dockers

The source code of the projects are available at the following link:

**[https://github.com/avbravo/Building-JakartaEE-Applications-Microservices](https://github.com/avbravo/Building-JakartaEE-Applications-Microservices)**

To create a Web project that supports the Jakarta EE 8 specification, you must just include your Maven project dependency:

`<dependency>`

`<groupId>jakarta.platform</groupId>`

`<artifactId>jakarta.jakartaee-api</artifactId>`

`<version>8.0.0</version>`

`</dependency>`

A Jakarta EE applications run in containers that provide a running environment for the components and APIs of the Jakarta EE platform.

Java EE defines four main container types which are the following:

*   **Application client container:** They are usually applications that run on the desktop, with a user interface and use the platform components.
*   **Applet container:** Manages the execution of applets.
*   **Web container**: Servlet. JSP, Java Server Faces are applications that run in a Web container using HTTP protocols.
*   **EJB container:** Manages the execution of Enterprise Java Beans, which runs inside a Java EE server.

# [Context and Dependency Injection CDI 2.0 (JSR 365)](#toc.xhtml_s7a)

**Context and Dependency Injection (CDI)** was introduced from Java EE 6, and the specification defines it as a set of services that allow objects to be linked to the context life cycle, injected, and associated with Interceptor and Decorator.

It allows interacting with events. Offers integration with Enterprise Java Beans components, Java Server Faces Managed Beans, Bean Validation. It supports asynchronous events.

Injection of dependencies can be performed at the level of:

*   Attributes
*   Constructor
*   Method

The permitted scoped are:

*   RequestScoped
*   SessionScoped
*   ApplicationScoped
*   ConversationScoped
*   DependentScoped

The specification defines some annotations that we will show in the following table:

| `@Named` | It is the standard qualifier that allows us to define beans to be used by injecting dependencies with other beans and with expression language. |
| `@Model` | It helps to define a stereotype that identifies a particular bean that usually defines the layer of the MVC model. |
| `@Inject` | Allows declaring fields, methods, and constructors of a bean. To be injected. |
| `@Producer` | A production method acts as a source of objects to be injected. These objects do not need to be instantiated, may vary in run time, and cannot be initialized in the bean constructor. |
| `@Qualifier` | Through the annotation `@Qualifier`, we create qualifiers that help us identify one or a group of classes of other classes, among the advantages it provides is that we have more readable code. |
| `@Intercepetor` | Allows cross-cutting transversals to be applied, these can be enabled or disabled at runtime. They intercept calls to methods, and we can specify calls to other methods before or after invoking a method. |
| `@Decorator` | They apply only to beans in a particular Java interface, and they are very similar to Interceptors. |
| `@Delegate` | Identifies the delegate which uses the Decorator to delegate method calls to the container. |

**Table 1.7**

The `@Model` annotation helps us define a model that we can use with other components:

`@Model`

`public class Dog {`

`private Integer id;`

`private String name;`

`//Setters/Getters`

`}`

`define @Qualifier Domestic:`

`@Qualifier`

`@Retention(RUNTIME)`

`@Target({METHOD, FIELD, PARAMETER, TYPE})`

`public @interface Domestic {`

`}`

The `@Qualifier` can be applied to a class or attribute definition levels:

`@Domestic`

`public class AnimalProcessor {`

`}`

Or by dependency injection:

`public class AnimalController{`

`@Inject`

`@Domestic`

`AnimalProcessor animalProcessor;`

`}`

To declare a Bean from a session bean we use the annotation `@SessionScoped:`

`@Named`

`@SessionScoped`

`public class MySession implements Serializable {`

`private Dog dog;`

`//Setters/Getters`

`}`

CDI allows easy integration with Java Server Faces components through **Expression Language (EL):**

`<h:form>`

`<h:outputLabel value=”Id”/>`

`<h:inputText value=”#{mySession.dog.id}”/>`

`</h:form>`

# [@Interceptor example](#toc.xhtml_s8a)

We’ll develop an example of an interceptor. When the `start`() method is executed through the interceptor, it will invoke the `authorize`() method defined in `ExerciseInterceptor`, and then the control is returned to the `start`() method.

![](images/68-1.jpg)

**Figure 1.1**

Steps to show the use of interceptor in a project that we will use as a very basic example:

1.  Firstly, we will define the `Model` as shown below:

    `@Model`

    `public class Person {`

    `private Integer id;`

    `private String name;`

    `private Boolean walk;`

    `public Person() {`

    `}`

    `//Setter/Getter`

    `}`

2.  Next, we will define the Feed interface for use with the interceptor:

    `Inherited`

    `@InterceptorBinding`

    `@Target({TYPE, METHOD})`

    `@Retention(RUNTIME)`

    `public @interface Exercise {`

    `}`

3.  We declare the `FeedInterceptor` to intercept the event of the method where we use the `@Exercise interface`:

    `@Exercise`

    `@Interceptor`

    `public class Exercisenterceptor {`

    `@Inject`

    `Person person;`

    `@AroundInvoke`

    `public Object authorize(InvocationContext ic) throws Exception {`

    `try {`

    `if (!person.getWalk()) {`

    `return ic.proceed();`

    `} else {`

    `throw new Exception();`

    `}`

    `} catch (Exception e) {`

    `throw e;`

    `}`

    `}`

    `}`

4.  Create the `beans.xml` file in the `WEB-INF` directory and specify the class that was created of `Interceptor` type:

    `<?xml version=”1.0” encoding=”UTF-8”?>`

    `<beans xmlns=”http://java.sun.com/xml/ns/javaee”`

    `xmlns:xsi=”http://www.w3.org/2001/XMLSchema-instance”`

    `xsi:schemaLocation=”http://java.sun.com/xml/ns/javaee http://java.sun.com/xml/ns/javaee/beans_1_0.xsd”>`

    `<interceptors>`

    `<class>com.book.mycdi.interceptor.ExerciseInterceptor</class>`

    `</interceptors>`

    `</beans>`

5.  Use the `@Exercise` annotation in the `start`() method to intercept invocations to this method that will be trapped by `ExerciseInterceptor`:

    `@Model`

    `public class ExerciseController implements Serializable {`

    `@Inject`

    `Person person;`

    `public Person getPerson() {`

    `return person;`

    `}`

    `public void setPerson(Person person) {`

    `this.person = person;`

    `}`

    `@Feed`

    `public String start() {`

    `try {`

    `System.out.println(“The person started the walk”);`

    `} catch (Exception e) {`

    `System.out.println(“error “ + e.getLocalizedMessage());`

    `}`

    `return “”;`

    `}`

    `}`

# [Enterprise Java Beans 3.2 (JSR-345)](#toc.xhtml_s9a)

The specification defines the **Enterprise Java Beans architecture (EJB)** as architecture for the development and deployment of components for enterprise applications.

The EJBs have as goals to be:

*   Standard component architecture for enterprise applications in Java.
*   Development, deployment and distribution of enterprise components in Java.
*   Development and deployment of Web services.
*   Make easy the creation of business applications.

The session types used by EJBs are:

*   Singleton
*   Stateful
*   Stateless

The components can be used simply utilizing annotations `@EJB:`

`@EJB`

`MyResources myResources;`

`This example implements a @Schedule that sends a notification every 10 minutes:`

`@Singleton`

`public class SendCongratulations {`

`@Schedule(minute = “*/10“, hour = “*“, persistent = false)`

`public void send() {`

`// …`

`}`

`}`

# [@Producer example](#toc.xhtml_s10a)

Create an Enterprise Java Beans project from NetBeans IDE to manage a connection to a MongoDB database using a `@Producer.`

Select from the menu **`File | New | Project`**. In **`Categories`**, select **`Java With Maven`** option and in Projects, select **`EJB Module`** option:

![](images/68-2.jpg)

**Figure 1.2**

Indicate the name of the project as `nosqlejb`

![](images/68-3.jpg)

**Figure 1.3**

In the following dialog we leave the **`Server at <No Server Selected>`** in our case because we are using Payara Micro from NetBeans, but if you have another server installed select from the list. And in Java EE Version select Java EE 8:

![](images/68-4.jpg)

**Figure 1.4**

At the end of the process, the project structure is generated with all the necessary files. Our next step is to configure it according to our requirements:

![](images/68-5.jpg)

**Figure 1.5**

Edit the `pom.xml` file and locate the `javaee-api` dependency:

`<dependency>`

`<groupId>javax</groupId>`

`<artifactId>javaee-api</artifactId>`

`<version>8.0</version>`

`<scope>provided</scope>`

`</dependency>`

And replace with Jakarta EE dependency:

`<dependency>`

`<groupId>jakarta.platform</groupId>`

`<artifactId>jakarta.jakartaee-api</artifactId>`

`<version>8.0.0</version>`

`<scope>provided</scope>`

`</dependency>`

Add the MongoDB Java driver, and it is important to remember that in the following chapters, will be used **JNoSQL** and **Jmoordb** to communicate our Java applications with NoSQL databases:

`<dependency>`

`<groupId>org.mongodb</groupId>`

`<artifactId>mongodb-driver</artifactId>`

`<version>3.6.0</version>`

`</dependency>`

From NetBeans IDE, select the project and then in the package `com.jakartaeebook.nosqleb`, right click and select **`New | Session Bean:`**

![](images/68-6.jpg)

**Figure 1.6**

Enter the **`EJB`** Name as **`MyProducer`** and in **`Session Type`**, select **`Singleton`** option:

![](images/68-7.jpg)

**Figure 1.7**

The IDE generates the `Singleton MyProducer.java,` where you can add the enterprise components:

`import javax.ejb.Singleton;`

`@Singleton`

`@LocalBean`

`public class MyProducer{`

`// Add business logic below. (Right-click in editor and choose`

`// “Insert Code > Add Business Method”)`

`}`

A `@Singleton` notation is used to define a single instance of a bean and this is controlled by the container.

The annotation `@ConcurrencyManagement` gives control to the EJB container controlling access to singleton methods through.

Blocking access to methods is possible using annotations for reading `@Lock(READ)` generally with methods that return a value and writing `@Lock(WRITE)`, blocking access to those methods to other clients.

Producer will return the connection to the database as a single instance for all clients, so only the `myConnection`() method needs to be injected:

`@Singleton`

`@ConcurrencyManagement(ConcurrencyManagementType.CONTAINER)`

`public class MyProducer {`

`private static final Logger LOG = Logger.getLogger(MyProducer.class.getName());`

`@Produces`

`@ApplicationScoped`

`public MongoClient myConnection() {`

`MongoClient mongo = new MongoClient(“localhost”, 27017);`

`try {`

`mongo = new MongoClient();`

`} catch (Exception e) {`

`LOG.severe(e.getLocalizedMessage());`

`}`

`return mongo;`

`}`

`}`

# [Java Servlet 4.0 (JSR-369)](#toc.xhtml_s11a)

The specification of Java Servlet (**[https://www.jcp.org/en/jsr/detail?id=369](https://www.jcp.org/en/jsr/detail?id=369)),** defines as:

*   **Servlet**: As a Java technology based on Web components, which run in a container and generate dynamic content.
*   **Servlet Container**: As part of an application Server or Web Server that provides network services through which requests/responses are sent among other functionalities. A Servlet container supports HTTP, HTTPS protocols.
*   **Servlet Context**: Defines a view of the Servlet of the Web application that is running the servlet.
*   **Request:** Encapsulates all the information on the client’s request.
*   **Response**: Encapsulates the information that is returned from the server to the client.

    Servlet allows us to define filters and manage sessions.

The `HttpServlet` class defines the methods that we will show in the following table:

| **HttpServlet** | **HTTP** |
| `doGet` | GET requests |
| `doPost` | POST requests |
| `doPut` | PUT requests |
| `doDelete` | DELETE requests |
| `doHead` | HEAD requests |
| `doOptions` | OPTIONS requests |
| `doTrace` | TRACE requests |

**Table 1.8**

A servlet is very easy to create using the `@WebServlet` annotation:

`@WebServlet(name = “Hello“, urlPatterns = {“/Hello“})`

`public class Hello extends HttpServlet {`

`protected void doGet(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response)`

`throws ServletException, IOException {`

`}`

`@Override`

`protected void doPost(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response)`

`throws ServletException, IOException {`

`}`

`}`

The HTTP/2 protocol offers new features over the HTTP/1 protocol that are supported by Jakarta EE 8:

*   Server push,
*   Request/Response multiplexing
*   Multiplexed stream.
*   Compression of request headers

# [Server Push](#toc.xhtml_s12a)

It is one of the most important improvements in HTTP2 aimed at improving performance. Since the server knows the resources requested by the client and can send them before being requested, imagine that the page would contain CSS, JS, images, the server can start sending.

`HttpServletRequest` offers `PushBuilder` that allows us to `push`() content from the server to the client taking advantage of the advances of the HTTP2 protocol.

Below we will show you how to create the application using NetBeans IDE, you can use your favorite IDE:

1.  Next, a Web project will be developed from NetBeans IDE. Select from the menu **`File | New | Project`**. In **`Categories`**, select **`Java With Maven`** option and in **`Projects`**, select **`Payara Micro Application`** option**:**

    ![](images/68-8.jpg)

    **Figure 1.8**

2.  Enter the name of the project as `myservlet`:

    ![](images/68-9.jpg)

    **Figure 1.9**

3.  On the server select **`Payara Micro Version`** as **`5.191`** (Or select the one you have installed as Java EE Server):

    ![](images/68-10.jpg)

    **Figure 1.10**

    At the end, the Web project is generated.

4.  Now we will proceed to create a servlet, select the package and right-click and select **`Categories`** as **`Web`** and in **`File Types`** select as **`Servlet`**:

    ![](images/68-11.jpg)

    **Figure 1.11**

5.  Enter the **`Class Name`** as `Hello`:

    ![](images/68-12.jpg)

    **Figure 1.12**

6.  In **`Configure Servlet Deployment`**, we define the **`Servlet Name`** as `Hello` and in the **`URL Patterns(s)`** enter /`Hello`, it is the path we will use to access the Servlet:

    ![](images/68-13.jpg)

    **Figure 1.13**

The generated Servlet will contain the `doGet()/doPost()` methods corresponding to GET/POST of the HTTP protocol:

*   `doGet(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response)`
*   `doPost(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response)`
*   `processRequest()` is a method that we will use to process the request/response and return an HTML to the client that will be interpreted by the browser:

    `@WebServlet(name = “Hello”, urlPatterns = {“/hello”})`

    `public class Hello extends HttpServlet {`

    `protected void processRequest(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response)`

    `throws ServletException, IOException {`

    `response.setContentType(“text/html;charset=UTF-8”);`

    `try (PrintWriter out = response.getWriter()) {`

    `/* TODO output your page here. You may use the following sample code. */`

    `out.println(“<!DOCTYPE html>”);`

    `out.println(“<html>”);`

    `out.println(“<head>”);`

    `out.println(“<title>Servlet Hello</title>”);`

    `out.println(“</head>”);`

    `out.println(“<body>”);`

    `out.println(“<h1>Servlet Hello at “ + request.getContextPath() + “</h1>”);`

    `out.println(“</body>”);`

    `out.println(“</html>”);`

    `}`

    `}`

    `@Override`

    `protected void doGet(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response)`

    `throws ServletException, IOException {`

    `processRequest(request, response);`

    `}`

    `@Override`

    `protected void doPost(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response)`

    `throws ServletException, IOException {`

    `processRequest(request, response);`

    `}`

    `@Override`

    `public String getServletInfo() {`

    `return “Short description”;`

    `}`

    `}`

Execute the project and open the Web browser and enter the address: `http://localhost:8080/myservlet/Hello`

You can see that the Servlet sends us a reply HTML:

![](images/68-14.jpg)

**Figure 1.14**

# [Example of a servlet with MongoDB](#toc.xhtml_s13a)

It consists of a servlet that communicates with a MongoDB database and displays a list of people and their photos in a `<table>` component.

We will apply the concepts learned from CDI, EJB and Servlet to inject the `@Producer myConnection()` that will return us the connection to the database. The documents will be processed through API JSON-B, to convert them to objects of the class `Person.java.` The example uses a MongoDB database called book and a collection called `Person`. You must create them and enter the documents, as shown below:

`{`

`“_id” : ObjectId(“5cfbe59606f2be9d8d6c667d”),`

`“id” : “78-96”,`

`“name” : “Ann”,`

`“photo” : “images/ann.png”`

`}`

`{`

`“_id” : ObjectId(“5cfbe5b306f2be9d8d6c667e”),`

`“id” : “4-74”,`

`“name” : “Jhon”,`

`“photo” : “images/jhon.jpg”`

`}`

In the project add the dependency `nosqlejb`:

`<dependency>`

`<groupId>com.book</groupId>`

`<artifactId>nosqlejb</artifactId>`

`<version>0.1</version>`

`</dependency>`

The components that will be used for the example are:

*   **Plain Object Java Object**: Person.java
*   **Servlet**: `PersonServlet`

Next, we will see the class Person given below:

`public class Person {`

`private String id;`

`private String name;`

`private String photo;`

`public Person() {}`

`//Getters Setters /methods`

`}`

In the servlet `PersonServlet`:

*   Inject `MongoClient`
*   Define a method `getMongoDatabase`() that returns the database.
*   The `processRequest()` method converts documents to the class Person.
*   `PushBuilder` takes advantage of the new HTTP/2 features and sends the image to the client prematurely.

The complete samples of code are available into the repository on GitHub. In the following code segment, we will create a Servlet called `PersonServlet`:

`@WebServlet(name = “PersonServlet”, urlPatterns = {“/PersonServlet”})`

`public class PersonServlet extends HttpServlet {`

`@Inject`

`MongoClient myConnection;`

`public MongoDatabase getMongoDatabase() {`

`try {`

`MongoDatabase db = myConnection.getDatabase(“book”);`

`return db;`

`} catch (Exception ex) {`

`Logger.getLogger(PersonServlet.class.getName()).log(Level.SEVERE, null, ex);`

`}`

`return null;`

`}`

`protected void processRequest(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response)`

`throws ServletException, IOException {`

`Jsonb jsonb = JsonbBuilder.create();`

`PushBuilder pb = request.newPushBuilder();`

`response.setContentType(“text/html;charset=UTF-8”);`

`try (PrintWriter out = response.getWriter()) {`

`/* TODO output your page here. You may use the following sample code. */`

`out.println(“<!DOCTYPE html>”);`

`out.println(“<html>”);`

`out.println(“<head>”);`

`out.println(“<title>Servlet PersonServlet</title>”);`

`out.println(“</head>”);`

`out.println(“<body>”);`

`out.println(“<h1> List of People</h1>”);`

`out.println(“<table style\”width:100%\”>”);`

`out.println(“<tr>”);`

`out.println(“ <th>id</th>”);`

`out.println(“ <th>name</th>”);`

`out.println(“</tr>”);`

`for (Document doc : getMongoDatabase().getCollection(“person”).find()) {`

`Person person = jsonb.fromJson(doc.toJson(), Person.class);`

`out.println(“<tr>”);`

`out.println(“ <th>” + person.getId() + “</th>”);`

`out.println(“ <th>” + person.getName() + “</th>”);`

`if (pb != null) {`

`pb.path(person.getPhoto())`

`.addHeader(“content-type”, person.getPhoto())`

`.push();`

`}`

`out.println(“ <th><img src=’” + person.getPhoto() + “’></th>”);`

`out.println(“</tr>”);`

`}`

`out.println(“</table>”);`

`out.println(“</body>”);`

`out.println(“</html>”);`

`}`

`…`

`}`

When we execute the project, we notice the Servlet connects to the database and returns the list of people with their respective photos.

**http://localhost:8080/myservelt/PersonServlet**

![](images/68-15.jpg)

**Figure 1.15**

# [Java API for RESTful Web services JAX-RS 2.1 (JSR-370)](#toc.xhtml_s14a)

It is an API for the creation of Web services using as style **Representational State Transfer (REST)**. It’s main components are:

*   Resources
*   Suppliers
*   **Configuration**: Through a subclass of application to load the resources and suppliers.

Some important definitions to keep in mind are:

*   Client API: It is used to access Web resources.

    `Client client = ClientBuilder.newClient();`

    `Response res = client.target(“http://localhost:8080/person”)`

    `.request(“text/plain”).get();`

*   **WebTarget**: It’s useful when we need to build complex URLs
*   **Invocations**: It is a request that was previously prepared and is ready for execution.
*   **Reactive Clients:** The specification supports asynchronous programming, via `CompletionStage<T>.`
*   RxJava for reactive programming with `Observable<T>` and now with Flowable.
*   Supports interceptor and filter.
*   Offers native support for validation.

In the next example, you will create a Web project using JAX-RS will be developed. It will be composed by:

*   Entity
*   Repository
*   Application
*   Services

Start NetBeans IDE and follow the steps below:

1.  From NetBeans IDE, we will create a Web project named as `myjaxrs`:

    ![](images/68-16.jpg)

    **Figure 1.16**

    The class Person contains the following attributes:

    `public class Person {`

    `private String id;`

    `private String name;`

    `public Person() {`

    `}`

    `public Person(String id, String name) {`

    `this.id = id;`

    `this.name = name;`

    `}`

    `//Getters/Setters`

    `}`

    The `Repository` class simulates interaction with a NoSQL database:

    `@Stateless`

    `public class PersonRepository {`

    `List<Person> personList = new ArrayList<>();`

    `public PersonRepository() {`

    `//Old method`

    `personList.add(new Person(“78-10”,”Marie”));`

    `personList.add(new Person(“426-3”,”Stephanie”));`

    `}`

    `public List<Person> findAll(){`

    `return personList;`

    `}`

    `public Person findById(String id){`

    `Person person = personList.stream()`

    `.filter(p -> id.equals(p.getId()))`

    `.findAny()`

    `.orElse(null);`

    `return person;`

    `}`

    `}`

2.  Create a class to inherit from `Application`, here are the application resources:

    `import java.util.Set;`

    `import javax.ws.rs.core.Application;`

    `@javax.ws.rs.ApplicationPath(“rest”)`

    `public class ApplicationConfig extends Application {`

    `@Override`

    `public Set<Class<?>> getClasses() {`

    `Set<Class<?>> resources = new java.util.HashSet<>();`

    `resources.add(com.book.myjaxrs.PersonService.class);`

    `return resources;`

    `}`

    `}`

    To convert the `PersonServices` class into Web services, add the `@Path` annotation at the class level and implement it at the methods level as well.

    The `@GET` annotation is used for the get methods of the HTTP protocol. With `@Produces` we indicate the `MediaType` of the results to be sent. `PathParam` is used to set the name and type of parameters that the method receives.

3.  The `find`() method is used to find a person by the id, and the `findAll`() method to return the list of all persons:

    `@Stateless`

    `@Path(“personService”)`

    `public class PersonService {`

    `@Inject`

    `PersonRepository personRepository;`

    `@GET`

    `@Path(“{id}”)`

    `@Produces({MediaType.APPLICATION_XML, MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON})`

    `public Person find(@PathParam(“id”) String id) {`

    `return personRepository.findById(id);`

    `}`

    `@GET`

    `@Produces({MediaType.APPLICATION_XML, MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON})`

    `public List<Person> findAll() {`

    `return personRepository.findAll();`

    `}`

    `}`

    When executing the project in this case using `PayaraMicro` you can see the REST Endpoints that are available.

Payara Micro URLs:

`http://localhost:8080/myjaxrs`

`‘myjaxrs-0.1’ REST Endpoints:`

`GET /myjaxrs/rest/application.wadl`

`GET /myjaxrs/rest/personService`

`GET /myjaxrs/rest/personService/{id}`

Endpoints REST can be accessed from a client in this case from the Web browser and you can consume the service by invoking the `findAll()` method.

`http://localhost:8080/myjaxrs/rest/personServices/`

![](images/68-17.jpg)

**Figure 1.17**

To perform the queries invoking the find method just pass the parameter in the URL

`http://localhost:8080/myjaxrs/rest/personService/426-3`

The person specified by the id will be returned to us.

`{“id”:”426-3”,”name”:”Stephanie”}`

# [Java EE Security API 1.0 (JSR -375)](#toc.xhtml_s15a)

Security is one of the most important aspects of today’s business applications. Through the incorporation of the JSR-375, authentication mechanisms are offered within the Java EE platform to ensure the security of Web resources.

The specifications define some terminologies:

*   **Authentication mechanism:** Authentication mechanism in which a Caller invokes to obtain his credentials and compare them with the existing ones in an Identity Store.
*   **Caller, Main Caller:** It is the user who makes a request to the application through his credentials.
*   **HAM:** Abbreviation for HttpAuthenticationMechanism.
*   **Identity Store:** It is a component that specifies access to the application through credentials, groups, roles and permissions.
*   **JACC:** JSR-115, “Java Authorization Contract for Containers”,
*   **JASPIC:** JSR-196, “Java Authentication SPI for Containers”

`HttpAuthenticationMechanism` defines three methods we can use that are follows:

*   `ValidateRequest()`: Summoned before the `doFilter`() method of some Servlet or Service.
*   `SecureResponse():` Summoned after the `doFilter`() method of some Servlet or Service.
*   `CleanSubject():` Summoned when calling the `HttpServletRequest logout`() method.

An example of authentication will be created against a MongoDB database that stores users and their credentials, and the user interface will be created with Java Server Faces.

Open the web.`xml` file located in the `WEB-INF` folder and define roles in the `web.xml` file:

`<security-constraint>`

`<web-resource-collection>`

`<web-resource-name>Application pages</web-resource-name>`

`<url-pattern>/faces/pages/*</url-pattern>`

`</web-resource-collection>`

`<auth-constraint>`

`<role-name>ADMIN</role-name>`

`<role-name>TESTING</role-name>`

`</auth-constraint>`

`</security-constraint>`

`<security-role>`

`<role-name>ADMIN</role-name>`

`</security-role>`

`<security-role>`

`<role-name>TESTING</role-name>`

`</security-role>`

In the class method that validates user login, implement credential validation:

`public String login() {`

`try {`

`Credential credential = new UsernamePasswordCredential(username, new Password(password));`

`AuthenticationStatus status = securityContext.authenticate(`

`(HttpServletRequest) externalContext.getRequest(),`

`(HttpServletResponse) externalContext.getResponse(),`

`withParams().credential(credential));`

`switch (status) {`

`case SEND_CONTINUE:`

`facesContext.responseComplete();`

`break;`

`case SEND_FAILURE:`

`facesContext.addMessage(null, new FacesMessage(FacesMessage.SEVERITY_ERROR, “Login failed”, null));`

`break;`

`case SUCCESS:`

`facesContext.addMessage(null, new FacesMessage(FacesMessage.SEVERITY_INFO, “Welcome”, null));`

`return “/faces/pages/index.xhtml?faces-redirect=true”;`

`case NOT_DONE:`

`}`

`} catch (Exception e) {`

`JsfUtil.errorMessage(e.getLocalizedMessage());`

`}`

`return “”;`

`}`

# [Identity Store](#toc.xhtml_s16a)

Create a class that implements `IdentityStore` to validate credentials:

`public class MyIdentityStore implements IdentityStore{`

`@Override`

`public CredentialValidationResult validate(Credential credential) {`

`if (!valid(usernamePassword.getCaller(),usernamePassword.getPasswordAsString())) {`

`return CredentialValidationResult.NOT_VALIDATED_RESULT;`

`}`

`return new CredentialValidationResult(“mygroup”, new HashSet<>(Arrays.asList(“mygroup”)));`

`}`

`}`

At the level of JavaServer Faces pages we can establish the restrictions using `ifGranted`() among other methods that are integrated by means of CDI in the EL:

`<h:form id=”form” prependId=”false” rendered=”#{p:ifGranted(‘ADMIN’)}” >`

The constraints can be implemented at class or method level:

`@ServletSecurity(@HttpConstraint(rolesAllowed = “myrol”))`

# [The Java API for JSON Binding JSON-B 1.0 (JSR-367)](#toc.xhtml_s17a)

The specification defines JSON-B as an API that provides mechanisms for serialization and deserialization of JSON documents that can be mapped to Java classes and vice versa.

The API supports the basic Java types:

*   Integer
*   String
*   Byte
*   Short
*   Date
*   Long
*   Float
*   Double
*   Character

JSON-B also offers support for:

*   Collections
*   Generic

**JSON (JavaScript Object Notation)** is a format for data exchange. A JSON document consists of *key:value* pairs. It is the format most used by most document-oriented NOSQL databases.

An example of a JSON document:

`{`

`“id”:”785-2”,`

`“name”:”Dog”`

`}`

JSON-B provides a `JsonBuilder` constructor containing the `Jsonb` class with the `toJson()` and `fromJson()` methods to serialize/deserialize Java objects to JSON documents and vice versa.

Using `JsonConfig`, it makes possible the formatting of the output of the documents.

The specification defines a series of annotations that we will show in the following table:

| **Annotation** | **Description** |
| `@JsonbProperty` | Change the name of the property. Works at field level and sets serialization) and get (serialization) methods. |
| `@JsonbTransient` | It allows you to ignore a property by the JSON engine. |
| `@JsonbDateFormat` | It allows you to format dates. |
| `@JsonbNumberFormat` | Formatting numbers. |

**Table 1.9**

Example of a simple implementation of JSON-B to serialize and deserialize JSON documents and Java classes:

`public class Animal{`

`private String id;`

`@JsonbProperty(“nameofanimal”)`

`private String name;`

`//Getters/Setters methods`

`}`

`Animal animal = new Animal(“785-2”,”Dog”);`

Now, let’s convert Java to JSON using `toJson()` method:

`Jsonb jsonb = JsonbBuilder.create();`

`String jsonString = jsonb.toJson(animal);`

Now, converting a JSON to Java class, we use the `fromJson()` method:

`Animal animal2 =jsonb.fromJson(jsonString, Animal.class);`

If we want to apply the format to the output:

`JsonbConfig myconfig = new JsonbConfig();`

`Jsonb jsonb = JsonbBuilder.create(myconfig);`

# [JavaServer Faces JSF 2.3 (JSR-372)](#toc.xhtml_s18a)

**Java Server Faces (JSF)** is a framework for creating user interfaces for Java Web applications. It is based on the Model-View-Controller. It is component-oriented.

Among the main features:

*   Contains a large number of components
*   It allows to extend these components in a simple way
*   Offers request/response lifecycle management
*   Support CDI injection in Expression Language (EL).
*   Supports HTML5
*   Supports WebSocket through `<f:websocket>`
*   Validation by groups `<f:validateWholeBean>`
*   Component Search Expression Framework
*   `<f:converterTime>` supports Java 8 Time

The reference implementations are Eclipse Mojarra, Oracle Mojarra, and Apache MyFaces.

Among the most used frameworks we have:

*   Primefaces
*   Bootsfaces
*   Omnifaces

The components are easily integrated into the `xhtml` pages, and these can be injected via CDI into the EL.

In the following example we show a form with panels, labels, tables, and buttons created using Java Server Faces, Primefaces and Bootsfaces.

`<h:form id=”form” prependId=”false”>`

`<b:growl id=”growl”/>`

`<b:panel title=”Country” id=”content” look=”primary” >`

`<b:panelGrid colSpans=”1,4,1,6” columns=”4” size=”xs”>`

`<p:outputLabel value=”id”/>`

`<p:inputText value=”#{countryController.country.id}” required=”true”/>`

`<p:outputLabel value=”name”/>`

`<p:inputText value=”#{countryController.country.name}” rendered=”true”/>`

`<p:commandButton value=”Save” action=”#{countryController.save()}” update=”:form”/>`

`</b:panelGrid>`

# [WebSocket](#toc.xhtml_s19a)

Java Server Faces 2.3 supports integration with websocket through `<f:websocket>`. It registers in the client a push connection from a server. Its use is very useful for chat clients, sending notifications to clients.

Its use is relatively easy, we will describe the steps:

1.  Let’s add `javax.websocket-api` dependency in pom.xml file:

    `<dependency>`

    `<groupId>javax.websocket</groupId>`

    `<artifactId>javax.websocket-api</artifactId>`

    `<version>1.1</version>`

    `<scope>provided</scope>`

    `</dependency>`

2.  Configure the web.xml file by enabling ENABLE_CDI_RESOLVER_CHAIN, ENABLE_WEBSOCKET_ENDPOINT:

    `<context-param>`

    `<param-name>javax.faces.ENABLE_CDI_RESOLVER_CHAIN</param-name>`

    `<param-value>true</param-value>`

    `</context-param>`

    `<context-param>`

    `<param-name>javax.faces.ENABLE_WEBSOCKET_ENDPOINT</param-name>`

    `<param-value>true</param-value>`

    `</context-param>`

3.  Now, inject @Push(channel = “notification”) to register the Websocket:

    `@Named`

    `@ApplicationScoped`

    `public class MySocket implements Serializable {`

    `Integer count = 0;`

    `@Inject`

    `@Push(channel = “notification”)`

    `private PushContext push;`

    `public Integer getCount() {`

    `return count;`

    `}`

    `public void setCount(Integer count) {`

    `this.count = count;`

    `}`

    `public void pushAction() {`

    `try {`

    `count++;`

    `push.send(count);`

    `} catch (Exception e) {`

    `JsfUtil.error(e.getLocalizedMessage());`

    `}`

    `}`

    `}`

4.  Add the following lines to the page::

    `<f:websocket channel=”notification” onmessage=”webSocketListener” />`

    The JavaScript function will be invoked when the websocket event is received:

    `<script type=”text/javascript”>`

    `function webSocketListener(message, channel, event) {`

    `document.getElementById(“label”).innerHTML += message + “<br/>”;`

    `}`

    `</script>`

5.  Now, use a <h:commandButton> with <f:ajax> to place a message on the websocket:

    `<h:commandButton value=”Socket” action=”#{mySocket.pushAction()}”>`

    `<f:ajax />`

    `</h:commandButton>`

6.  Establish the HTML component that will be updated with the message obtained in the client from the server:

    `<div id=”label”></div>`

**The interface for the user would have a similar appearance, in the example we can see that it is updated from two clients in the socket:**

![](images/68-19.jpg)

**Figure 1.19**

To use it with other components of Java Server Faces, it can be implemented by running a `<p:remoteCommand>` that will be invoked from JavaScript.

To use it with other components of Java Server Faces, can be implemented by running a `<p:remoteCommand>` that will be invoked from JavaScript, to update the components that are necessary and can invoke methods that are necessary.

`<script type=”text/javascript”>`

`function webSocketListener(message, channel, event) {`

`remoteCommandTop();`

`}`

`</script>`

`<p:remoteCommand name=”remoteCommandTop”`

`update=”:growl_top”`

`actionListener=”#{mySocket.actionWebSocket()}”`

`process=”@all”>`

`</p:remoteCommand>`

Add a growl to show the notifications and that they disappear after a certain time:

`<p:growl id=”growl_top” globalOnly=”true” life=”5000” showDetail=”true” />`

In the class we send a notification to the component `<p:growl>:`

`public String actionWebSocket() {`

`JsfUtil.warningMessage(“you have a notification”);`

`return “”;`

`}`

When a push notification is received from the websocket it will display a growl for a short period of time indicating to the user that there are new notifications just as shown in the following screenshot:

# [Java Persistence API 2.2 (JSR-338)](#toc.xhtml_s20a)

Most applications use databases to store information. When we develop Java applications we generally need an **Object Relational Mapping (ORM)** or we use JDBC API which is the interface for Java to communicate with a Database System management (DBMS).

In the Java platform there is a standard that defines the data persistence mechanisms called Java Persistence API.

The specification defines the Java Persistence API architecture composed of:

*   `EntityManagerFactory`: Interface is used by the application to create an application-managed entity manager.
*   `EntityManager:` Creates and destroys persistence contexts managed by the application. Obtained by Injection or through JNDI.
*   `Entity`: A lightweight persistent domain object.
*   `EntityTransaction:` Interface is used to control resource transactions on resource-local entity managers.
*   `Persistence`: Get instances of EntityManagerFactory.
*   `Query`: an interface to interact with vendors DBMS.

The new version of JPA 2.2 supports:

*   Repeating annotations
*   Inject into attribute converters
*   Support for Java 8 Date/Time
*   Query and TypeQuery as Streams.

Some annotations offered by JPA:

*   `@Entity:` Defines a class as an entity
*   `@Id`: Identifies primary key.
*   `@GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY)`
*   `@NamedQuery():` These are static queries defined in the entity.
*   `@Table`: Used to indicate the name of the table.
*   `@PersistenceContext:` Managing the context of persistence
*   `@Embeddable`: Compound keys
*   `@OneToOne`: One-to one relationships
*   `@ManyToOne:` Relationships many to one
*   `@ManyToMany`: Relationships many to many

An entity is a POJO to which we add a series of annotations:

`@Entity`

`@NamedQuery(name = “Products.findAll”, query = “SELECT p FROM Products p”)`

`@NamedQuery(name = “Products.findByProductID”, query = “SELECT p FROM Products p WHERE p.productID = :productID”)`

`public class Product implements Serializable {`

`@Id`

`@GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY)`

`private Integer productID;`

`private String productCode;`

`private String name;`

`public Products() {}`

`}`

Let’s use `Facade or Repository` patterns to interact with database operations:

`@Stateless`

`public class ProductsFacade extends AbstractFacade<Products> {`

`@PersistenceContext(unitName = “com.book_myjpa_war_0.1PU”)`

`private EntityManager em;`

`@Override`

`protected EntityManager getEntityManager() {`

`return em;`

`}`

`public ProductsFacade() {`

`super(Products.class);`

`}`

`}`

In a controller inject `Facade` or `Repository` and create the methods to do the CRUD operations:

`@EJB`

`ProductsFacade productFacade;`

`Product product = new Product();`

`public void save(){`

`productFacade.persist(product);`

`}`

`public void update(){`

`productFacade.merge(product);`

`}`

`public void delete(){`

`productFacade.remove(product);`

`}`

# [CHAPTER 2](#toc.xhtml_c02)

# [NoSQL](#toc.xhtml_c02)

The NoSQL DB is a database that gives a mechanism for storage and retrieval of data, which is modeled by means, other than the tabular relations used in relational databases. These databases have speed and high scalability. This variety of databases is becoming more popular in several applications, which include financial ones. As a result of the increase, the number of users and vendors is increasing too. After cover briefly about Jakarta/Java technologies, this chapter will talk about this new way to store information, the NoSQL technology; it includes some terminologies to help the reader in this book.

# [Structure](#toc.xhtml_s23a)

*   NoSQL
*   Key-value
*   Column-family
*   Document
*   Graph
*   Multi-model
*   Scalability versus complexity
*   BASE versus ACID
*   CAP

# [Objective](#toc.xhtml_s24a)

This chapter will talk about NoSQL databases, the NoSQL kinds, and the best practice to use each one.

# [NoSQL types](#toc.xhtml_s25a)

There are around two hundred and fifty NoSQL databases vendors, each one with specific behavior and features, to make it easier to understand this variety of persistence technology, the NoSQL databases have groups based on the structure how a NoSQL based storage?

# [Key-value](#toc.xhtml_s26a)

The following is the diagrammatic representation of the key-value structure:

![](images/key-value.jpg)

**Figure 2.1**

This database has a structure that looks like a `java.util.Map` API, where we can store any value from a key. For examples:

*   Amazon Dynamo
*   Amazon S3
*   Redis
*   Scalaris
*   Voldemort

It is natural to compare with one knowledge that a developer already has with a new one; thus, this chapter will compare each new NoSQL structure with standard relational technology. When developers associate key-value against a relational database, they can see that key-value is smoother than relational. At Key-value there is no way to do join operation between buckets.

Furthermore, the whole information is on a blob instead of being on a small piece in a column like the relational structure.

| Relational structure | Key-value structure |
| Table | Bucket |
| Row | Key/value pair |
| Column | ---- |
| Relationship | ---- |

**Table 2.1**

# [Column family](#toc.xhtml_s27a)

The following is the diagrammatic representation of Column-Family structure:

![](images/column.jpg)

**Figure 2.2**

This model became popular with the Bigtable paper by Google, to be a distributed system storage, projected to have either high scalability or volume. As a key-value structure, the data is reclaimed from the ID column. These Column-Family kinds are crucial to handle a high volume of write operations; however, the read operations have limited activities. For example:

*   HBase
*   Cassandra
*   Scylla
*   Cloud Data
*   SimpleDB

When developers confront column family against a relational structure, the column family is faster than a relational database it, usually, makes it more comfortable to work with a distributed database. However, these benefits come with a high price at the read operations. To retrieve any information, it uses a key, and it is not allowed to do joins actions. The column family structure looks like the key-value one; the unique point is the value, at the column family, the information is on blocks of columns instead of a BLOB such as on key-value.

Furthermore, we can compare column family with the relational database, as shown in the table below:

| **Relational structure** | **Column Family structure** |
| Table | Column Family |
| Row | Column |
| Column | Key/value pair |
| Relationship | --- |

**Table 2.2**

# [Document collection](#toc.xhtml_s28a)

The following code represents the Document structure:

`{`

`"name":"Diana",`

`"duty":[`

`"Hunt",`

`"Moon",`

`"Nature"`

`],`

`"age":1000,`

`"siblings":{`

`"Apollo":"brother"`

`}`

`}`

The document NoSQL database structure looks like either JSON or XML file. They have a considerable number of fields that will, usually, make on run time. That is the schema-less approach that allows the persisting of any database type where the developer wants to.

Document structures can recover information to fields that are not the key; it allows more complexity on reading operations, but it does not have support to do joins service. Another point to point it out, at the Document database, they are usually schema-less.

Furthermore, we can compare document collection with the relational database, as shown in the table below:

| Relational structure | Document structure |
| Table | Collection |
| Row | Document |
| Column | Key/value pair |
| Relationship | ---- |

**Table 2.3**

For examples:

*   Amazon SimpleDB
*   Apache CouchDB
*   MongoDB

# [Graph](#toc.xhtml_s29a)

The following figure is the diagrammatic representation Graph database structure:

![](images/graph.jpg)

**Figure 2.3**

The graph database works with connection; this relationship is more profound than a relational database. The graph database connects several vertexes through edges structure. This kind of database became popular on social media to make recommendation systems.

The graph has three components:

*   **Property**: A small component in the structure; a tuple where the key is the name, and the value is the information itself.
*   **Vertex**: Looks like the table in an SQL technology that has an unlimited number of properties.
*   **Edge**: The element that represents the relationship between vertices; it has a small similarity with a relationship in SQL. However, the edge has properties — so, a connection deeper than relational technology.

The following screenshot show two related graphs:

![](images/graph_deep.jpg)

**Figure 2.4**

The graph direction is another concept pretty important in a graph structure, i.e., you can know a person despite this person not knowing you. For examples:

*   Neo4j
*   InfoGrid
*   Sones
*   HyperGraphDB

# [Scalability versus complexity](#toc.xhtml_s30a)

Every kind with specific persistence structures to solve particular problems. As the graph below shows, there is a balance regarding model complexity; more complicated models are less scalable. For example, a key-value NoSQL database is more scalable, but it has smooth complexity once all queries and operations are key-based.

# [BASE verses ACID](#toc.xhtml_s31a)

While in the relational persistence technology, they use the ACID, which is an acronym for Atomicity, Consistency, Isolation, and Durability.

*   **Atomicity:** All of the operations in the transaction will complete, or none will.
*   **Consistency**: The database will be in a consistent state when the transaction begins and ends.
*   **Isolation**: The transaction will behave as if it is the only operation being performed upon the database.
*   **Durability**: Upon completion of the transaction, the operation will not be reversed.

In the NoSQL world, they usually focus on the BASE. As ACID the BASE is an acronym.

*   **Basic availability:** The database appears to work most of the time.
*   **Soft-state**: Stores don't have to be write-consistent, nor do different replicas have to be mutually consistent all the time.
*   **Eventual consistency:** Stores exhibit consistency at some later point (e.g., lazily at the reading time).

# [CAP theorem](#toc.xhtml_s32a)

There is no silver bullet; the CAP can explain it, based on three behavior. The following figure illustrates the same:

![](images/cap.jpg)

**Figure 2.5**

The CAP theorem applies to distributed systems that store state. Eric Brewer, at the 2000 Symposium on **Principles of Distributed Computing (PODC),** conjectured that in any networked shared-data system, there is a fundamental trade-off between consistency, availability, and partition.

Tolerance: In 2002, *Seth Gilbert and Nancy Lynch* of MIT published a formal proof of Brewer's conjecture. The theorem states that networked shared-data systems can only guarantee/strongly support two of the following three properties:

*   **Consistency**: A guarantee that every node in a distributed cluster returns the same, most recent, successful write. Consistency refers to every client having the same view of the data. There are various types of consistency models. Consistency in CAP (used to prove the theorem) refers to linearizability or sequential consistency - a very strong form of consistency.
*   **Availability**: Every non-failing node returns a response for all read and writes requests in a reasonable amount of time. The keyword here is *every*. To be available, every node (on either side of a network partition) must be able to respond in a reasonable amount of time.
*   **Partition tolerance**: The system continues to function and uphold its consistency guarantees in spite of network partitions. Network partitions are a fact of life. Distributed systems guaranteeing partition tolerance can gracefully recover from partitions once the partition heals.

# [The diversity in NoSQL](#toc.xhtml_s33a)

At the current moment, there are around two hundred and twenty-five NoSQL databases. These databases usually go beyond to support one or more types of structures, and they also have specific behavior. These particular features make the developer's life more comfortable in different ways, such as Cassandra Query language at Cassandra database, a search engine to Elasticsearch, the live query at OrientDB, N1QL at Couchbase, and so on. These aspect matters when the topic is a NoSQL database.

# [Standard in SQL](#toc.xhtml_s34a)

Looking to Java application that uses a relational database, it's a good practice to have a layer bridge between a Java application and relationship database: a DAO - the data access object. Talking more about relational databases, there are APIs such as JPA and JDBC that have some advantages to a Java developer:

*   There isn't a lock-in vendor. In other words, with the standard, a database change will happen easier and with transparency because we just need to change a simple driver.
*   It isn't necessary to learn a new API for each new database once there is a common database communication.
*   There isn't any impact in change from one vendor to another; in some moments, it's necessary to use a specific database resource, but in this case, not everything in the DAO layer is lost.

Currently, in NoSQL, the database has no standard, so a Java developer has some issues:

*   Lock-in vendor
*   To each new database, it's necessary to learn a new API. Any change to another database has a high impact, and once all the communication layers will be lost, there isn't a standard API. This happens even with the same kind of NoSQL database; for example, a change in a column to another column.

There is a massive effort to create a common API to make the Java developer's life easier, such as Spring Data, Hibernate ORM, and TopLink. The JPA is a popular API in the Java world, which is why all solutions try to use it. However, this API is created for SQL and not for NoSQL and doesn't support all behaviors in the NoSQL database. Many NoSQL hasn't a transaction, and many NoSQL database hasn't support asynchronous insertion.

The solution for this case would be the creation of a specification that covers the four kinds of NoSQL database; as described, each NoSQL database has specific structures that must be recognized. The new API should look like the JPA once the developer has familiarity with this API, plus be extensible when a database has more than a particular behavior. However, that cannot be JPA as JPA has the goal of a relational database instead of NoSQL. As described briefly, NoSQL has more than one structure that must be covered. They usually use BASE instead of ACID, they typically don't use SQL as a query language, scheme-less, and so on.

# [Conclusion](#toc.xhtml_s35a)

This chapter gives the reader introductory concepts about NoSQL databases comparing with the usual solution to persistence operation, the relational database. It shows a bunch of new ideas, the NoSQL structures, the impact to use any Mapper in the application, everything to make more accessible to a developer start to think about the NoSQL database as a software option. These new databases technology were born to handle the unique challenge of the application that is a performance, in other words, to decrease the response time, even when a developer leaves the normalization process and duplicate information in the database.

# [CHAPTER 3](#toc.xhtml_c03)

# [Jakarta NoSQL](#toc.xhtml_c03)

Communicating Java applications with NoSQL databases is a fundamental requirement for Java applications in the cloud, in this chapter we go through Jakarta NoSQL as the adopted specification.

# [Structure](#toc.xhtml_s37a)

*   Why Jakarta NoSQL?
*   Why not JPA as NoSQL specification?

# [Objective](#toc.xhtml_s38a)

Learn about the new Jakarta NoSQL specification to create applications that communicate with NoSQL databases.

Jakarta NoSQL is a Jakarta EE specification that streamlines the integration of Java applications with NoSQL databases. It defines a set of APIs and provides a standard implementation for most NoSQL databases. This helps to achieve very low coupling with the underlying NoSQL technologies used in applications. The project has two layers:

The specification has two layers:

*   **Communication Layer:** A set of APIs that defines communication with NoSQL databases. Compared with the traditional RDBMS world, they are like the JDBC API. It contains four modules, one for each NoSQL database type: Key-Value, Column Family, Document, and Graph.
*   **Mapping Layer**: These APIs help developers to integrate their Java application with the NoSQL database. This layer is annotation-driven and uses technologies like CDI and Bean Validation, making it simple for developers to use. In the traditional RDBMS world, this layer can be compared to the Java Persistence API or object-relational mapping frameworks such as Hibernate.

    The following screenshot shows the specification layers:

    ![](images/3-1.jpg)

    **Figure 3.1**

# [Why Jakarta NoSQL?](#toc.xhtml_s39a)

When a developer asks about Jakarta NoSQL specification, that first question is why?

Why, as a developer, should we care about it? And about you as a reader, why should not skip this chapter about Jakarta NoSQL?

The first point is vendor-lockin:

**Vendor-lockin** is one of the things any Java project needs to consider when choosing NoSQL databases. If there's a need for a switch, other considerations include time spent on the change, the learning curve of a new API to use with this database, the code that will be lost, the persistence layer that needs to be replaced, etc. Jakarta NoSQL avoids most of these issues through Communication APIs. It also has template classes that apply the design pattern *template method* to database operations. And the Repository interface allows Java developers to create and extend interfaces, with implementation automatically provided by Jakarta NoSQL: support method queries built by developers will automatically be implemented for them.

To make it clear, let's create a sample code, you know as Java Developer, I believe in you when I see a piece of code and for sure have fun. Thus, Java makes anyone happy:

*   ArangoDB
*   MongoDB
*   Couchbase
*   OrientDB

The following screenshot is of different databases:

![](images/3-2.jpg)

**Figure 3.2**

What do those databases have in common?

Yep, all they are document NoSQL database, and they are trying to create a document, a tuple with name, and the information itself. They are doing the exact something with the same behavior goal, however, with a different class, method name, and so on. So, if you would like to move your code to another that means you need to learn a new API and also update the whole database code to the database API code target.

The following figure shows document-oriented databases:

![](images/3-3.jpg)

**Figure 3.3**

Through the communication specification, we can easily change between databases only using the driver databases that look like JDBC drivers. Therefore, a developer is more comfortable to learn a new NoSQL database to the software architecture perspective; we can smoothly jump to another NoSQL without spending more time.

# [Why not JPA as NoSQL specification?](#toc.xhtml_s40a)

JPA is a good API for object-relationship mapping and it's already a standard in the Java world defined in JSRs. It would be great to use the same API for both SQL and NoSQL, but there are behaviors in NoSQL that SQL does not cover, such as time to live and asynchronous operations. JPA was simply not made to handle those features.

# [Particular behavior matters in NoSQL database](#toc.xhtml_s41a)

Particular behavior matters. Even within the same type, each NoSQL database has a unique feature that is a considerable factor when choosing a database over another. This feature might make it easier to develop, making it more scaleable or consistent from a configuration standpoint, have the desired consistency level or search engine, etc. Some examples are Cassandra and its Cassandra Query Language and consistency level, OrientDB with live queries, ArangoDB and its Arango Query Language, Couchbase with N1QL - the list goes on. Each NoSQL has a specific behavior and this behavior matters, so JNoSQL is extensible enough to capture this substantiality different feature elements.

Let's have fun with some code. After talking about what is Jakarta NoSQL and why we as Java Developer should pay attention to this specification, let's move it forward to show some code. On this sample, we'll use create a smooth Java SE application to storage on these NoSQL databases:

*   Redis to key-value
*   Cassandra to the column family
*   MongoDB to document
*   Neo4J to graph

To store information into the NoSQL database, let's create an entity class; in this sample, we'll create a God to represent God's mythology. A god will have three attributes: an id, name, and power:

`‘‘‘java`

`public class God {`

`private Long id;`

`private String name;`

`private String power;`

`public God(Long id, String name, String power) {`

`this.id = id;`

`this.name = name;`

`this.power = power;`

`}`

`public God() {`

`}`

`@Override`

`public String toString() {`

`return "God{" + "id=" + id + ", name='" + name + '\'' + ", power=" + power + '}';`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

# [Install databases](#toc.xhtml_s42a)

We have the goal and the entity whose we want to persist on those NoSQL databases, but before, we need to install each NoSQL database. As a developer, we have several options, such as go to each database site and install it manually.

One option and my favorite one is to install both `docker` and `docker-compose.` Briefly, docker is a set of the platform as service products that use OS-level to deliver software in packages called containers, and `docker-compose.` is a tool that enables to run several containers from a file.

If you want to use `docker-compose.` you can create a file `docker-compose.yml` and append this information:

`‘‘‘yaml`

`version: '3.2'`

`services:`

`key-value:`

`image: "redis"`

`- nosql`

`- 6379:6379`

`column:`

To start a fresh environment:

**`‘docker-compose -f docker-compose.yml up -d’`**

# [Maven project](#toc.xhtml_s43a)

The project is a Java SE project to make it easier to handle all the dependencies and the dependencies of these dependencies; we'll use Maven as a build automation process. The main requirement of this kind of project is the `pom.xml`, a Project Object Model or POM is the fundamental unit of work in Maven. It is an XML file that contains information about the project and configuration details used by Maven to build the project.

We can split our dependencies into five categories:

1.  **CDI**: (Contexts and Dependency Injection) is a standard dependency injection framework.
2.  **JSON Binding** is a Java framework that provides a standard binding layer between Java classes and JSON documents.
3.  **Eclipse MicroProfile Configuration**: It is a solution to externalize configuration out of Java application and following good practices such as twelve-factor. We'll take more about Eclipse MicroProfile later.
4.  **Jakarta NoSQL Mapping**: Think about JPA to NoSQL databases
5.  **Jakarta NoSQL communication:** Think about JDBC to the NoSQL database.

As shown in the code below:
