# [Entity model](#toc.xhtml_s44a)

Given the entity class, what we need to do is to create a mechanism to convert to/from the NoSQL database. Jakarta NoSQL is annotation driven, thus it uses a similar approach as JPA does.

On this sample, we're using three annotations:

*   The entity annotation to define that class is permissible in a database.
*   The Id annotation to define the key to either Bucket in a key-value or a `DocumentCollection` to a `Document` and so on.
*   The `Column` annotation to define each field will be stored into the NoSQL database.

As shown in the code below:

`import jakarta.nosql.mapping.Column;`

`import jakarta.nosql.mapping.Entity;`

`import jakarta.nosql.mapping.Id;`

`import java.util.List;`

`@Entity`

`public class God {`

`@Id`

`private Long id;`

`@Column`

`private String name;`

`@Column`

`private String power;`

`public God(Long id, String name, String power) {`

`this.id = id;`

`this.name = name;`

`this.power = power;`

`}`

`public God() {`

`}`

`@Override`

`public String toString() {`

`return "God{" + "id=" + id + ", name='" + name + '\'' + ", power=" + power + '}';`

`}`

# [Key-value database](#toc.xhtml_s45a)

In this section, we'll explore the relationship between Java and key-value database, to key-value, we'll use Redis. Like any database, we need to define the connection and to Jakarta NoSQL, it isn't different. Briefly, we need to make a `BucketManager`, an `EntityManager` to Key-value NoSQL, available to CDI.

As shown in the following code:

`‘‘‘java`

`import jakarta.nosql.keyvalue.BucketManager;`

`import org.eclipse.microprofile.config.inject.ConfigProperty;`

`import javax.enterprise.context.ApplicationScoped;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.Disposes;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.Produces;`

`import javax.inject.Inject;`

`@ApplicationScoped`

`class BucketManagerProducer {`

`@Inject`

`@ConfigProperty(name = "keyvalue")`

`private BucketManager manager;`

`@Produces`

`public BucketManager getManager() {`

`return manager;`

`}`

`public void destroy(@Disposes BucketManager manager) {`

`manager.close();`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

To simplify code, we'll split the configuration information to the code; also, it's safer than putting the critical information such as password hardcode; indeed we'll use Eclipse MicroProfile Configuration.

In the `META-INF/microprofile-config.properties` we'll put these information:

`‘‘‘`

`#keyvalue`

`keyvalue=keyvalue`

`keyvalue.provider=org.eclipse.jnosql.diana.redis.keyvalue.RedisConfiguration`

`keyvalue.database=olympus`

`’’’`

`Let's create our hello world with a key-value database; this first piece of code will do the create, update, retrieve, and delete:`

`‘‘‘`

`import jakarta.nosql.mapping.keyvalue.KeyValueTemplate;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainer;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainerInitializer;`

`import java.time.Duration;`

`import java.util.Collections;`

`import java.util.Optional;`

`import java.util.concurrent.TimeUnit;`

`public class KeyValueApp {`

`public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {`

`try (SeContainer container = SeContainerInitializer.newInstance().initialize()) {`

`God diana = new God(1L, "Diana", "Hunt");`

`KeyValueTemplate template = container.select(KeyValueTemplate.class).get();`

`template.put(diana);`

`final Optional<God> god = template.get(1L, God.class);`

`System.out.println("query : " + god);`

`template.delete(1L);`

`System.out.println("query again: " + template.get(1L, God.class));`

`}`

`System.exit(0);`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

An important feature is TTL, time to live, this feature defines the time to the information expire in the database:

`‘‘‘java`

`import jakarta.nosql.mapping.keyvalue.KeyValueTemplate;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainer;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainerInitializer;`

`import java.time.Duration;`

`import java.util.Optional;`

`import java.util.concurrent.TimeUnit;`

`public class KeyValueApp2 {`

`public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {`

`try (SeContainer container = SeContainerInitializer.newInstance().initialize()) {`

`God diana = new God(1L, "Diana", "Hunt");`

`KeyValueTemplate template = container.select(KeyValueTemplate.class).get();`

`template.put(diana, Duration.ofSeconds(1L));`

`final Optional<God> god = template.get(1L, God.class);`

`System.out.println("query : " + god);`

`TimeUnit.SECONDS.sleep(2L);`

`System.out.println("query again: " + template.get(1L, God.class));`

`}`

`System.exit(0);`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

Jakarta NoSQL has a standard query as text to each NoSQL database so that you can use either a plain query or a `PrepareStatement` query:

`‘‘‘java`

`import jakarta.nosql.mapping.PreparedStatement;`

`import jakarta.nosql.mapping.keyvalue.KeyValueTemplate;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainer;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainerInitializer;`

`import java.time.Duration;`

`import java.util.Optional;`

`import java.util.concurrent.TimeUnit;`

`public class KeyValueApp3 {`

`public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {`

`try (SeContainer container = SeContainerInitializer.newInstance().initialize()) {`

`God diana = new God(1L, "Diana", "Hunt");`

`KeyValueTemplate template = container.select(KeyValueTemplate.class).get();`

`template.put(diana, Duration.ofSeconds(1L));`

`Optional<God> result = template.getSingleResult("get 1", God.class);`

`System.out.println("Query by plain text: " + result);`

`PreparedStatement prepare = template.prepare("get @id", God.class);`

`prepare.bind("id", 1L);`

`System.out.println("Query by prepare query");`

`prepare.getSingleResult().ifPresent(System.out::println);`

`}`

`System.exit(0);`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

To simplify as Spring Data Jakarta NoSQL also has the repository feature:

`‘‘‘java`

`import jakarta.nosql.mapping.Repository;`

`import java.util.List;`

`public interface GodRepository extends Repository<God, Long> {`

`}`

`import org.eclipse.jnosql.artemis.DatabaseQualifier;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainer;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainerInitializer;`

`import java.util.Optional;`

`public class KeyValueApp4 {`

`public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {`

`try (SeContainer container = SeContainerInitializer.newInstance().initialize()) {`

`God diana = new God(1L, "Diana", "Hunt");`

`GodRepository repository = container.select(GodRepository.class, DatabaseQualifier.ofKeyValue()).get();`

`repository.save(diana);`

`Optional<God> result = repository.findById(1L);`

`System.out.println("Find by id: " + result);`

`}`

`System.exit(0);`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

As Spring Data, you can use a method by the query, where you can create a method by convention and the Jakarta NoSQL implementation will implement automatically to you. However, Key-value only allows query by the key. Therefore, that is not possible to find by name in our God entity.

# [Column family database](#toc.xhtml_s46a)

In this section, we'll explore the relationship between Java and Column-Family database, to column family, we'll use Apache Cassandra. Like any database, we need to define the connection and to Jakarta NoSQL, it isn't different. Briefly, we need to make a `ColumnFamilyManager,` an `EntityManager` to Column-Family NoSQL, available to CDI:

`‘‘‘java`

`import jakarta.nosql.column.ColumnFamilyManager;`

`import org.eclipse.microprofile.config.inject.ConfigProperty;`

`import javax.enterprise.context.ApplicationScoped;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.Disposes;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.Produces;`

`import javax.inject.Inject;`

`@ApplicationScoped`

`class ColumnManagerProducer {`

`@Inject`

`@ConfigProperty(defaultValue = "column")`

`private ColumnFamilyManager manager;`

`@Produces`

`public ColumnFamilyManager getManagerCassandra() {`

`return manager;`

`}`

`public void destroy(@Disposes ColumnFamilyManager manager) {`

`manager.close();`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

To simplify code, we'll split the configuration information to the code; also, it's safer than putting the critical information such as password hardcode; indeed we'll use Eclipse MicroProfile Configuration.

In the `META-INF/microprofile-config.properties` we'll put these information:

`‘‘‘`

`column=column`

`column.database=olympus`

`column.provider=org.eclipse.jnosql.diana.cassandra.column.CassandraConfiguration`

`column.settings.jakarta.nosql.host.1=localhost`

`column.settings.cassandra.query.1=CREATE KEYSPACE IF NOT EXISTS olympus WITH replication = {'class': 'SimpleStrategy', 'replication_factor' : 3};`

`column.settings.cassandra.query.2=CREATE COLUMNFAMILY IF NOT EXISTS olympus.God ("_id" bigint PRIMARY KEY, name text, power text);`

`’’’`

The Cassandra settings have more information than Redis that is because Cassandra is a scheme database. Yeap, Apache Cassandra is not schemaless and we need to create both Keyspace and Column family.

Let's create our hello world with a column-family database; this first piece of code will do the create, update, retrieve, and delete:

`‘‘‘java`

`import jakarta.nosql.column.ColumnDeleteQuery;`

`import jakarta.nosql.mapping.column.ColumnTemplate;`

`import jakarta.nosql.mapping.keyvalue.KeyValueTemplate;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainer;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainerInitializer;`

`import java.time.Duration;`

`import java.util.Collections;`

`import java.util.Optional;`

`import java.util.concurrent.TimeUnit;`

`import static jakarta.nosql.column.ColumnDeleteQuery.delete;`

`public class ColumnApp {`

`public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {`

`try (SeContainer container = SeContainerInitializer.newInstance().initialize()) {`

`God diana = new God(1L, "Diana", "Hunt");`

`ColumnTemplate template = container.select(ColumnTemplate.class).get();`

`template.insert(diana);`

`final Optional<God> god = template.find(God.class, 1L);`

`System.out.println("query : " + god);`

`ColumnDeleteQuery deleteQuery = delete().from("God").where("_id").eq(1L).build();`

`template.delete(deleteQuery);`

`System.out.println("query again: " + template.find(God.class, 1L));`

`}`

`System.exit(0);`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

An important feature is TTL, time to live, this feature defines the time to the information expire in the database:

`‘‘‘java`

`import jakarta.nosql.mapping.column.ColumnTemplate;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainer;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainerInitializer;`

`import java.time.Duration;`

`import java.util.Optional;`

`import java.util.concurrent.TimeUnit;`

`public class ColumnApp2 {`

`public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {`

`try (SeContainer container = SeContainerInitializer.newInstance().initialize()) {`

`God diana = new God(1L, "Diana", "Hunt");`

`ColumnTemplate template = container.select(ColumnTemplate.class).get();`

`template.insert(diana, Duration.ofSeconds(1L));`

`final Optional<God> god = template.find(God.class, 1L);`

`System.out.println("query : " + god);`

`TimeUnit.SECONDS.sleep(2L);`

`System.out.println("query again: " + template.find(God.class, 1L));`

`}`

`System.exit(0);`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

Jakarta NoSQL has a standard query as text to each NoSQL database so that you can use either a plain query or a `PrepareStatement` query:

`‘‘‘java`

`import jakarta.nosql.mapping.PreparedStatement;`

`import jakarta.nosql.mapping.column.ColumnTemplate;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainer;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainerInitializer;`

`import java.util.Optional;`

`public class ColumnApp3 {`

`public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {`

`try (SeContainer container = SeContainerInitializer.newInstance().initialize()) {`

`God diana = new God(1L, "Diana", "Hunt");`

`ColumnTemplate template = container.select(ColumnTemplate.class).get();`

`template.insert(diana);`

`Optional<God> god = template.singleResult("select * from God where _id = 1");`

`System.out.println("Plain query text : " + god);`

`PreparedStatement prepare = template.prepare("select * from God where _id = @id");`

`prepare.bind("id", 1L);`

`System.out.println("Query by prepare query" + prepare.getSingleResult());`

`}`

`System.exit(0);`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

To simplify as Spring Data Jakarta NoSQL also has the repository feature:

`‘‘‘java`

`import jakarta.nosql.mapping.Repository;`

`import java.util.List;`

`public interface GodRepository extends Repository<God, Long> {`

`}`

`import org.eclipse.jnosql.artemis.DatabaseQualifier;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainer;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainerInitializer;`

`import java.util.Optional;`

`public class ColumnApp4 {`

`public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {`

`try (SeContainer container = SeContainerInitializer.newInstance().initialize()) {`

`God diana = new God(1L, "Diana", "Hunt");`

`GodRepository repository = container.select(GodRepository.class, DatabaseQualifier.ofColumn()).get();`

`repository.save(diana);`

`Optional<God> god = repository.findById(1L);`

`System.out.println("Query by id : " + god);`

`}`

`System.exit(0);`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

Unlike Key-value with Redis, Apache Cassandra allows you to query by the field, but, you need to either create a secondary index that might decrease query performance. So, we need to append a new setting that has a Cassandra query language to create an index in the name field.

`‘‘‘`

`column.settings.cassandra.query.3=CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS nameIndex ON olympus.God (name);`

`‘‘‘`

`‘‘‘java`

`import org.eclipse.jnosql.artemis.DatabaseQualifier;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainer;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainerInitializer;`

`public class ColumnApp5 {`

`public static void main(String[] args) {`

`try (SeContainer container = SeContainerInitializer.newInstance().initialize()) {`

`God diana = new God(1L, "Diana", "Hunt");`

`GodRepository repository = container.select(GodRepository.class, DatabaseQualifier.ofColumn()).get();`

`repository.save(diana);`

`System.out.println("Query by name : " + repository.findByName("Diana"));`

`}`

`System.exit(0);`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

# [Document database](#toc.xhtml_s47a)

In this section, we'll explore the relationship between Java and Document database, to document, we'll use MongoDB. Like any database, we need to define the connection and to Jakarta NoSQL, it isn't different. Briefly, we need to make a `DocumentCollectionManager`, an `EntityManager` to Column-Family NoSQL, available to CDI:

`‘‘‘java`

`import jakarta.nosql.document.DocumentCollectionManager;`

`import org.eclipse.microprofile.config.inject.ConfigProperty;`

`import javax.enterprise.context.ApplicationScoped;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.Disposes;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.Produces;`

`import javax.inject.Inject;`

`@ApplicationScoped`

`class DocumentManagerProducer {`

`@Inject`

`@ConfigProperty(name = "document")`

`private DocumentCollectionManager manager;`

`@Produces`

`public DocumentCollectionManager getManager() {`

`return manager;`

`}`

`public void destroy(@Disposes DocumentCollectionManager manager) {`

`manager.close();`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

To simplify code, we'll split the configuration information to the code; also, it's safer than putting the critical information such as password hardcode; indeed we'll use Eclipse MicroProfile Configuration.

In the `META-INF/microprofile-config.properties we'll put these information:`

`‘‘‘`

`document=document`

`document.database=olympus`

`document.settings.jakarta.nosql.host=localhost:27017`

`document.provider=org.eclipse.jnosql.diana.mongodb.document.MongoDBDocumentConfiguration`

`’’’`

Let's create our hello world with a Document database; this first piece of code will do the create, update, retrieve, and delete:

`‘‘‘java`

`import jakarta.nosql.document.DocumentDeleteQuery;`

`import jakarta.nosql.mapping.document.DocumentTemplate;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainer;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainerInitializer;`

`import java.util.Optional;`

`import static jakarta.nosql.document.DocumentDeleteQuery.delete;`

`public class DocumentApp {`

`public static void main(String[] args) {`

`try (SeContainer container = SeContainerInitializer.newInstance().initialize()) {`

`God diana = new God(1L, "Diana", "Hunt");`

`DocumentTemplate template = container.select(DocumentTemplate.class).get();`

`template.insert(diana);`

`final Optional<God> god = template.find(God.class, 1L);`

`System.out.println("query : " + god);`

`DocumentDeleteQuery deleteQuery = delete().from("God").where("_id").eq(1L).build();`

`template.delete(deleteQuery);`

`System.out.println("query again: " +`

`template.find(God.class, 1L));`

`}`

`System.exit(0);`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

The Jakarta NoSQL Document as key-value and `Column` API has the method insert with TTL, but MongoDB does not have support, thus if you execute this method it will throw an `UnsupportedOperationException`.

Jakarta NoSQL has a standard query as text to each NoSQL database so that you can use either a plain query or a `PrepareStatement` query:

`‘‘‘java`

`import jakarta.nosql.mapping.PreparedStatement;`

`import jakarta.nosql.mapping.document.DocumentTemplate;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainer;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainerInitializer;`

`import java.util.Optional;`

`public class DocumentApp2 {`

`public static void main(String[] args) {`

`try (SeContainer container = SeContainerInitializer.newInstance().initialize()) {`

`God diana = new God(1L, "Diana", "Hunt");`

`DocumentTemplate template = container.select(DocumentTemplate.class).get();`

`template.insert(diana);`

`Optional<God> god = template.singleResult("select * from God where _id = 1");`

`System.out.println("Plain query text : " + god);`

`PreparedStatement prepare = template.prepare("select * from God where _id = @id");`

`prepare.bind("id", 1L);`

`System.out.println("Query by prepare query" + prepare.getSingleResult());`

`template.query("delete from God where _id = 1");`

`System.out.println("query : " + template.find(God.class, 1L));`

`}`

`System.exit(0);`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

To simplify as Spring Data Jakarta NoSQL also has the repository feature:

`‘‘‘java`

`import jakarta.nosql.mapping.Repository;`

`import java.util.List;`

`public interface GodRepository extends Repository<God, Long> {`

`}`

`import org.eclipse.jnosql.artemis.DatabaseQualifier;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainer;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainerInitializer;`

`import java.util.Optional;`

`public class DocumentApp3 {`

`public static void main(String[] args) {`

`try (SeContainer container = SeContainerInitializer.newInstance().initialize()) {`

`God diana = new God(1L, "Diana", "Hunt");`

`GodRepository repository = container.select(GodRepository.class, DatabaseQualifier.ofDocument()).get();`

`repository.save(diana);`

`Optional<God> god = repository.findById(1L);`

`System.out.println("Query by id : " + god);`

`System.out.println("Query by Name : " + repository.findByName("Diana"));`

`repository.deleteById(1L);`

`}`

`System.exit(0);`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

# [Graph database](#toc.xhtml_s48a)

In this section, we'll explore the relationship between Java and Graph database, to Graph, we'll use Neo4J. Like any database, we need to define the connection and to Jakarta NoSQL, it isn't different. Briefly, we need to make a Graph, an `EntityManager` to Graph NoSQL, available to CDI:

`‘‘‘java`

`import org.apache.tinkerpop.gremlin.structure.Graph;`

`import org.eclipse.microprofile.config.inject.ConfigProperty;`

`import javax.enterprise.context.ApplicationScoped;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.Disposes;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.Produces;`

`import javax.inject.Inject;`

`@ApplicationScoped`

`class GraphProducer {`

`@Inject`

`@ConfigProperty(name = "graph")`

`private Graph graph;`

`@Produces`

`@ApplicationScoped`

`public Graph getGraph() {`

`return graph;`

`}`

`public void destroy(@Disposes Graph graph) throws Exception {`

`graph.close();`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

To simplify code, we'll split the configuration information to the code; also, it's safer than putting the critical information such as password hardcode; indeed we'll use Eclipse MicroProfile Configuration.

In the `META-INF/microprofile-config.properties` we'll put these information:

`‘‘‘`

`graph.settings.jakarta.nosql.host=bolt://localhost:7687`

`graph.settings.jakarta.nosql.user=neo4j`

`graph.settings.jakarta.nosql.password=admin`

`graph.provider=org.eclipse.jnosql.artemis.graph.connections.Neo4JRemote`

`’’’`

Let's create our hello world with a Graph database; this first piece of code will do the create, update, retrieve, and delete:

`‘‘‘java`

`import org.eclipse.jnosql.artemis.graph.GraphTemplate;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainer;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainerInitializer;`

`import java.util.Optional;`

`public class GraphApp {`

`public static void main(String[] args) {`

`try (SeContainer container = SeContainerInitializer.newInstance().initialize()) {`

`GraphTemplate template = container.select(GraphTemplate.class).get();`

`God diana = template.getTraversalVertex().hasLabel(God.class).has("name", "Diana").<God>next().orElseGet(() -> template.insert(new God(null, "Diana", "Hunt")));`

`System.out.println("query : " + diana);`

`template.delete(diana.getId());`

`Optional<God> god = template.getTraversalVertex(diana.getId()).next();`

`System.out.println("Query: " + god);`

`}`

`System.exit(0);`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

You can create a relationship and feed the Edges. The Communication Layer defines three new APIs: Key-Value, Document, and Column Family. It does not have a new Graph API, because a very good one already exists. Apache TinkerPop is a graph computing framework for both graph databases (OLTP) and graph analytic systems (OLAP). Using Apache TinkerPop as Communication API for Graph databases, the Mapping API has tight integration with it:

`‘‘‘java`

`import org.eclipse.jnosql.artemis.graph.GraphTemplate;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainer;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.se.SeContainerInitializer;`

`import java.util.Optional;`

`public class GraphApp2 {`

`public static void main(String[] args) {`

`try (SeContainer container = SeContainerInitializer.newInstance().initialize()) {`

`GraphTemplate template = container.select(GraphTemplate.class).get();`

`God diana = template.getTraversalVertex().hasLabel(God.class).has("name", "Diana").<God>next().orElseGet(() -> template.insert(new God(null, "Diana", "Hunt")));`

`God apollo = template.getTraversalVertex().hasLabel(God.class).has("name", "Apollo").<God>next().orElseGet(() -> template.insert(new God(null, "Apollo", "Sun")));`

`God zeus = template.getTraversalVertex().hasLabel(God.class).has("name", "Zeus").<God>next().orElseGet(() -> template.insert(new God(null, "Zeus", "Thunder")));`

`template.edge(diana, "brother", apollo);`

`template.edge(apollo, "brother", diana);`

`template.edge(zeus, "father", apollo);`

`template.edge(zeus, "father", diana);`

`template.delete(diana.getId());`

`template.delete(zeus.getId());`

`template.delete(apollo.getId());`

`}`

`System.exit(0);`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

The query that we created will run a query like a figure below:

![](images/3-4.jpg)

**Figure 3.4**

# [Conclusion](#toc.xhtml_s49a)

This chapter introduces Jakarta NoSQL, the motivation behind the API, and how we Java developers have to get involved. An important point is that a simple Java SE was made that with only one entity, God, was persisted into four databases, one for each supported database type.

In the next chapter, we will learn about JMoordb and how to use it in your Jakarta EE application.

# [CHAPTER 4](#toc.xhtml_c04)

# [Understanding Jmoordb](#toc.xhtml_c04)

Jmoordb is an Object Document Mapper for Java that supports NoSQL databases (MongoDB, OrientDB, Couchbase).

# [Structure](#toc.xhtml_s51a)

*   Introduction a Jmoordb
*   Entity
*   Annotation
    *   @Id
    *   @Embedded
    *   @Referenced
*   Repository
*   Create
*   Update
*   Delete
*   Search
*   Builder
*   Aggregation
*   Database Operations
*   Databases without schemas

# [Objective](#toc.xhtml_s52a)

This chapter will show the basic features of the Jmoordb framework and how to use them for Jakarta EE applications.

# [Introduction a JMoordb](#toc.xhtml_s53a)

It is a Java API for working with NoSQL databases. It supports methods to perform the most common operations on databases, collections, and documents. It is built to integrate with Java EE/Eclipse Jakarta. Benefiting from the APIs offered by the platform. Its syntax is very similar to the JPA specification, currently working to support Jakarta NoSQL.

This API is not a replacement for Jakarta NoSQL, it is an API focused mainly on MongoDB and document-oriented databases.

The following are the main features:

*   Syntax similar to JPA
*   Beans Validation (JSR-349)
*   Embedded documents
*   Referenced documents
*   Advanced searches.
*   Builders
*   Aggregation
*   Documents without schemas
*   SQL integration

Before starting the development of applications that use jmoordb it is important to understand some basic concepts that will help in the development of the applications.

# [Entity](#toc.xhtml_s54a)

An entity is a Java class that represents the attributes of a document. They are defined by the standard Java data types by adding a set of annotations provided by Jmoordb. These Java classes are serialized and deserialized to be stored and retrieved from the NoSQL database.

The following screenshot show a JSON document in a database:

![](images/4-1.jpg)

**Figure 4.1**

# [Annotation](#toc.xhtml_s55a)

Annotations allow us to decorate the attributes with types and aspects that are used by the API to interact with databases and perform operations on them. The following are the list of annotations we have the following:

*   `@Id:` Defines the key field of the document.
*   `@Ignore`: The attribute will be ignored in the document.
*   `@Embedded`: Defines an embedded document, the entire content will be stored in the document.
*   `@Referenced`: Defines a document that is referenced, only the value of the key field of the referenced document is stored.
*   `@Secondary`: Indicates that the attribute is a secondary key.

An example of a simple entity that represents a Person class:

`public class Person {`

`@Id`

`private String id;`

`private String name;`

`}`

# [@Id](#toc.xhtml_s56a)

The @Id annotation identifies the attribute used as the primary key, remember that NoSQL databases such as MongoDB use an internal field that identifies one document from another. Each entity must have a `@Id` attribute.

The primary keys are used to identify a unique document within the collection and avoid duplication of data. When key fields are `autoincrementable` type they do not have a direct representation in databases such as MongoDB we must establish their control by programming.

When you want to work with `autoincrementable` as primary keys, we have to keep in mind that some databases like MongoDB internally generate a field that is unique for each document but does not offer a mechanism to establish another field as a primary key that is autoincrementable. In jmoordb, we offer a simple way to implement it by means of `autoincrementableServices` that offers a `getContador("")` method that allows to take a counter of documents of the collections that are specified as parameters.

`Integer idperson = autoincrementableServices.getContador("person");`

# [@Ignore](#toc.xhtml_s57a)

In some situations, you define some attributes in the entity, but you do not want them to be stored in the database documents. That is, they are ignored by the API. For these types of fields we use the @Ignore annotation:

`public class Person {`

`@Id`

`private String id;`

`private String name;`

`@Ignore`

`private String comment;`

`}`

# [@Embedded](#toc.xhtml_s58a)

An embedded document is a document that is inserted into another document. It is one of the characteristics of NoSQL databases that makes it very useful and allows us to improve data models that are not possible to do in relational databases. The API allows us to define an embedded document or a list of embedded documents. This document is defined by another entity defined using the respective annotations.

The use of embedded documents depends on the particular situation in which you want to use them. For example, if you have documents embedded in others, and these need to be updated, you would have to review all the documents to update the embedded documents. This can be a limitation if the database is not well designed.

The following is an example of how to define embedded documents:

`public class Country {`

`@Id`

`private String id;`

`private String country;`

`}`

`public class Person {`

`@Id`

`private String id;`

`private String name;`

`@Embedded`

`private Country country;`

`}`

To define a list of embedded documents, use a syntax similar to this `List<T>:`

`public class Person {`

`@Id`

`private String idperson;`

`private String name;`

`@Embedded`

`List<Country> country;`

`}`

# [@Referenced](#toc.xhtml_s59a)

A document referenced is a document that stores the reference to the key field of the other document. Jmoordb offers a simple mechanism to manage references between documents. Using references avoids duplication of documents that can occur when using embedded documents. Their use is associated with the design of the database and is adapted to particular needs, simulating the relationships between documents.

For example, a document embedded with the information of a country is stored within 100000 documents of a collection called `Person`. You can see that the same information from the country Panama is found in all the documents of people living in Panama.

With that design the information is duplicated, consuming more storage space, and to make any change in the country's information, one would have to review all the documents of the people where they are and edit them, which would directly impact the performance of the application.

The following screenshot shows embedded and repeated documents:

![](images/4-2.jpg)

**Figure 4.2**

To avoid situations like the one described, we have the annotation `@Referenced,` which allows us to create references between documents. These references can be implemented through a specific attribute or a list.

This is very similar to the relationships between tables given in relational databases. When a search is made in a referenced document this query the other collection looking for the document containing the related field. Jmoordb offers the possibility to indicate if you want to load or not the entity that represents the referenced document that is to say to search or not in the other collection:

The following screenshot show documents referenced to `Country =pa:`

![](images/4-3.jpg)

**Figure 4.3**

`@Referenced` allows you to specify references to collections that do not necessarily have to be stored in the same database or on the same server. The following screenshot show referenced documents that are in databases stored in different servers:

![](images/4-4.jpg)

**Figure 4.4**

The `@Referenced` syntax is very simple that is as follows:

`@Referenced(`**`collection="", field="", javatype="", lazy="", repository="")`**

The following table shows the syntax of `@Referenced:`

| **Attribute** | **Description** |
| `collection` | Name of the referenced collection |
| `field` | Name of the key field that identifies the document in the collection |
| `javatype` | Indicates the data type of the primary key. The allowed types are (`String/Integer)` |
| `lazy` | It allows you to set whether or not you want to load the entities of the referenced documents by searching them. 
*   **False:** It indicates that when searching in the main document.
*   **True:** Indicates that when searching in a collection, the related collection will not be searched, only the id of the related collection will be returned.

 |
| `repository` | The name of the `Repository` of the referenced collection, we must indicate the package where it is located. |

**Table 4.1**

Example of an entity with referenced documents:

`public class Person {`

`@Id`

`private String idperson;`

`private String name;`

`@Referenced(collection = "Country",`

`field = "idperson", javatype = "String", lazy = false,`

`repository = "com.repository.CountryRepository")`

`Country country;`

`}`

Example of a list of referenced documents:

`public class Person {`

`@Id`

`private String idperson;`

`private String name;`

`@Referenced(collectiont = "Country",`

`field = "idperson", javatype = "String", lazy = false,`

`repository = "com.repository.CountryRepository")`

`List< Country> country;`

`}`

# [Repository](#toc.xhtml_s60a)

A repository is a class of type `@Stateless` (without state) where we can implement the methods to execute the operations on databases, collections, and documents. The API is integrated with Java Enterprise Edition/Jakarta, so it uses many components such as **Enterprise Java Beans (EJB)**, CDI, and others.

Jmoordb directly implements MongoDB's java driver which allows us to directly use all the methods offered by the driver. The MongoDB `Document` class is supported by Jmoordb and is used in many operations.

The API is integrated with Java Enterprise Edition/Jakarta, so it uses many components such as EJB, CDI and others. Example to define a simple repository:

`@Stateless`

`public class EntityRepository extends Repository<Entity> {`

`public EntityRepository(){`

`super(Entity.class);`

`}`

`}`

In the constructor you can indicate the name of the database and the collection:

`@Stateless`

`public class EntityRepository extends Repository<Entity> {`

`public EntityRepository(){`

`super(Entity.class,"databases","collecction");`

`}`

`}`

The methods have been organized into groups as described below:

*   C.R.U.D.
    *   Create
    *   Read
    *   Update
    *   Delete
*   Database Operations
*   Databases without schemas

# [C.R.U.D.](#toc.xhtml_s61a)

In the world of relational databases, the acronym CRUD (Create, Read, Update, and Delete) is used for the most common database operations. In NoSQL databases, we can handle very similar persistence operations with this type of operation. Many of these databases include transaction support, which makes them ideal for a large number of solutions.

The most common methods used to perform these operations are described below.

# [Create](#toc.xhtml_s62a)

One of the common operations is to store a series of documents in a collection. From java through a repository performs the conversion to JSON/BSON to be stored in the database. Jmoordb performs serialization and deserialization to ensure data integrity. Using the `save`() method, it is possible to save an entity in a collection.

The following table shows the syntax of the `save`() method:

| **Method** | **Description** |
| `Boolean save(T t, Boolean… verifyID)` | Saves the entity in the database. |
| `Boolean save(Document doc,Boolean… verifyID)` | Saves a document in the database |

**Table 4.2**

The following table describes the parameters of the `save`() method:

| **Parameter** | **Description** |
| `T t` | Represents the entity to be saved |
| `Document doc` | It's a MongoDB document. |
| `Boolean… verifyID: true` | It is optional, by default it indicates that you must verify that no other document with that id exists. |
| `Boolean… verifyID: false` | Tells jmoordb not to search if there is another document with the id. |

**Table 4.3**

Here's an example of saving an entity in a MongoDB collection:

`Planet planet = new Planet("earth", "Earth", new Date());`

`if (planetRepository.save(planet)) {`

`}`

Remember that the MongoDB java driver is supported, so you can use a document class to be stored in the collection:

`Document doc = new Document("id", 3).append("planet", "Jupiter").append("date", new Date());`

`if (planetRepository.save(doc)) {`

`}`

# [Update](#toc.xhtml_s63a)

To update one or more documents the `update`() methods are offered with their different variants. They allow you to carry out most of the operations taking advantage of them. The methods supported for updating are described in the following table:

| **Method** | **Description** |
| `Boolean update(T t)` | Updates a document with the values of the entity. |
| `Integer update(Document docSearch, Document docUpdate)` | Updates a document based on the restrictions to a document containing the values to be updated and the search is performed based on a document. |
| `Integer updateMany(Document docSearch, Document docUpdate)` | Updates multiple documents. |
| `Integer replaceOne(String key, String value, Document docUpdate)` | Replaces a document on the basis of the value pair used to perform the search. |
| `Integer replaceOne(Bson search, Document docUpdate)` | `Replaces one document with another.` |
| `Integer updateMany(Document docSearch, Document docUpdate)` | Updates multiple documents. |

**Table 4.4**

In the following code segment the `Population` attribute of an entity called `Planet` is updated, note that it is a simple invocation to the `update`() method.

`planet.setPopularion(50000);`

`if(planetRepository.update(planet)){`

`}`

Through the MongoDB `Document` class you can update the document in the collection:

`planetRepository.update(newDocument("id","earth"),new Document("$set", new`

`Document("planet","New Earth")));`

Embedded documents can be updated in a simple way using `document.field`. In the following example two entities will be created where an embedded document will be implemented:

`@Getter`

`@Setter`

`public class Country {`

`@Id`

`private String idcountry;`

`private String country;`

`private Integer population;`

`@Embedded`

`private Continent continent;`

`}`

`@Getter`

`@Setter`

`public class Continent {`

`@Id`

`private String idcontinent;`

`private String continent;`

`private String active;`

`}`

`countryRepository.updateOne(new Document("continent.idcontinent","am").append("active", "yes"),`

`new Document("$set", new Document("country","Panama").append("population",860000)));`

The update method updates a single document, for cases where several documents need to be updated use the `updateMany` method:

`countryRepository.updateMany(new Document("country","Colombia"),`

`new Document("$set", new Document("country","Colombia").append("population",545337)));`

# [Delete](#toc.xhtml_s64a)

Delete a document or several documents are supported in the API, in a very simple way using the `delete`() method. The following table shows the different ways to implement the `delete` method:

| **Method** | **Description** |
| `delete(Document)` | Deletes the document. |
| `delete(String sql)` | Supports SQL to create the filter to remove the document that meets the condition. |
| `delete(key, value)` | Deletes a document whose search matches the specified conditions. |
| `deleteAll()` | Deletes all documents. |
| `deleteMany(doc)` | Deletes all documents that meet the condition specified in the search document. |
| `deleteMany(sql)` | Deletes all documents that meet the condition indicated in the SQL query. |
| `deleteMany(key, value)` | Deletes all documents that meet the condition specified in the filter. |

**Table 4.5**

To delete a document, you should specify the from field and the value, which will be used to find the document that meets that condition and then delete it:

`if(countryRepository.delete("idcountry", country.getIdcountry())){`

`}`

The `deleteAll()` method allows us to remove all documents from a collection, it is an operation that must be considered when being used to avoid situations:

`if (countryRepository.deleteAll() != 0) {`

`}`

# [Read](#toc.xhtml_s65a)

In this section, you will learn the methods to search for documents. Remember that NoSQL databases have their syntax and search mechanisms different from relational databases. Jmoordb offers a simple way to interact with documents to retrieve them.

The searches within jmoordb have been classified into the following groups:

*   Search by primary key
*   Search by a secondary key
*   Searches by any attribute

# Search by primary key

The following table shows the methods for searching by primary key:

| **Method** | **Description** |
| `public Optional<T> findById(T t2)` | Searches a collection for the document by comparing the primary key defined in the `@Id` attribute. |
| `public Optional<T> findById(Document doc)` | Search in a collection the document comparing the last search document as a parameter. |
| `public Optional<T> findById(String sql)` | MongoDB (Supports direct SQL) |

**Table 4.6**

*   `findById`(): Read operations are performed by querying collections. and complex queries can be performed as shown below:

    `Planet planet =new Planet();`

    `planet.setIdplanet("earth");`

    `Optional<Planet> p1 = planetRepository.findById(planet);`

    `if (p1.isPresent()) {`

    `planet = p1.get();`

    `}`

*   **Search based on documents:** Jmoordb supports direct search based on MongoDB documents. For its use it is recommended to have basic knowledge about MongoDB. The example is very simple: it shows the search through a document where we want to look for the `idplanet`.

    `Document doc = new Document("idplanet", "earth");`

    `Optional<Planet> p1 = planetRepository.findById(doc);`

    `if (p1.isPresent()) {`

    `planet = p1.get();`

    `}`

*   Direct SQL query support: Many developers are familiar with the SQL language, the API allows the use of these instructions to perform many search operations:

    `Optional<Planet> p1 = planetRepository.findById("select * from planet where idplanet='earth'");`

    `if (p1.isPresent()) {`

    `planet = p1.get();`

    `}`

**Searches a document by secondary key(s)**

The keys you can use the attributes directly or in a faster way through `findBySecondaryKey`() method that identifies in the entity the attributes declared with `@Secondary` and that will be explained in this section. The following table shows the methods for searching by secondary key:

| **Method** | **Description** |
| `Optional<T> findBySecondaryKey(T t2)` | Searches for an entity with the `@Secondary` attribute defined. |
| `Optional<T> findBySecondaryKey(Document doc)` | Searches for an entity with a document that defines the secondary key. |

**Table 4.7**

The `Client` class is an entity that contains a `@Secondary` attribute that defines the code attribute as a secondary key:

`public class Client {`

`@Id`

`private Integer id;`

`@Secondary`

`private String code;`

`private String name;`

`….`

`}`

Now implement a `save`() method where it is verified if there is a document with the secondary key to avoid data duplication before saving the document:

`public String save(){`

`Client client = new Client(25,"av","avbravo");`

`Optional<Client> optional = clientRepository.findBySecondaryKey(client);`

`if (!optional.isPresent()) {`

`clientRepository.save(cliente);`

`}`

`}`

# Searches by any attribute

Jmoordb allows searches by any attribute, in addition to supporting complex searches that offer greater functionality for routine operations with NoSQL databases. This allows us to easily extend the API for a series of operations.

The following table shows the syntax of the method:

| **Method** | **Description** |
| `public Optional<T> find(String key, Object value);` | Performs a search based on key/value pair |
| `public Optional<T> find(Document document)` | Searches based on a document |
| `public Optional<T> find(Bson filter)` | Search based on a filter |
| `public Optional<T> find(String statement)` | Search based on a SQL instruction |

**Table 4.8**

In the following example, a search is performed on a collection of documents that matches `idplanet="earth":`

`Planet planet = new Planet();`

`Optional<Planet> p1 = planetRepository.find("idplanet", "earth");`

`if (p1.isPresent()) {`

`planet = p1.get();`

`}`

You can use the MongoDB document class to extend the search conditions within Jmoordb. As shown in the following code segment:

`Document doc = new Document("idplanet","earth");`

`Planet planet = new Planet();`

`Optional<Planet> p2 = planetRepository.find(doc);`

`if (p2.isPresent()) {`

`planet = p2.get();`

`}`

# [Filter](#toc.xhtml_s66a)

Filters simplify the construction of complex queries. MongoDB filters are supported directly in jmoordb. In the following link, you will find the definitions of filters used in MongoDB: **[https://mongodb.github.io/mongo-java-driver/3.0/builders/filters/](https://mongodb.github.io/mongo-java-driver/3.0/builders/filters/)**

Example using operator eq, in the same way, we can implement the other operators.

`Planet planet = new Planet();`

`Optional<Planet> p2 = planetRepository.find(eq("idplanet",""earth");`

`if (!p2.isPresent()) {`

`planet = p2.get();`

`}`

# Searching embedded documents

Usually, you will have embedded documents, so looking inside them is a fairly common task. Look at the following document showing an embedded planet document:

`{`

`"_id" : ObjectId("5884e4ffb2a08a10f017f673"),`

`"idcontinent" : "as",`

`"continent" : "Asia",`

`"planet" : {`

`"idplanet" : "mars",`

`"planet" : "Mars",`

`"fecha" : ISODate("2019-01-21T21:21:42.044Z")`

`}`

`}`

To perform the search, indicate the name of the embedded document and the attribute by which we want to perform the search:

`Continent continent = new Continent();`

`Optional<Continent> c = continentRepository.find(new Document("planet.idplanet", "mars"));`

`if (c.isPresent()) {`

`continent =c.get();`

`}`

The result would be similar to:

`Continent{`

`idcontinent=as,`

`continent=Asia,`

`planet=Planet{`

`idplanet=mars,`

`planet=Mars,`

`fecha=Sat Jan 21 16:21:42 EST 2019`

`}`

`}`

# Search a list of embedded documents

Searching a list of embedded documents is the same as searching a single embedded document. Create a document-type object with the conditions you want to search for and run the search operation through the repository.

In the following example, we see a document with embedded documents and a segment of code in java where the implementation takes place:

`db.continent.find().pretty()`

`{`

`"_id" : ObjectId("5884f516b2a08a0ceb287e99"),`

`"idcontinent" : "eu",`

`"continent" : "Europe",`

`"planet" : [`

`{`

`"idplanet" : "mars",`

`"planet" : "Mars",`

`"fecha" : ISODate("2017-01-21T21:21:42.044Z")`

`},`

`{`

`"idplanet" : "earth",`

`"planet" : "Earth",`

`"fecha" : ISODate("2017-01-21T21:21:42.044Z")`

`}`

`]}`

`{`

`"_id" : ObjectId("5884f585b2a08a0da194491d"),`

`"idcontinent" : "am",`

`"continent" : "America",`

`"planet" : [`

`{`

`"idplanet" : "mars",`

`"planet" : "Mars",`

`"fecha" : ISODate("2017-01-21T21:21:42.044Z")`

`}`

`]}`

The Continent class is then created in which a list of embedded documents is defined:

`public class Continent {`

`@Id`

`private String idcontinent;`

`private String continent;`

`@Embedded`

`private List<Planet> planet;;`

`}`

Now we perform the search

`Continent continent = new Continent();`

`List<Continent> l = continentRepository.findBy(new Document("planet.idplanet", "mars"));`

# Search in referenced documents

A document referenced in jmoordb is a document that stores only the reference to the key field of the other document, so that the content of the referenced document is not stored inside it. The framework performs the operations to serialize and deserialize respectively. The results will be different depending on the lazy value defined in the `@Referenced`() notation. The annotation `@Referenced` has the following syntax:

`@Referenced(collection = " ",field = " ", javatype = " ", lazy = false, repository = "")`

`private List<Planet> planet;`

*   `collection`: It's the name of the referenced collection..
*   `field`: Name of the key field that identifies the document in a collection
*   `javatype`: Indicates the data type of the primary key.(`String/Integer`)
*   `lazy`: Sets whether or not to browse documents in referenced collections. False indicates that searching a Collection will search the related collection.

    `public class Continent {`

    `@Id`

    `private String idcontinent;`

    `private String continent;`

    `@Referenced(collection= "Planet",`

    `field = "idplanet", javatype = "String", lazy = false,`

    `repository = “com.avbravo.repository.PlanetRepository”)`

    `private Planet planet;`

    `}`

*   `findAll`: The following table shows the syntax of the method:

    | **Method** | **Description** |
    | `List <T> findAll()` | Returns all documents from the collection. |
    | `List <T> findAll(Document… docSort)` | The docSort parameter is optional and represents a document specifying the sorting of the results list.To sort we place the attribute we want to sort by and assign the value of 1. |
    | `Integer limitOfDocumentInFindAllMethod` | Defines the limit of documents in a `findAll`() method, default is 4000. |

**Table 4.9**

Examples

`List<Continent> list = continentRepository.findAll();`

Set the limit for the documents you will return, remember that jmoordb also supports pagination, in this case we will limit the amount of documents the `findAll`() method can return. With the method `setLimitOfDocumentInFindAllMethod`() we can indicate the limit of documents:

`continentRepository.setLimitOfDocumentInFindAllMethod(125);`

`List<Continent> list = continenteRepository.findAll();`

*   `findBy`: The `findBy` method allows us to perform various searches and return a list of entities of the same type indicated in the `Repository` class. The following table shows the `findBy` method with the different parameters it supports:

    | **Method** | **Description** |
    | `public List < T > findBy(Document doc, Document… docSort)` | Search a collection based on one document and sorting is optional based on another document. |
    | `public List< T > findBy(String key, Object value, Document… docSort)` | Search a collection based on a key/value pair and sorting is optional based on another document. |
    | `public List<T> findBy(String sql)` | Search based on a SQL condition |
    | `public List<T> findBy(Bson builder, Document… docSort)` | Search based on a filter. |

    **Table 4.10**

We will show a series of short examples showing the use of the `findBy`() method:

`List <Continent > list =continentRepository.findBy(new Document("idcontinent", "am"));`

Find and sort documents:

`List <Continent > list =continentRepository.findBy(new Document("idcontinent", "am"), new Document("idcontinent",1);`

Searches for the embedded document without sorting:

`List<Country> list =countryRepository.findBy(new Document("continent.idcontinent", "5"));`

Search the embedded document sorted by Country:

`List<Country > list =countryRepository..findBy(new Document("continent.idcontinent", "5"), new Document("country", 1));`

Search the document for those with a population greater than 500 unordered:

`List<Country> list =countryRepository.findBy(new Document("population", new Document("$gt", 500));`

Search in the document those that the population is greater than 500 ordered by Country:

`List<Country> list =countryRepository.findBy(new Document("population", new Document("$gt", 500), new Document("country",1));`

Search in the document for those with a population of less than 200:

`List <Country> list =countryRepository.findBy(new Document("population", new Document("$lt", 200));`

Search the collection for documents with a population equal to 800:

`List<Country > list =countryReository.findBy(new Document("population", new Document("$eq", 800));`

Search referenced documents:

`List<Country > list =countryReository.findBy(new Document("idcontinent","am");`

# [Builder](#toc.xhtml_s67a)

Builder allows us to build complex queries through operators. In the MongoDB portal, you can find more references (**[https://mongodb.github.io/mongo-java-driver/3.2/builders/](https://mongodb.github.io/mongo-java-driver/3.2/builders/)).**

Among some of the operators:

*   `eq`
*   `gt`
*   `gte`
*   `lt`
*   `lte`
*   `ne`
*   `in`
*   `nin`

For example a compound search (`active="yes" and mark.idmark=9):`

`List<Article> list = articleRepository.findBy(and(eq("active","yes"),eq("mark.idmark",9)));`

# [Pagination](#toc.xhtml_s68a)

The pagination helps us to process a large volume of data in an efficient way to avoid overloading entities with unnecessary documents. Instead of loading all the documents of a collection only a few are loaded and then they are moved in the pages, this impacts favorably on the performance of the applications.

The following table shows the syntax of the method:

| **Method** | **Description** |
| `public List< T> findPagination(Document filter, Integer pageNumber, Integer rowsForPage, Document… docSort)` | Indicate the filter, the page number, and the number of rows per page. |
| `public List< T> findPagination(String sql, Integer pageNumber, Integer rowsForPage` | Indicate the SQL condition, the page number, and the number of rows per page. |

**Table 4.11**

Parameters are used for:

*   `pageNumber:` Current page number.
*   `rowsForPage` Number of rows per page.
*   `Document… docSort`: Optional document to order.

You can use the `sizePage`(`Integer rowsForPage, Document …doc`) method to know the number of pages in the collection based on the number of rows you want per page:

`Integer size = countryRepository.sizePage(10);`

Get the first page composed by the first 10 documents of the collection:

`List<Country> countryList = countryRepository.findPagination(1,10);`

It is possible to use sorting and other operations implementing paging.

`List<Country> countryList =countryRepository.findPagination(1,10, new Document("country",1));`

Filter and sort by country name and bring the first page of 10 rows per page:

`List<Country> countryList = countryRepository.findPagination(new Document("country", "America")1,10, new Document("country", 1));`

Implementing a page with a SQL query:

`String sql = "select * from country where idcountry='pa'";`

List<Country> countryList = countryRepository.findPagination(sql,1,10);

*   `findFirst`: It returns the first document from the collection. The following table shows the syntax of the method:

    | **Method** | **Description** |
    | `public Optional<T> findFirst(Document… doc)` | Returns the first document in the collection that meets the condition stated in `Document`. |
    | `public Optional<T> findFirst(String sql)` | Returns the first document in the collection that meets the condition indicated in the SQL command. |

    **Table 4.12**

This example searches for the first document in the collection:

`Optional<Country> optional = countryRepository.findFirst(new Document("idcountry","pa");`

`if (optional.isPresent()) {`

`Country c = optional.get();`

`}`

# [SQL support](#toc.xhtml_s69a)

The basic SQL support has been included within the framework, through the integration of an interesting library that converts SQL instructions to MongoDB queries. The integration has been done to make it easy to use with jmoordb through the repository class. The API was developed by Vincent Russell. You can check the code in the repository:

**[https://github.com/vincentrussell/sql-to-mongo-db-query-converter](https://github.com/vincentrussell/sql-to-mongo-db-query-converter)**

These methods have been described in the various sections of this chapter, and a quick guide to their application is shown in the following sections. The following table shows the syntax of the method:

| **Method** | **Description** |
| `public Optional <T> findById(String sql)` | Search by primary key. |
| `public List< T> findPagination(String sql, Integer pageNumber, Integer rowsForPage)` | Search using pagination. |
| `public List<T> findBy(String sql)` | Find a document that meets the SQL condition. |
| `public Boolean delete(String sql)` | Deletes a document that matches the condition indicated in the SQL. |
| `public Boolean deleteMany(String sql)` | Deletes many documents that match the condition indicated in the SQL. |

**Table 4.13**

The following are examples of the use of direct SQL instructions operating on database collections.

Obtain the first country of the collection where the population is older than 50 years:

`String sql ="select * from country where population > 50";`

`Optional<Country> optional = countryRepository.findFirst(sql);`

`if (optional.isPresent()) {`

`Country c = optional.get();`

`}`

Execute a SQL query with invoke sorting:

`List<Invoice> invoice = invoiceRepository.findBy("select * from Invoice where status.idstatus=1 order by invoice");`

Example using `findById`() filtering by `idplanet`:

`Planet planet = new Planet();`

`Optional<Planet> p2 = planetRepository.findById("select * from p where idplanet='earth'");`

`if (p2.isPresent()) {`

`planetas =p2.get();`

`}`

Using a SQL filter with paginations:

`String sql = "select * from country where idcountry='pa' order by idcountry";`

`List<Country> countryList = countryRepository.findPagination(sql,1,10);`

For deletions, the instructions offer several alternatives, which are described below. Delete a document whose `idcountry` is equal to pa:

`String sql ="delete from country where idcountry='pa'";`

`countryRepository.delete(sql);`

To delete many documents we use the `deleteMany`() method:

`String sql ="delete from country where idcountry='pa'";`

`countryRepository.deleteMany(sql);`

# [Aggregation](#toc.xhtml_s70a)

Aggregation allows us to execute operations to return results. These operations can be grouped on a set of data, which facilitates the operations to be carried out on the documents of the database. Using Mongodb's Java driver you get support for aggregates and can implement it with jmoordb for ease of use.

An example of them can be seen in the MongoDB site:

**[https://docs.mongodb.com/manual/aggregation/](https://docs.mongodb.com/manual/aggregation/)**

*   $match
*   $group

Jmoordb uses the `JmoordbResult` class to return the results of the Aggregation operations to be performed.

The following screenshot show the aggregation implementation in jmoordb:

![](images/4-5.jpg)

**Figure 4.5**

A list of methods for aggregation support can be found in the following table. The following table shows the syntax of the method:

| **Method** | **Description** |
| `List<JmoordbResult> aggregateFromDocument(List<Document> documentList)` | Executes aggregation operations based on a Document list. |
| `List<JmoordbResult> aggregateFromBuilder(List<Bson> builder)` | Executes aggregation operations based on a list of filters in a `Document`. |
| `List<T> aggregateToEntity(List<Document> documentList)` | Returns an entity list based on the list of past aggregations in the document. |

**Table 4.14**

Supports Aggregation Builder, makes building sentences easier. The `JmoordbResult` class contains a Hashmap, with the result of the aggregation. Values can be obtained by using the `get("name")` method. It will return the name of the field used to add it as `_id`. The following table shows the syntax of the method:

| **Method** | **Description** |
| `String get("_id")` | The value of the field used to group it. It will always return as `_id`. |
| `String get("name")` | What is defined for the result? |

**Table 4.15**

Example of the use of the get method:

`list.forEach((a) -> {`

`System.out.println(a.get("_id") + " " + a.get("idcountry"));`

`})`

*   `$match:` Filter all countries whose continent is America, note that it is an embedded document:

    `List<Document> documentList = Arrays.asList(`

    `new Document("$match",`

    `new Document("continent.idcontinent", "am")));`

    `List<Country> list = countryRepository.aggregateToEntity(documentList);`

    `list.forEach((a) -> {`

    `System.out.println(" "+a.getDescription());`

    `});`

*   `$group:` It allows us to group collections and execute operations on them. For example, in MongoDB we will group on the basis of the active field and we will total for each group.

    Example: To count the articles grouped by active, if we used the MongoDB shell we should execute an instruction similar to this one:

    `db.article.aggregate([{"$group": {"_id": {"_active" : "$active"}, "total": {"$sum": 1}}}])`

    `An output like this would be generated:`

    `{"_id" : {"_active" : "yes"}, "total" : 1790}`

    `{"_id" : {"_active" : "no"}, "total" : 2}`

    Below is an example of its implementation using jmoordb. An `ArrayList` of Document is created, where the aggregation is used and the result will be returned in a `List<>` of type `JmoordbResult`, which consists of a key/value to obtain the values:

    `List<Document> documentList = Arrays.asList(`

    `new Document("$group",`

    `new Document("_id", "$" + "active").append("quantity", new Document("$sum", 1))));`

    `List<JmoordbResult> list = articleRepository.aggregateFromDocument(documentList);`

    `list.forEach((a) -> {`

    `System.out.println("_id " + a.get("_id") + " quantity: " + a.get("quantity"));`

    `});`

**$match, $group**

Filter items with `brand.idmark=9` groups them by asset and counts the quantity. Example using MongoDB's shell:

`db.article.aggregate([{"$match" :{"trademark.idtrademark:9}}, {"$group":{_id:{"active":"$active"},quantity:{$sum",1}}}])`

`The result would be:`

`{"_id" : {"active" : "yes"}, "quantity" : 95}`

`{"_id" : {"active" : "no"}, "quantity" : 2}`

We will follow some steps for the realization:

*   Define the filter with $match where the articles will be filtered with `brand.idmark=9.`
*   Group by active field `$group`.
*   Count the number of documents quantity using `$sum, 1`.

`List<Document> documentList = Arrays.asList(new Document("$match",`

`new Document("trademark.idtrademark", 9)),`

`new Document("$group",new Document("_id", "$" + "active").append("quantity", new Document("$sum", 1))));`

`List<JmoordbResult> list = articleRepository.aggregateFromDocument(documentList);`

`list.forEach((a) -> {`

`System.out.println("_id " + a.get("_id") + " quantity: " + a.get("quantity"));`

`});`

$match with $group totaling the total cost

Filter the articles with `mark.idmark=9` groups them by asset and adds the `$cost`. We run it from the MongoDB shell:

`db.article.aggregate([{"$match" :{"trademark.idtrademark":9}}, {"$group":{_id:{"active":"$active"},cost_total:{$sum:"$cost"}}}])`

The result could be similar to this:

`{"_id" : {"active" : "yes"}, "cost_total" : 13844.6}`

`{"_id" : {"active" : "no"}, "cost_total" : 370}`

The following code is the implementation in Java through jmoordb:

`List<Document> documentList = Arrays.asList(`

`new Document("$match", new Document("trademark.idtrademark", 9)),`

`new Document("$group",`

`new Document("_id", "$" + "active").append("totalcost", new Document("$sum", "$cost"))));`

`List<JmoordbResult> list = articleRepository.aggregateFromDocument(documentList);`

`list.forEach((a) -> {`

`System.out.println("_id " + a.get("_id") + "Total Cost: " + a.get("totalcost"));`

`});`

The output can be similar to this:

`id: yes Total Cost: 13844.6`

`id:no Total Cost: 370`

In the following example where we add the amount grouped by the active field:

`List<Bson> builder = Arrays.asList(group("$active", sum("total", "$quantity")));`

`List<JmoordbResult> list = articleRepository.aggregateFromBuilder(builder);`

`list.forEach((a) -> {`

`System.out.println("_id " + a.get("_id") + " Total: " + a.get("total"));`

`});`

# [Database operations](#toc.xhtml_s71a)

Jmoordb offers a number of methods and utilities to manage the database and collections. Such as counters, index creation, dynamically create databases, and collections, among the most common functions. The following table shows the syntax of the method:

| **Method** | **Description** |
| `internalQueryExecMaxBlockingSort Bytes(Integer bytes)` | Number of RAM bytes for collections with thousands of documents |
| `JmoordbStatistics statistics()` | Returns statistics from the database |
| `Integer count(Document… doc)` | Returns the number of documents in a collection. |
| `Integer count(Filter filter)` | Returns the number of documents in a collection by implementing a filter. |
| `sizeOfPage` | Returns the total number of pages in a collection. |
| `createIndex(Document… doc)` | Create indexes in a collection |
| `public Boolean createPrimaryIndex()` | It creates a primary index. |
| `public List<String> listCollecctions()` | Get the list of collections in the database. |
| `public Boolean createCollection(String nameCollection)` | Create a collection |
| `public Boolean drop()` | Eliminate a collection |
| `public Document toDocument(Object t)` | Converts an object to document, useful for operations where we need to handle a document and not a Java object. |
| `public Boolean dropDatabase()` | Delete database |
| `public Boolean disconnect()` | Disconnects the database connection |
| `ListOfPage` | Returns a list of integers corresponding to all available page numbers. |

**Table 4.16**

# [Allocate memory](#toc.xhtml_s72a)

In this example, we allocate RAM memory to process the documents using the method `internalQueryExecMaxBlockingSortBytes()`:

`if(myRepository.internalQueryExecMaxBlockingSortBytes(150151432)){`

`}`

# [Statistics](#toc.xhtml_s73a)

The `JmoordbStatictics` class allows us to obtain statistics from the database offered by MongoDB. The class has the following structure that you can use at your discretion:

`public class JmoordbStatistics {`

`private String db;`

`private Integer collections;`

`private Integer objects;`

`private Double avgObjSize;`

`private Integer dataSize;`

`private Integer storageSize;`

`private Integer numExtents;`

`private Integer indexes;`

`private Integer indexSize;`

`private Integer fileSize;`

`private Integer nsSizeMB;`

`private String dataFileVersion;`

`}`

Example:

`JmoordbStatistics jmoordbStatitics = myepository.statistics();`

`jmoordbStatitics.toString();`

# [count](#toc.xhtml_s74a)

To count the number of documents in a collection or count based on some filters use the `count()` method. Example of a collection in MongoDB:

`{`

`"idcontinent" : "am",`

`"continent" : "America",`

`"planet" : {`

`"idplanet" : "earth"`

`}`

`}`

`{`

`"idcontinent" : "eu",`

`"continent" : "Europe",`

`"planet" : {`

`"idplanet" : "earth"`

`}`

`}`

`{`

`"idcontinent" : "af",`

`"continent" : "Africa",`

`"planet" : {`

`"idplanet" : "earth"`

`}`

`}`

Next, you can see a series of examples of the `count()` method. Counts all the documents in the collection:

`Integer n = continentRepository.count();`

**Results: 3**

Count the elements on the basis of a filter:

`Integer n = continentRepository.count(new Document("idcontient","am");`

**Results: 1**

**Count referenced documents:** Counts the number of documents in the collection continents that belongs to planet Earth. In referenced documents:

`Integer n = continentRepository.count(new Document("planet",new Document("idplanet","earth")));`

# [sizeOfPage](#toc.xhtml_s75a)

Returns the total number of pages in a collection. It also allows you to assign the number of pages per collection. I will show you some examples of use. Assign 5 pages per collection:

`Integer size = countryRepository.sizeOfPage(5);`

Browse all the countries of the continent, with 5-row pages:

`Integer size = countryRepository.sizeOfPage(5, new Document("idcontinent","am")`

# [createIndex(Documment… doc)](#toc.xhtml_s76a)

Indexes allow you to optimize database query operations, greatly improving application performance. Following are some examples of use: improving notably the performance of the applications. Here are some examples of use:

*   Create a primary key index:

    `planetRepository.createIndex()`

*   Create an index based on the planet attribute:

    `planetRepository.createIndex(new Document("planet",1));`

*   Creates a composite index:

    `planetRepository.createIndex(new Document("idplanet",1).append("planet",1);`

*   Create text index for text search, widely used in components such as autocomplete:

    `planetRepository.createIndex(new Document("idplanet","text"));`

`list.forEach((s) -> {`

`});`

Displays all collections in the database.

`if(planetRepository.createCollection("planet")){`

`}`

    `if(planetRepository.drop("continent")){`

It returns a list of integers corresponding to all available page numbers. It can be used when we have a datatable and we want to show in a `SelectOneMenu` the list of pages and that the user selects a number from the list to scroll to that page:

`Integer rowPage =25;`

`List<Integer> pages = new ArrayList<>();`

# [Databases without schemas](#toc.xhtml_s83a)

NoSQL databases allow us to store unstructured information, Jmoordb offers the jmoordbResult class to manage the results:

*   Within the framework, we can make queries about these databases and return a `List<JmoordbResult>` that contains a HashMap, on which we can go through it to process the results.
*   Among the limitations the embedded documents are returned as a String with the following format `Document{field1:value1, fieldn:valuen}}`.

The following table shows the syntax of the method:

| **Method** | **Description** |
| `List<JmoordbResult> unknown(String database,String collection, Document… doc)` | Query on an unstructured database. |
| `List<JmoordbResult>unknown Sort(String database,String collection, Document doc,Document… docSort)` | Searches through the query made in a Document and optionally supports sorting. |
| `List<JmoordbResult> unknown(String database,String collection, Bson filter,Document… docSort)` | It searches through a filter implemented in a Document and optionally supports sorting. |
| `List<JmoordbResult> unknownPagination(String database,String collection,Integer pageNumber, Integer rowsForPage, Document… doc)` | Makes searches with pagination |
| `List<JmoordbResult> unknownPaginationSort(String database,String collection,Integer pageNumber, Integer rowsForPage, Document doc,Document… docSort)` | Makes searches with page and sorting |

**Table 4.17**

The following MongoDB collection will be used to develop an example. If you look at the stored information it's not structured:

`{`

`{`

`"id":"78",`

`"name":"Ann"`

`}`

`{`

`"id":"pa",`

`"average":54`

`}`

`{`

`"father":{`

`"name":"Peter",`

`"age":47`

`}`

`}`

To run queries on these documents, use the `unknown()` method. As shown below:

`List<JmoordbResult> list = documentRepository.unknown("database","collection");`

`list.stream().map((j) -> {`

`return j;`

`}).forEachOrdered((j) -> {`

`j.getField().forEach((k, v) -> System.out.println(" Key: " + k + ": Value: " + v));`

`});`

The result would be similar to:

`key: id value: 58`

`key: name value: Ann`

`key: id value: pa`

`key: average value:54`

`key: father value: Document{"name":"Peter","age":47}}`

The following table shows the syntax of the method for insertion, editing and deletion operations:

| **Method** | **Description** |
| `Boolean unknownSave(String database, String collection,Document doc)` | Save a document in the collection |
| `Integer unknownReplaceOne(String database, String collection, Bson builder, Document docUpdate)` | Updates a single document based on a filter condition, |
| `Boolean unknownDelete(String database, String collection, Document doc)` | Deletes a document based on a query using a Document() class |
| `Boolean unknownDelete(String database, String collection, Bson builder)` | Removes a document on the basis of a Bson filter |
| `Boolean unknownDeleteAll(String database, String collection)` | Removes all documents from the database |
| `Boolean unknownDeleteMany(String database, String collection, Document doc)` | Deletes multiple documents with the set condition. |
| `Boolean unknownDeleteMany(String database, String collection, Bson builder) {` | Eliminates multiple documents based on a Bson |

**Table 4.18**

Examples: Inserting a new unstructured document:

`Document doc = new Document("id","x5").append("name","Ann");`

`myRepository.unknownSave("book", "mycollection", doc);`

*   **Delete a document:** Delete an unstructured document in the collection, specify any attribute by which we want to perform the search.

    `Document doc = new Document("id","x5");`

    `if(myRepository.unknownDelete("store","unknown",doc)){`

    `}`

*   Deleting a document using a Filter `eq():`

    `if(myRepository.unknownDelete("mydatabase","mycollection",eq("id","x5"))){`

    `}`

*   Update a document with unstructured information.

    `Document docUpdate = new Document("id","x5").append("name","Stephany");`

    `if(myepository.unknownReplaceOne("mydatabase","mycollection",eq("id","x5"), docUpdate)>0){`

    `}`

# [Conclusion](#toc.xhtml_s84a)

This chapter shows an introduction to jmoordb with the basic operations for managing NOSQL databases. We explore the various methods grouped into the categories of most common use, you must remember that we implemented the java driver for MongoDB so you have access to all the features of the API. Jmoordb simplifies the development of NOSQL applications, especially for MongoDB databases.

# [CHAPTER 5](#toc.xhtml_c05)

# [Exploring Eclipse MicroProfile](#toc.xhtml_c05)

This chapter explores one of the most competitive areas in application development today, which is the creation of microservices with Microprofile.

# [Structure](#toc.xhtml_s86a)

*   Skeleton of Eclipse MicroProfile
*   Sample code
*   Databases
*   Join technologies together
*   Layers, layers, and more layers

*   Session service
