# [Objective](#toc.xhtml_s87a)

The goal of the Eclipse MicroProfile initiative is **to optimize Enterprise Java for microservices architecture**. It's based on a subset of Jakarta EE APIs, so we can build MicroProfile applications the same way we build Jakarta EE ones.

Divide and conquer — the most critical Roman strategy — works efficiently in several areas. The context to take any giant issue and break it down into small pieces to make the job easier to understand, making it scalable and agile, and allowing more people to work on it to deliver faster. The microservice was born from this principle. The microservices architecture is a variant of the service-oriented architecture style that structures an application as a collection of loosely coupled services. In a microservices architecture, services are fine-grained, and protocols are lightweight. In Java, there are several frameworks. In this chapter, let's walk through the Java/Jakarta EE side and talk about Eclipse MicroProfile.

The goal of the Eclipse MicroProfile initiative is to optimize Enterprise Java for the microservices architecture. It's based on a subset of Jakarta EE APIs, so we can build MicroProfile applications the same way we build Jakarta EE ones.

MicroProfiles define standard APIs for building microservices and deliver portable applications across multiple MicroProfile runtimes.

# [Skeleton of Eclipse MicroProfile](#toc.xhtml_s88a)

Microservices are software architecture, where the systems are a collection of several independent services. Each service has a specific business focus and communicates with the others with a language-agnostic protocol, such as REST. The Eclipse MicroProfile initiative optimizes Enterprise Java for the microservices architecture. It's based on a subset of Jakarta EE APIs. The first MicroProfile release was based on three existing Jakarta EE technologies: JAX-RS, CDI, and JSON-P.

The new technologies have been creating based on that to allow easy integration between Eclipse MicroProfile and Jakarta EE technology, mainly, thanks to CDI whose works as a glue of all specifications. The new technologies from Eclipse MicroProfile have a process to release a version as Jakarta EE; thus, both have are based on a specification to avoid vendor-locking and to each release, a component must have an API and a TCK.

In addition to the three basic specifications mentioned earlier, there are the following specifications created in Eclipse MicroProfile within your process:

![](images/5-1.jpg)

**Figure 5.1**

The Eclipse MicroProfile has several components, as shown below:

*   The **MicroProfile OpenTracing** specification defines behaviors and an API for accessing an OpenTracing compliant Tracer object within your JAX-RS application. The behaviors specify how incoming and outgoing requests will have OpenTracing Spans automatically created. The API defines how to explicitly disable or enable tracing for given endpoints.
*   **Eclipse MicroProfile OpenAPI:** This MicroProfile specification aims at providing a unified Java API for the OpenAPI v3 specification, that all application developers can use to expose their API documentation.
*   The **MicroProfile Rest Client** provides a type-safe approach to invoke RESTful services over HTTP. As much as possible the MP Rest Client attempts to use JAX-RS 2.0 APIs for consistency and easier re-use.
*   **Eclipse MicroProfile Config:** Eclipse MicroProfile Config is a solution to externalize configuration from the application.
*   **Eclipse MicroProfile Fault Tolerance** provides a simple and flexible solution to build a Fault Tolerance microservice, which is easy to use and configurable.
*   **Eclipse Microprofile Metrics:** This specification aims at providing a unified way for Microprofile servers to export Monitoring data ("Telemetry") to management agents and also a unified Java API that all (application) programmers can use to expose their telemetry data.
*   The **MP-JWT** feature defines the interoperability and container integration requirements for **JSON Web Token (JWT)** based bearer token authentication and authorization for use with Java EE style RBAC permissions. It defines `java.security.Principal` based extensions for representing the JWT and associated claims as well as the CDI injection annotation types and requirements, as well as the JAX-RS container security API integration.
*   **MicroProfile Health:** Health checks are used to probe the state of a computing node from another machine (i.e. Kubernetes service controller) with the primary target being cloud infrastructure environments where automated processes maintain the state of computing nodes.

# [Sample code](#toc.xhtml_s89a)

We'll create a microservices architecture based on the context of handle IT conferences. To manage the conferences, we'll have three microservices:

*   **Speaker:** The speaker service that handles the name, bio, and social media links for each speaker.
*   **Conference:** The conference service that works with lectures, which handles the name, city, and year of the conference trip.
*   **Session:** The session service that handles the name, title, description, and both conference and speaker information.

# [Databases](#toc.xhtml_s90a)

Each database has its proposal, structure, and particular behavior, so we'll use different databases according to each service. Indeed, that's the advantage of microservices; each service can use any technology that conforms to its requirements without impacting the other services. Each microservice uses an appropriate database for its particular use case:

| ![](images/5-1a.jpg) | PostgreSQL (also known as Postgres). Free and open-source relational database management system that emphasizes extensibility and technical standards compliance. It's designed to handle a range of workloads, from single machines to data warehouses or web services with many concurrent users. |
| ![](images/5-1b.jpg) | MongoDB. Cross-platform document-oriented database program. Classified as a NoSQL database program, MongoDB uses JSON-like documents with the schema. |
| ![](images/5-1c.jpg) | Elasticsearch. A search engine based on the Lucene library. Elasticsearch provides a distributed, multitenant-capable full-text search engine with an HTTP web interface and schema-free JSON documents. Elasticsearch is developed in Java. |

**Table 5.1**

Eclipse MicroProfile implementations

The relatively new MicroProfile community dedicates itself to optimizing the Enterprise Java mission for microservice-based architectures. Their goal? A microservices application platform that's portable across multiple runtimes. Currently, IBM, Red Hat, Tomitribe, Payara, the London Java Community (LJC), and SouJava lead the pack in this group.

In this sample we will use those components:

| ![](images/5-1d.jpg) | Thorntail offers an innovative approach to packaging and running Java EE applications by packaging them with just enough of the server runtime to `java -jar` your application. |
| ![](images/5-1e.jpg) | Less than 70MB in size, Payara Micro—the open-source, lightweight middleware platform of choice for containerized Java EE (Jakarta EE)—microservices deployments requires no installation or configuration and no need for code rewrites. |
| ![](images/5-1f.jpg) | KumuluzEE develops microservices with Java EE /Jakarta EE technologies and extends them with Node.js, Go, and other languages. It migrates existing Java EE applications to microservices and cloud-native architecture. |
| ![](images/5-1g.jpg) | Apache TomEE is the Eclipse MicroProfile flavor that uses several Apache Project flavors, including Apache Tomcat and Apache OpenWebBeans. |

**Table 5.2**

# [Join technologies together](#toc.xhtml_s91a)

![](images/5-2.jpg)

**Figure 5.2**

Our project has four components:

*   **KumuluzEE** will handle the Session microservice. Users will want to search for session details, such as finding sessions that talk about either Eclipse Microprofile or Jakarta EE. To work with this requirement, the project will use a NoSQL that's also a powerful search engine (yes, we're talking about Elasticsearch).
*   **Thorntail** will handle the Speaker microservice, the most uncomplicated service that will manage the speaker information. This service will work with PostgreSQL.
*   **Payara Micro** will handle the Conference microservice. This service is strongly related to KumuluzEE and Thorntail. To create a conference, we need both sessions and speakers. MongoDB seems to fit easily with this service because we can use the concept of subdocuments to put a list of presentations and speakers into the Conference entity, instead of making the relationship as in a relational database.
*   **Apache TomEE** is the client browser using HTML 5 as frontend.

The significant benefits of the Eclipse MicroProfile? Well, first, it uses standards on Jakarta EE, such as CDI, JAX-RS, and JSON-P.

To connect to PostgreSQL or any relational database, there's a JPA specification that describes the management of relational data in applications using the Java platform. To communicate to the NoSQL databases, we'll use the Jakarta NoSQL specification (not a final version yet, but hopefully will come out soon).

On the client-side, we'll use Eclipse Krazo with an HTML 5 extension, using Thymeleaf as the template engine. At the moment, the client doesn't have a database; it will only communicate with the services to organize the values, then show them in the browser. Therefore, it will use Eclipse MicroProfile Rest Client, which provides a type-safe approach to invoke RESTful services over HTTP.

# [Layers, layers, and more layers](#toc.xhtml_s92a)

The number of layers in an application is always a colossal discussion when we talk about microservices. Layers are essential to split responsibility and write clean code, while maintaining the several patterns built following this approach, like the MVC.

However, a vast number of layers are also hard to keep, and to maintain; we need to pay attention to the delay layers. Otherwise, instead of an MVC, we're creating the onion pattern, where you cry each time you go through a layer. There is no magic formula to determine the number of layers you'll need. Rather, it will be based on the complexity of the business rules, and how the infrastructure code can give us the cleanest architecture.

In our sample, we have three layers:

*   **The entity:** The first instance of the business and where the domain lives. These entities, for best practice, should follow a rich model. We can make these entities agnostic to a database, but this time, let's keep it simple and use the entity highly coupled with the database.
*   **The data transfer object (DTO):** Just a dump class to allow transfer values between the controller and the entity. It's most prominent goal is to prevent that entity from becoming an anemic model, because some frameworks might require getter and setter methods to serialization, which usually means weak encapsulation.
*   **The controller:** The bridge between the view and the model. It only manipulates the flow among them. With small exceptions, it must keep clean as possible–you remember the high cohesion rule, right?

    ![](images/5-3.jpg)

    **Figure 5.3**

# [Speaker service](#toc.xhtml_s93a)

The first service that we'll cover is the Speaker service, which uses Thorntail and PostgreSQL. The Eclipse MicroProfile doesn't have support for a relational database. However, we can use the specification from its older and more mature brother, Jakarta EE. As a first step, let's define the Speaker entity:

`‘‘‘java`

`import javax.persistence.Column;`

`import javax.persistence.Entity;`

`import javax.persistence.GeneratedValue;`

`import javax.persistence.Id;`

`import javax.persistence.Table;`

`import java.util.Objects;`

`@Entity`

`@Table(name = "speaker")`

`public class Speaker {`

`@Id`

`@GeneratedValue`

`private Integer id;`

`@Column`

`private String name;`

`@Column`

`private String bio;`

`@Column`

`private String twitter;`

`@Column`

`private String github;`

`//more code`

`}`

`’’’`

The CDI has impressive features, including the scope. Therefore, we can define an `EntityManager` to be used in the whole application, and when the application shuts down, it will close the containers one-by-one:

`‘‘‘`

`import javax.interceptor.InterceptorBinding;`

`import java.lang.annotation.Inherited;`

`import java.lang.annotation.Retention;`

`import java.lang.annotation.Target;`

`import static java.lang.annotation.ElementType.METHOD;`

`import static java.lang.annotation.ElementType.TYPE;`

`import static java.lang.annotation.RetentionPolicy.RUNTIME;`

`@Inherited`

`@InterceptorBinding`

`@Retention(RUNTIME)`

`@Target({METHOD, TYPE})`

`public @interface Transactional {`

`}`

`import sh.platform.config.Config;`

`import sh.platform.config.JPA;`

`import javax.annotation.PostConstruct;`

`import javax.enterprise.context.ApplicationScoped;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.Disposes;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.Produces;`

`import javax.persistence.EntityManager;`

`import javax.persistence.EntityManagerFactory;`

`@ApplicationScoped`

`class EntityManagerConfiguration {`

`private EntityManagerFactory entityManagerFactory;`

`private EntityManager entityManager;`

`@PostConstruct`

`void setUp() {`

`Config config = new Config();`

`final JPA credential = config.getCredential("postgresql", JPA::new);`

`entityManagerFactory = credential.getPostgreSQL("jpa-example");`

`entityManager = entityManagerFactory.createEntityManager();`

`}`

`@Produces`

`@ApplicationScoped`

`EntityManagerFactory getEntityManagerFactory() {`

`return entityManagerFactory;`

`}`

`@Produces`

`@ApplicationScoped`

`EntityManager getEntityManager() {`

`return entityManager;`

`}`

`void close(@Disposes EntityManagerFactory entityManagerFactory) {`

`entityManagerFactory.close();`

`}`

`void close(@Disposes EntityManager entityManager) {`

`entityManager.close();`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

The `SpeakerService` class will integrate with the `EntityManager` enable in the CDI container, this class will operate in a relational database thanks to JPA and Jakarta EE specification:

`‘‘‘`

`import javax.enterprise.context.ApplicationScoped;`

`import javax.inject.Inject;`

`import javax.persistence.EntityManager;`

`import javax.transaction.Transactional;`

`import java.util.List;`

`import java.util.Optional;`

`@ApplicationScoped`

`public class SpeakerService {`

`@Inject`

`private EntityManager entityManager;`

`@Transactional`

`public Speaker insert(Speaker speaker) {`

`entityManager.persist(speaker);`

`return speaker;`

`}`

`@Transactional`

`public void update(Speaker speaker) {`

`entityManager.persist(speaker);`

`}`

`@Transactional`

`public void delete(Integer id) {`

`find(id).ifPresent(c -> entityManager.remove(c));`

`}`

`public Optional<Speaker> find(Integer id) {`

`return Optional.ofNullable(entityManager.find(Speaker.class, id));`

`}`

`public List<Speaker> findAll() {`

`String query = "select e from Speaker e";`

`return entityManager.createQuery(query).getResultList();`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

We have a resource to expose the services through an HTTP request. We've created a layer on DTO to avoid losing the encapsulation:

`‘‘‘java`

`import javax.enterprise.context.RequestScoped;`

`import javax.inject.Inject;`

`import javax.validation.Valid;`

`import javax.ws.rs.Consumes;`

`import javax.ws.rs.DELETE;`

`import javax.ws.rs.GET;`

`import javax.ws.rs.POST;`

`import javax.ws.rs.PUT;`

`import javax.ws.rs.Path;`

`import javax.ws.rs.PathParam;`

`import javax.ws.rs.Produces;`

`import javax.ws.rs.WebApplicationException;`

`import javax.ws.rs.core.MediaType;`

`import javax.ws.rs.core.Response;`

`import java.util.List;`

`import java.util.Optional;`

`import java.util.stream.Collectors;`

`import static javax.ws.rs.core.Response.Status.NO_CONTENT;`

`import static javax.ws.rs.core.Response.status;`

`@Path("speakers")`

`@RequestScoped`

`@Produces(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON + "; charset=UTF-8")`

`@Consumes(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON+ "; charset=UTF-8")`

`public class SpeakerResource {`

`@Inject`

`private SpeakerService speakerService;`

`@GET`

`public List<SpeakerDTO> findAll() {`

`return speakerService.findAll().stream().map(SpeakerDTO::of).collect(Collectors.toList());`

`}`

`@GET`

`@Path("{id}")`

`public SpeakerDTO findById(@PathParam("id") Integer id) {`

`final Optional<Speaker> conference = speakerService.find(id);`

`return conference.map(SpeakerDTO::of).orElseThrow(this::notFound);`

`}`

`@PUT`

`@Path("{id}")`

`public SpeakerDTO update(@PathParam("id") Integer id, @Valid SpeakerDTO speakerUpdated) {`

`final Optional<Speaker> optional = speakerService.find(id);`

`final Speaker speaker = optional.orElseThrow(() -> notFound());`

`speaker.update(Speaker.of(speakerUpdated));`

`speakerService.update(speaker);`

`return SpeakerDTO.of(speaker);`

`}`

`@DELETE`

`@Path("{id}")`

`public Response remove(@PathParam("id") Integer id) {`

`speakerService.delete(id);`

`return status(NO_CONTENT).build();`

`}`

`@POST`

`public SpeakerDTO insert(@Valid SpeakerDTO speaker) {`

`return SpeakerDTO.of(speakerService.insert(Speaker.of(speaker)));`

`}`

`private WebApplicationException notFound() {`

`return new WebApplicationException(Response.Status.NOT_FOUND);`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

# [Session service](#toc.xhtml_s94a)

The Session service will handle the contents of the conference so that a user can find sessions related to topics like cloud and Java. This service uses Elasticsearch with Jakarta NoSQL and KumuluzEE.

The first step is to make available the Elasticsearch client to CDI, as we mentioned in the Jakarta NoSQL chapter. The reference implementation of Jakarta NoSQL has tight integration with Eclipse MicroProfile Config.

The `config` properties are provided by `ConfigSources. ConfigSources` are ordered according to their ordinal, with a higher value taking priority over a lower one. For example, in Websphere Liberty, this means that if the same `config` is set in `server.env (ordinal=300)` and `microprofile-config.properties (ordinal=100)`, then the value from `server.env` will be used. By default, there are 3 default `ConfigSources`:

*   `System.getProperties() (ordinal=400)`
*   `System.getenv() (ordinal=300)`
*   `All META-INF/microprofile-config.properties` files on the `ClassPath.` (default ordinal=100, separately configurable via a `config_ordinal` property inside each file)

The default values can be specified in the file `microprofile-config.properties` within the application and the value can be overwritten later for each deployment. A higher ordinal number takes precedence over a lower number:

`‘‘‘java`

`import jakarta.nosql.document.DocumentCollectionManager;`

`import org.eclipse.microprofile.config.inject.ConfigProperty;`

`import javax.enterprise.context.ApplicationScoped;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.Disposes;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.Produces;`

`import javax.inject.Inject;`

`@ApplicationScoped`

`public class ManagerConfig {`

`@Inject`

`@ConfigProperty(name = "document")`

`private DocumentCollectionManager manager;`

`@Produces`

`public DocumentCollectionManager getManager() {`

`return manager;`

`}`

`public void destroy(@Disposes DocumentCollectionManager manager) {`

`manager.close();`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

We can set the configuration file as the value to run locally and overrides when we move it to either container or a cloud provider where the database information might be different without touch in the code:

`‘‘‘`

`document=document`

`document.database=conference`

`document.settings.jakarta.nosql.host=localhost:9200`

`document.provider=org.eclipse.jnosql.diana.elasticsearch.document.ElasticsearchDocumentConfiguration`

`’’’`

To mapping the Session entity, we have a terminology pretty similar to JPA, thus `@Entity, @Id, and @Column` to define the class as an entity, the entity id and the fields that will be stored in the database:

`‘‘‘java`

`import jakarta.nosql.mapping.Column;`

`import jakarta.nosql.mapping.Entity;`

`import jakarta.nosql.mapping.Id;`

`import java.util.Objects;`

`@Entity`

`public class Session {`

`@Id`

`private String id;`

`@Column`

`private String name;`

`@Column`

`private String title;`

`@Column`

`private String description;`

`@Column`

`private String conference;`

`@Column`

`private Integer speaker;`

`}`

`’’’`

We're applying a distinct aspect of Elasticsearch to use it as a search engine. Jakarta NoSQL looks to the specialization that allows particular features for each NoSQL database. Therefore, we'll have a mix of repository and `ElasticsearchTemplate`, a specialty of DocumentTemplate:

`‘‘‘java`

`import jakarta.nosql.mapping.Repository;`

`import java.util.List;`

`public interface SessionRepository extends Repository<Session, String> {`

`List<Session> findAll();`

`}`

`import org.elasticsearch.index.query.QueryBuilder;`

`import org.jnosql.artemis.elasticsearch.document.ElasticsearchTemplate;`

`import org.jnosql.artemis.util.StringUtils;`

`import javax.enterprise.context.RequestScoped;`

`import javax.inject.Inject;`

`import javax.validation.Valid;`

`import javax.ws.rs.Consumes;`

`import javax.ws.rs.DELETE;`

`import javax.ws.rs.GET;`

`import javax.ws.rs.POST;`

`import javax.ws.rs.PUT;`

`import javax.ws.rs.Path;`

`import javax.ws.rs.PathParam;`

`import javax.ws.rs.Produces;`

`import javax.ws.rs.QueryParam;`

`import javax.ws.rs.WebApplicationException;`

`import javax.ws.rs.core.MediaType;`

`import javax.ws.rs.core.Response;`

`import java.util.Collections;`

`import java.util.List;`

`import java.util.Optional;`

`import java.util.UUID;`

`import java.util.logging.Logger;`

`import java.util.stream.Collectors;`

`import static javax.ws.rs.core.Response.Status.NO_CONTENT;`

`import static javax.ws.rs.core.Response.status;`

`import static org.elasticsearch.index.query.QueryBuilders.boolQuery;`

`import static org.elasticsearch.index.query.QueryBuilders.termQuery;`

`@Path("sessions")`

`@RequestScoped`

`@Produces(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON + "; charset=UTF-8")`

`@Consumes(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON + "; charset=UTF-8")`

`public class SessionResource {`

`private static Logger LOGGER = Logger.getLogger(SessionResource.class.getName());`

`@Inject`

`private SessionRepository speakerRepository;`

`@Inject`

`private ElasticsearchTemplate template;`

`@GET`

`public List<SessionDTO> findAll(@QueryParam("search") String search) {`

`LOGGER.info("searching with the field: " + search);`

`if (StringUtils.isNotBlank(search)) {`

`QueryBuilder queryBuilder = boolQuery()`

`.should(termQuery("name", search))`

`.should(termQuery("title", search))`

`.should(termQuery("description", search));`

`LOGGER.info("the query: " + queryBuilder);`

`List<Session> sessions = template.search(queryBuilder, "Session");`

`LOGGER.info("the result: " + sessions);`

`return sessions.stream().map(SessionDTO::of).collect(Collectors.toList());`

`}`

`return speakerRepository.findAll().stream().map(SessionDTO::of).collect(Collectors.toList());`

`}`

`@GET`

`@Path("{id}")`

`public Session findById(@PathParam("id") String id) {`

`final Optional<Session> conference = speakerRepository.findById(id);`

`return conference.orElseThrow(this::notFound);`

`}`

`@PUT`

`@Path("{id}")`

`public SessionDTO update(@PathParam("id") String id, @Valid SessionDTO sessionUpdated) {`

`final Optional<Session> optional = speakerRepository.findById(id);`

`final Session session = optional.orElseThrow(() -> notFound());`

`session.update(Session.of(sessionUpdated));`

`speakerRepository.save(session);`

`return SessionDTO.of(session);`

`}`

`@DELETE`

`@Path("{id}")`

`public Response remove(@PathParam("id") String id) {`

`speakerRepository.deleteById(id);`

`return status(NO_CONTENT).build();`

`}`

`@POST`

`public SessionDTO insert(@Valid SessionDTO session) {`

`session.setId(UUID.randomUUID().toString());`

`return SessionDTO.of(speakerRepository.save(Session.of(session)));`

`}`

`private WebApplicationException notFound() {`

`return new WebApplicationException(Response.Status.NOT_FOUND);`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

# [Conference service](#toc.xhtml_s95a)

The Conference service is the most connected of the services because it needs to keep information from both speaker and session services. It uses Payara Micro and MongoDB with Jakarta NoSQL.

As the session service, the first step is to make available a MongoDB connection through CDI. As we did earlier, we are going to use the integration with Eclipse MicroProfile Config:

`‘‘‘java`

`import jakarta.nosql.document.DocumentCollectionManager;`

`import org.eclipse.microprofile.config.inject.ConfigProperty;`

`import javax.enterprise.context.ApplicationScoped;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.Disposes;`

`import javax.enterprise.inject.Produces;`

`import javax.inject.Inject;`

`@ApplicationScoped`

`public class MongoConfig {`

`@Inject`

`@ConfigProperty(name = "document")`

`private DocumentCollectionManager manager;`

`@Produces`

`public DocumentCollectionManager getManager() {`

`return manager;`

`}`

`public void destroy(@Disposes DocumentCollectionManager manager) {`

`manager.close();`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

Let's create a configuration with a local environment activated:

`‘‘‘`

`document=document`

`document.database=conferences`

`document.settings.jakarta.nosql.host=localhost:27017`

`document.provider=org.eclipse.jnosql.diana.mongodb.document.MongoDBDocumentConfiguration`

`‘‘‘`

`‘‘‘java`

`import jakarta.nosql.mapping.Column;`

`import jakarta.nosql.mapping.Convert;`

`import jakarta.nosql.mapping.Entity;`

`import jakarta.nosql.mapping.Id;`

`import java.time.Year;`

`import java.util.Collections;`

`import java.util.List;`

`import java.util.Objects;`

`import java.util.Optional;`

`import java.util.stream.Collectors;`

`@Entity`

`public class Conference {`

`@Id`

`@Convert(ObjectIdConverter.class)`

`private String id;`

`@Column`

`private String name;`

`@Column`

`private String city;`

`@Column`

`private String link;`

`@Column`

`@Convert(YearConverter.class)`

`private Year year;`

`@Column`

`private List<Speaker> speakers;`

`@Column`

`private List<Session> sessions;`

`}`

`@Entity`

`public class Session {`

`@Column`

`private String id;`

`@Column`

`private String name;`

`}`

`@Entity`

`public class Speaker {`

`@Column`

`private Integer id;`

`@Column`

`private String name;`

`}`

`’’’`

One of the benefits of MongoDB is that we can use the subdocument instead of creating a relationship. Therefore, we can embed `Speaker` and `Session` instead of doing joins. Note, the `Speaker` and `Session` entities have brief information such as name and ID:

`‘‘‘java`

`import jakarta.nosql.mapping.Repository;`

`import java.util.List;`

`public interface ConferenceRepository extends Repository<Conference, String> {`

`List<Conference> findAll();`

`}`

`import javax.enterprise.context.RequestScoped;`

`import javax.inject.Inject;`

`import javax.validation.Valid;`

`import javax.ws.rs.Consumes;`

`import javax.ws.rs.DELETE;`

`import javax.ws.rs.GET;`

`import javax.ws.rs.POST;`

`import javax.ws.rs.PUT;`

`import javax.ws.rs.Path;`

`import javax.ws.rs.PathParam;`

`import javax.ws.rs.Produces;`

`import javax.ws.rs.WebApplicationException;`

`import javax.ws.rs.core.MediaType;`

`import javax.ws.rs.core.Response;`

`import java.util.List;`

`import java.util.Optional;`

`import java.util.stream.Collectors;`

`import static javax.ws.rs.core.Response.Status.NO_CONTENT;`

`import static javax.ws.rs.core.Response.status;`

`@Path("conferences")`

`@RequestScoped`

`@Produces(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON + "; charset=UTF-8")`

`@Consumes(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON+ "; charset=UTF-8")`

`public class ConferenceResource {`

`@Inject`

`private ConferenceRepository conferenceRepository;`

`@GET`

`public List<ConferenceDTO> findAll() {`

`return conferenceRepository.findAll().stream().map(ConferenceDTO::of).collect(Collectors.toList());`

`}`

`@GET`

`@Path("{id}")`

`public ConferenceDTO findById(@PathParam("id") String id) {`

`final Optional<Conference> conference = conferenceRepository.findById(id);`

`return conference.map(ConferenceDTO::of).orElseThrow(this::notFound);`

`}`

`@PUT`

`@Path("{id}")`

`public ConferenceDTO update(@PathParam("id") String id, @Valid ConferenceDTO conferenceUpdated) {`

`final Optional<Conference> optional = conferenceRepository.findById(id);`

`final Conference conference = optional.orElseThrow(() -> notFound());`

`conference.update(Conference.of(conferenceUpdated));`

`conferenceRepository.save(conference);`

`return ConferenceDTO.of(conference);`

`}`

`@DELETE`

`@Path("{id}")`

`public Response remove(@PathParam("id") String id) {`

`conferenceRepository.deleteById(id);`

`return status(NO_CONTENT).build();`

`}`

`@POST`

`public ConferenceDTO insert(@Valid ConferenceDTO conference) {`

`return ConferenceDTO.of(conferenceRepository.save(Conference.of(conference)));`

`}`

`private WebApplicationException notFound() {`

`return new WebApplicationException(Response.Status.NOT_FOUND);`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

The first step in the client is to create the bridge to request information from the services. Thankfully, we have an Eclipse MicroProfile Rest Client to deal just with interfaces and nothing more:

`‘‘‘java`

`import org.eclipse.microprofile.rest.client.inject.RegisterRestClient;`

`import javax.ws.rs.Consumes;`

`import javax.ws.rs.DELETE;`

`import javax.ws.rs.GET;`

`import javax.ws.rs.POST;`

`import javax.ws.rs.PUT;`

`import javax.ws.rs.Path;`

`import javax.ws.rs.PathParam;`

`import javax.ws.rs.Produces;`

`import javax.ws.rs.core.MediaType;`

`import javax.ws.rs.core.Response;`

`import java.util.List;`

`@Path("speakers")`

`@RegisterRestClient`

`@Produces(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON + "; charset=UTF-8")`

`@Consumes(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON+ "; charset=UTF-8")`

`public interface SpeakerService {`

`@GET`

`List<Speaker> findAll();`

`@GET`

`@Path("{id}")`

`Speaker findById(@PathParam("id") Integer id);`

`@PUT`

`@Path("{id}")`

`Speaker update(@PathParam("id") Integer id, Speaker speaker);`

`@DELETE`

`@Path("{id}")`

`Response remove(@PathParam("id") Integer id);`

`@POST`

`Speaker insert(Speaker speaker);`

`}`

`’’’`

To make sure that the services are up, we conduct an Eclipse Microprofile Health check, so we can evaluate the HTTP status and the response time in milliseconds:

`‘‘‘`

`import org.eclipse.microprofile.health.HealthCheck;`

`import org.eclipse.microprofile.health.HealthCheckResponse;`

`import javax.ws.rs.client.Client;`

`import javax.ws.rs.core.MediaType;`

`import javax.ws.rs.core.Response;`

`abstract class AbstractHealthCheck implements HealthCheck {`

`abstract Client getClient();`

`abstract String getUrl();`

`abstract String getServiceName();`

`@Override`

`public HealthCheckResponse call() {`

`try {`

`long start = System.currentTimeMillis();`

`Response response = getClient().target(getUrl()).request(MediaType.TEXT_PLAIN_TYPE).get();`

`long end = System.currentTimeMillis() - start;`

`return HealthCheckResponse.named(getServiceName())`

`.withData("service", "available")`

`.withData("time millis", end)`

`.withData("status", response.getStatus())`

`.withData("status", response.getStatusInfo().toEnum().toString())`

`.up()`

`.build();`

`} catch (Exception exp) {`

`return HealthCheckResponse.named(getServiceName())`

`.withData("services", "not available")`

`.down()`

`.build();`

`}`

`}`

`}`

`@Health`

`@ApplicationScoped`

`public class ConferenceHealthCheck extends AbstractHealthCheck {`

`@Inject`

`@ConfigProperty(name = "org.jespanol.client.conference.ConferenceService/mp-rest/url")`

`private String url;`

`private Client client;`

`@PostConstruct`

`public void init() {`

`this.client = ClientBuilder.newClient();`

`}`

`@Override`

`Client getClient() {`

`return client;`

`}`

`@Override`

`String getUrl() {`

`return url;`

`}`

`@Override`

`String getServiceName() {`

`return "Conference Service";`

`}`

`}`

`@Health`

`@ApplicationScoped`

`public class SessionHealthCheck extends AbstractHealthCheck {`

`@Inject`

`@ConfigProperty(name = "org.jespanol.client.session.SessionService/mp-rest/url")`

`private String url;`

`private Client client;`

`@PostConstruct`

`public void init() {`

`this.client = ClientBuilder.newClient();`

`}`

`@Override`

`Client getClient() {`

`return client;`

`}`

`@Override`

`String getUrl() {`

`return url;`

`}`

`@Override`

`String getServiceName() {`

`return "Session Service";`

`}`

`}`

`@Health`

`@ApplicationScoped`

`public class SpeakerHealthCheck extends AbstractHealthCheck {`

`@Inject`

`@ConfigProperty(name = "org.jespanol.client.speaker.SpeakerService/mp-rest/url")`

`private String url;`

`private Client client;`

`@PostConstruct`

`public void init() {`

`this.client = ClientBuilder.newClient();`

`}`

`@Override`

`Client getClient() {`

`return client;`

`}`

`@Override`

`String getUrl() {`

`return url;`

`}`

`@Override`

`String getServiceName() {`

`return "Speaker Service";`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

`"time millis":11,`

`"service":"available",`

`"status":"OK"`

`},`

`"name":"Speaker Service",`

`"state":"UP"`

`},`

`{`

`"data":{`

`"service":"available",`

`"status":"OK"`

`},`

`"name":"Conference Service",`

`"state":"UP"`

`},`

`{`

`"data":{`

`"time millis":10,`

`"service":"available",`

`"status":"OK"`

`},`

`"name":"Session Service",`

`"state":"UP"`

`}`

`],`

`"outcome":"UP",`

`"status":"UP"`

`}`

Once Services and health checks are ready, let's go to the controllers. Eclipse Krazo is an API built on JAX-RS. Therefore, any Jakarta EE developer will feel at home when creating a `Controller` class:

`‘‘‘java`

`import org.eclipse.microprofile.rest.client.inject.RestClient;`

`import org.jespanol.client.session.SessionService;`

`import org.jespanol.client.speaker.SpeakerService;`

`import javax.inject.Inject;`

`import javax.mvc.Controller;`

`import javax.mvc.Models;`

`import javax.mvc.View;`

`import javax.ws.rs.BeanParam;`

`import javax.ws.rs.GET;`

`import javax.ws.rs.POST;`

`import javax.ws.rs.Path;`

`import javax.ws.rs.PathParam;`

`import java.util.Optional;`

`@Controller`

`@Path("conference")`

`public class ConferenceController {`

`@Inject`

`private Models models;`

`@Inject`

`@RestClient`

`private SessionService sessionService;`

`@Inject`

`@RestClient`

`private ConferenceService conferenceService;`

`@Inject`

`@RestClient`

`private SpeakerService speakerService;`

`@GET`

`@View("conference.html")`

`public void home() {`

`this.models.put("conferences", conferenceService.findAll());`

`}`

`@Path("add")`

`@GET`

`@View("conference-add.html")`

`public void add() {`

`this.models.put("conference", new Conference());`

`this.models.put("speakers", speakerService.findAll());`

`this.models.put("presentations", sessionService.findAll());`

`}`

`@Path("delete/{id}")`

`@GET`

`@View("conference.html")`

`public void delete(@PathParam("id") String id) {`

`conferenceService.remove(id);`

`this.models.put("conferences", conferenceService.findAll());`

`}`

`@Path("edit/{id}")`

`@GET`

`@View("conference-add.html")`

`public void edit(@PathParam("id") String id) {`

`final Conference conference = Optional.ofNullable(conferenceService.findById(id))`

`.orElse(new Conference());`

`this.models.put("conference", conference);`

`this.models.put("speakers", speakerService.findAll());`

`this.models.put("presentations", sessionService.findAll());`

`}`

`@Path("add")`

`@POST`

`@View("conference.html")`

`public void add(@BeanParam Conference conference) {`

`conference.update(speakerService, sessionService);`

`if (conference.isIdEmpty()) {`

`conferenceService.insert(conference);`

`} else {`

`conferenceService.update(conference.getId(), conference);`

`}`

`this.models.put("conferences", conferenceService.findAll());`

`}`

`}`

`’’’`

The HTML5 resource and replacement are on Thymeleaf:

`‘‘‘html`

`<!DOCTYPE html>`

`<html xmlns:th="http://www.thymeleaf.org">`

`<html>`

`<head>`

`<title>Latin America Conf (Session)</title>`

`<meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">`

`<link rel="stylesheet" href="/css/bootstrap.min.css">`

`<meta charset="UTF-8">`

`</head>`

`<body>`

`<div class="container">`

`<h1>Conference</h1>`

`<form th:action="@{/conference/add}" method="post" accept-charset="UTF-8">`

`<input id="id" name="id" type="hidden" th:value="${conference.id}">`

`<div class="form-group">`

`<label for="conferenceName">Name</label>`

`<input type="text" class="form-control" th:value="${conference.name}" name="name" id="conferenceName" placeholder="Enter Session Name" required>`

`</div>`

`<div class="form-group">`

`<label for="conferenceCity">City</label>`

`<input type="text" class="form-control" th:value="${conference.city}" name="city" id="conferenceCity" placeholder="Enter Conference City" required>`

`</div>`

`<div class="form-group">`

`<label for="conferenceLink">Link</label>`

`<input type="url" class="form-control" th:value="${conference.link}" name="link" id="conferenceLink" placeholder="Enter Conference Link" required>`

`</div>`

`<div class="form-group">`

`<label for="conferenceYear">Year</label>`

`<input type="number" class="form-control" th:value="${conference.year}" name="year" id="conferenceYear" placeholder="Enter Conference Year" required>`

`</div>`

`<div class="form-group">`

`<label for="conferenceSpeakers">Speakers</label>`

`<select class="form-control" id="conferenceSpeakers" th:value="${conference.speakersIds}" name="speakers" multiple>`

`<tr th:each="speaker : ${speakers}">`

`<option th:value="${speaker.id}" th:text="${speaker.name}" th:selected="${conference.speakersIds.contains(speaker.id)}"></option>`

`</tr>`

`</select>`

`</div>`

`<div class="form-group">`

`<label for="conferenceSpeakers">Sessions</label>`

`<select class="form-control" id="conferenceSessions" th:value="${conference.sessionsIds}" name="presentations" multiple>`

`<tr th:each="presentation : ${presentations}">`

`<option th:value="${presentation.id}" th:text="${presentation.name}" th:selected="${conference.sessionsIds.contains(presentation.id)}"></option>`

`</tr>`

`</select>`

`</div>`

`<button type="submit" class="btn">Save</button>`

`</form>`

`</div>`

`<script src="https://code.jquery.com/jquery.js"></script>`

`<script src="/js/bootstrap.min.js"></script>`

`</body>`

`</html>`

`’’’`

# [Conclusion](#toc.xhtml_s97a)

With Eclipse MicroProfile, it's possible to make modern applications ready for microservices architectures. It has many features that ease the big problems that can occur within this type of structure. An important point is their integration with Jakarta EE technologies, i.e., it is possible to use as specifications as JPA or Jakarta NoSQL quickly and very quietly. There are still several features that may come in the not too distant future, for example, integration with GraphQL and also reactive programming API. The excellent point is that all of these features are open source and transparently realized for a community where you, dear reader, can make your contribution.

# [Questions](#toc.xhtml_s98a)

1.  What is Eclipse MicroProfile?
2.  What is DTO?

This chapter will introduce the Java Server Faces specification and the use of some frameworks such as Primefaces that facilitate the use of components in Java Enterprise Edition/Jakarta EE applications.

# [Objective](#toc.xhtml_s101a)

This chapter is an introduction to Java Server Faces, which is one of the most widely used Java Web frameworks today. The goal is for you to develop Web applications with great ease using this specification.

# [Introduction to Java Server Faces](#toc.xhtml_s102a)

The development of web applications in Java has its beginnings with Servlets. These APIs are at the heart of many of the Java web technologies. They offer an easy way to interact with the HTTP protocol from our applications. Over the years Java Server Pages were created that allowed HTML code to be mixed with Java code making it easier to create user interfaces. Then was born the Java Server Faces specification that offers us a component-oriented MVC framework.

Some of the features offered by the JSF are:

*   A large number of components
*   Integration with Specification APIs
*   Support for HTML5
*   Converters
*   Events and Listeners
*   Validators
*   Facelets
*   Support for Ajax

Until a few years ago Mojarra was the reference specification. Now that it is part of Jakarta EE it has been renamed Jakarta Server Faces. The official site of the new specification is **[https://jakarta.ee/specifications/faces/2.3/](https://jakarta.ee/specifications/faces/2.3/)**, which will be described in the following section.

# [Java Server Faces 2.3](#toc.xhtml_s103a)

The latest version of Java Server Faces 2.3 (**[https://javaserverfaces.github.io](https://javaserverfaces.github.io)**) offers several features among that are mentioned on the site:

*   CDI alignment
    *   Injection and EL resolving of JSF artifacts
    *   Injection in more JSF artifacts
*   WebSocket integration
    *   Ajax method invocation
*   Conversion / Validation
    *   Class level bean validation
    *   JDK 8 time support in `f:convertDateTime`
*   Java API
    *   Support for the `Iterable` interface in `UIData` and `UIRepeat`
    *   Support for the Map interface in `UIData` and `UIRepeat`
*   Components
    *   Component Search Expression framework
    *   Freely placeable radio button component
*   Views and Resources
    *   Basic support for exact mapping (extension-less URLs)
    *   Obtaining a list of all view resources

# [Frameworks](#toc.xhtml_s104a)

One of the advantages offered by Java Server Faces is the creation of a framework for extending components or creating new ones. The most commonly used frameworks are:

*   Primefaces
*   Bootsfaces
*   Omnifaces

It will also show a framework integrated with jmoordb.

*   Jmoordbjsf

# [Primefaces](#toc.xhtml_s105a)

It offers more than 100 ready-to-use components. It greatly simplifies the development of user interfaces:

![](images/6-1.jpg)

**Figure 6.1**

# [Bootsfaces](#toc.xhtml_s106a)

It supports a number of components, can be mixed with those of Primefaces and above all is integrated very well with Bootstrap.

# [Omnifaces](#toc.xhtml_s107a)

It is a library of non-visual utilities that facilitate the complex tasks of Java Server Faces.

# [Jmoordbjsf](#toc.xhtml_s108a)

It is a java server face library that implements many Primefaces and Bootsfaces components adapted to be used with jmoordb.

In the following sections, two examples will be developed, to demonstrate the most common characteristics of the Java Server Faces components.

# [Demo example](#toc.xhtml_s109a)

In this example, you will create a Java EE/Jakarta EE application that uses some components to demonstrate the ease of development of web applications based on **MVC (Model View Controller).**

Follow the steps described above to create the project:

1.  Open NetBeans IDE and run **`File | New Project`**.

    Then in Categories, select **`Java With Maven`** and in **`Projects`** select **`Web Application`** as shown in the image below:

    ![](images/6-2.jpg)

    **Figure 6.2**

2.  Enter the name of the project as demo. In the **`Server`** option leave it at **`<Not Server Selected>`** and in **`Java EE Version`** select **`Java EE 8 Web`**, as shown in the following screenshot:

    ![](images/6-3.jpg)

    **Figure 6.3**

3.  In the **`Project Properties`** select the **`Java Server Faces`** framework and in **`Components`** select **`Primefaces`**. As shown in the following screenshot, also, check the Java version and other project properties:

    ![](images/6-4.jpg)

    **Figure 6.4**

4.  Edit the `pom.xml` `file` and update the primefaces version to `8.0` and add `jmoordbutils`. In the code, in the repository, you can see the whole project:

    `<dependency>`

    `<groupId>org.primefaces</groupId>`

    `<artifactId>primefaces</artifactId>`

    `<version>8.0</version>`

    `</dependency>`

    `<dependency>`

    `<groupId>org.projectlombok</groupId>`

    `<artifactId>lombok</artifactId>`

    `<version>1.18.6</version>`

    `</dependency>`

    `<dependency>`

    `<groupId>com.github.avbravo</groupId>`

    `<artifactId>jmoordbutils</artifactId>`

    `<version>0.84</version>`

    `</dependency>`

# [Model](#toc.xhtml_s110a)

The entities will define the model that will be associated with the view through the controllers. In our example, these are simple Java classes. Let's create the class `Person` with the following data:

`public class Person {`

`private String name;`

`public Person() {`

`}`

`//Setters/Gettters`

`}`

# [View](#toc.xhtml_s111a)

The view is represented by Java Server Faces components that are rendered in a Web browser as HTML pages. Primefaces has more than 100 components, for the example, you will use only these:

*   InputText
*   Label
*   CommandButton
*   Panel grid
*   Datatable
*   Calendar

Primefaces requires no configuration, just add the dependency in the project and it is ready to be used:

`<dependency>`

`<groupId>org.primefaces</groupId>`

`<artifactId>primefaces</artifactId>`

`<version>8.0</version>`

`</dependency>`

Add to the namespaced of each page to use the components by `<p:>`:

`xmlns:p="http://primefaces.org/ui"`

To draw a calendar simply add the component `<p:calendar/>`:

`<p:calendar id="inline" mode="inline" />`

This will be rendered on the client as shown in the screenshot below:

![](images/6-5.jpg)

**Figure 6.5**

Some components will be used such as a panel with a label, a text box and a button that will insert the name in a list that will be displayed in the datable. A two-column panelGrid will be used:

`<p:panelGrid columns="2">`

`<p:outputLabel value="name"/>`

`<p:inputText value="#{personController.name}"/>`

`<f:facet name="footer">`

`<p:commandButton value="Insert" action="#{personController.save()}" update=":form:growl, :form:datatable"/>`

`</f:facet>`

`</p:panelGrid>`

The `datatable` will have a button that displays a dialogue with the selected element. As you can see, it is associated with a `List<Person>` that will be defined later in the controller:

`<p:dataTable var="item" value="#{personController.personList}" id="datatable">`

`<p:column headerText="name">`

`<p:outputLabel value="#{item.name}"/>`

`</p:column>`

`<p:column headerText="">`

`<p:commandButton action= "#{personController.show}" update=":form:outputPanelPerson, :form:growl" oncomplete="PF('personDialog').show()" icon="fa fa-print" >`

`<f:setPropertyActionListener value="#{item}" target="#{personController.personSelected}" />`

`</p:commandButton>`

`</p:column>`

`</p:dataTable>`

It would generate a view as shown in the screenshot below:

![](images/6-6.jpg)

**Figure 6.6**

To generate a dialog, use the `<p:dialog>` component and within it add the components you want:

`<p:dialog header="Person" widgetVar="personDialog" modal="true" showEffect="fade" hideEffect="fade" resizable="true">`

`<p:outputPanel id="outputPanelPerson" style="text-align:center;">`

`<p:panelGrid columns="2">`

`<p:outputLabel value="name" />`

`<p:outputLabel value="#{personController.personSelected.name}" />`

`</p:panelGrid>`

`</p:outputPanel>`

`</p:dialog>`

# [Controller](#toc.xhtml_s112a)

The controllers represent the business logic, the operations that allow the model to interact with the view.

They are Java classes decorated with a series of annotations that are defined in the specification.

Next, create the `PersonController` class where you define a `String` attribute called name, a list of `Person` and a Person type object, plus the `save()` method, which saves the name of the person in the list, as shown below:

`@Named`

`@ViewScoped`

`@Getter`

`@Setter`

`public class PersonController implements Serializable{`

`private String name;`

`List<Person> personList = new ArrayList<>();`

`Person personSelected = new Person();`

`/**`

`* Creates a new instance of MessagesController`

`*/`

`public PersonController() {`

`}`

`public String save(){`

`try{`

`Person person = new Person(name);`

`personList.add(person);`

`} catch (Exception e) {`

`JsfUtil.errorDialog("test", e.getLocalizedMessage());`

`}`

`return "";`

`}`

`public String show(){`

`return "";`

`}`

`}`

# [Jmoordbjsf sample project](#toc.xhtml_s113a)

In the following example, we will create a web application that will work with jmoordbjsf (**[https://github.com/avbravo/jmoordbjsf](https://github.com/avbravo/jmoordbjsf)**) components that have been optimized for use with Jmoordb, to interact with a MongoDB database.

# [Docker](#toc.xhtml_s114a)

Proceed to install Docker compose in Ubuntu, if you use another operating system follow the instructions for each of them:

**`sudo snap install docker`**

**`sudo apt install docker-compose`**

**`docker-compose --version`**

Next, install MongoDB using the following command:

**`docker pull mongo:latest`**

![](images/6-10.jpg)

**Figure 6.10**

At the end of the installation, check if the image was properly installed docker images:

![](images/6-11.jpg)

**Figure 6.11**

Create the `docker-compose.yml` file:

`version: '3.4'`

`services:`

`mongodb_container:`

`image: "mongo:latest"`

`networks:`

`- nosql`

`ports:`

`- 27017:27017`

`volumes:`

`- mongodb_data_container:/data/db`

Create the folder `data/db`:

**`sudo mkdir -p /data/db`**

Run the following command:

**`docker-compose up -d`**

To check if the container is running run the command:

**`docker ps`**

1.  Restore using `mongorestore` offered by the MongoDB client.
2.  Specify it in the docker-compose file.

Using some plugins for Apache NetBeans IDE like:

*   Using nbmongo plugins.
*   jmoordbbackup **([http://plugins.netbeans.org/plugin/75520/jmoordbbackup](http://plugins.netbeans.org/plugin/75520/jmoordbbackup)).**

As an example, we will use the `nbmongo` plugin for Apache NetBeans IDE.

In the next section, we will interact with a MongoDB database using the Jmoordb API. We'll implement some patterns and architectures to create microservices that expose the data in the MongoDB database.

The following figure shows the model based on the repository pattern used in the application and with the jmoordb library to interact with the database:

![](images/6-26.jpg)

**Figure 6.26**

Some important concepts will be defined:

# [Entity](#toc.xhtml_s117a)

The entity is a Java class that represents the documents of a collection in a database. You will use jmoordb to define them. Some considerations that must be taken into account, it is necessary to define the main key through the `@Id` notation, as you can see in the following figure, we will work with two entities: `Profiles` and `User`. The `User` and `Profiles` collection is referenced by the `@Referenced` notation, and each one stores a list of embedded `UserInfo` by the `@Embedded` notation. As shown in the following screenshot:

![](images/6-27.jpg)

**Figure 6.27**

To continue with the project, proceed to create the entity:

1.  Create the `Profile.java` class with the following content:

    `public class Profile {`

    `@Id`

    `private String idprofile;`

    `private String profile;`

    `private String active;`

    `@Embedded`

    `List<UserInfo> userInfo;`

    `//Getters/Setters`

    `}`

2.  Create the `User.java` class that will contain the user information, it also has the `@Referenced` notation that will be used to reference documents from another collection such as `Profile.java`:

    `public class User {`

    `@Id`

    `private String username;`

    `@Size(min = 10, max = 250)`

    `private String password;`

    `@Size(min = 8, max = 250)`

    `private String name;`

    `@Referenced(collection = "Profiles", field = "idprofile", javatype = "String", lazy = false, repository = "com.jsfmicroservices.repository.ProfileRepository")`

    `private List<Profile> profile;`

    `private String active;`

    `@Embedded`

    `List<UserInfo> userInfo;`

    `//Setters/Getters`

    `}`

# [Repository](#toc.xhtml_s118a)

The repository pattern defines how the database interacts with the database through a class. Its definition is very simple, it is an extension of the repository interface defined in jmoordb. In the constructor it is invoked in two ways:

*   Just by indicating the class name, it will be assumed that the collection will be the same as the class name.
*   By defining the database name and the collection.

Create the class `ProfileRepository.java`:

`@Stateless`

`public class ProfileRepository extends Repository<Profile> {`

`public ProfileRepository(){`

`super(Profile.class,"jsfdata","profile");`

`}`

`}`

Create the class `UserRepository.java`:

`@Stateless`

`public class UserRepository extends Repository<User> {`

`public UserRepository(){`

`super(User.class,"jsfdata","user");`

`}`

`}`

# [Resources](#toc.xhtml_s119a)

Next, you will create the classes for the resources `UserResources` and `ProfileResources`, where the data will be exposed by means of the JAX-RS API, this topic will be extended in the following chapters.

The security policies that will be explained in the following chapters, for now edit the `web.xml` file and add the roles that will be used in the application. To be validated using the Jakarta Security API:

`<security-constraint>`

`<web-resource-collection>`

`<web-resource-name>Application pages</web-resource-name>`

`<url-pattern>/faces/pages/*</url-pattern>`

`</web-resource-collection>`

`<auth-constraint>`

`<role-name>admin</role-name>`

`<role-name>testing</role-name>`

`</auth-constraint>`

`</security-constraint>`

`<security-role>`

`<role-name>admin</role-name>`

`</security-role>`

`<security-role>`

`<role-name>testing</role-name>`

`</security-role>`

Then create the class `UserResources.java`, inject the repository and set the security measures to authorize through `@RolesAllowed({" "})`, indicate the `Path` through `@Path("/")`. In the following chapters, we will expand the topics about the use of microservices. Look at the following example:

`@Path("user")`

`public class UserResources {`

`@Inject`

`UserRepository userRepository;`

`@GET`

`@RolesAllowed({"admin"})`

`@Produces({MediaType.APPLICATION_XML, MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON})`

`public List<User> get() {`

`return userRepository.findAll();`

`}`

`@GET`

`@Path("/findall")`

`@RolesAllowed({"admin"})`

`@Produces({MediaType.APPLICATION_XML, MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON})`

`public List<User> findAll() {`

`return userRepository.findAll();`

`}`

`@POST`

`@Path("/add")`

`@RolesAllowed({"admin"})`

`@Consumes(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)`

`public Response create(User user) {`

`try {`

`if (userRepository.update(user)) {`

`return Response.status(201).entity("User saved").build();`

`}`

`return Response.status(400).entity("User could not be saved").build();`

`} catch (Exception e) {`

`return Response.status(400).entity("Error!!" + e.getLocalizedMessage()).build();`

`}`

`}`

`@PUT`

`@Path("/update")`

`@RolesAllowed({"admin"})`

`@Consumes(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)`

`public Response update(User user) {`

`try {`

`if (userRepository.update(user)) {`

`return Response.status(201).entity("Updated user").build();`

`}`

`return Response.status(400).entity("User was not updated").build();`

`} catch (Exception e) {`

`return Response.status(400).entity("Error!!" + e.getLocalizedMessage()).build();`

`}`

`}`

`@GET`

`@Path("/search/{username}")`

`@RolesAllowed({"admin"})`

`@Produces(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)`

`public User findByUsername(@PathParam("username") String username) {`

`User user = new User();`

`try {`

`user.setUsername(username);`

`Optional<User> optional = userRepository.findById(user);`

`if (!optional.isPresent()) {`

`user = optional.get();`

`}`

`} catch (Exception e) {`

`System.out.println("findByUsername() " + e.getLocalizedMessage());`

`}`

`return user;`

`}`

`}`

# [Entity](#toc.xhtml_s121a)

Create the two classes `User` and `Profile` exactly as they are in the other project:

`public class User {`

`@Id`

`private String username;`

`@Size(min = 10, max = 250)`

`private String password;`

`@Size(min = 8, max = 250)`

`private String name;`

`@Referenced(collection = "Profiles",`

`field = "idprofile", javatype = "String", lazy = false,`

`repository = "com.jsfmicroservices.repository.ProfileRepository")`

`private List<Profile> profile;`

`private String active;`

`@Embedded`

`List<UserInfo> userInfo;`

`public User() {`

`}`

`//`

`}`

# [Services](#toc.xhtml_s122a)

To communicate with the microservices we use several clients, one of the most used implementations in Java is Jersey (JAX-RS). Therefore, you will create services to consume the microservices available in the previous project. The following figure shows the general scheme of application communication:

![](images/6-36.jpg)

**Figure 6.36**

Create the `UserServices` class that contains the JAX-RS client. Note that in this example a method is used to consume all the available resources. Use the `Client` class to create a client that communicates with the microservice. Also register `HttpAuthenticationFeature`, to indicate the user and password you use for security. Through the `Webtarget` specify the path of the resource. `GenericType<List<T>>` is used to convert to the specified data type:

`@Stateless`

`public class UserServices implements Serializable {`

`private static final String PASS = "pass";`

`private static final String FAIL = "fail";`

`private static final String SUCCESS_RESULT = "<result>success</result>";`

`@Inject`

`AuthentificationProducer authentificationProducer;`

`// <editor-fold defaultstate="collapsed" desc="List<User> findAll()">`

`public List<User> findAll() {`

`List<User> userList = new ArrayList<>();`

`try {`

`Client client = ClientBuilder.newClient();`

`client.register(authentificationProducer.httpAuthenticationFeature());`

`WebTarget target = client.target("http://localhost:8080" + "/jsfmicroservices/resources/user/findall");`

`GenericType<List<User>> data = new GenericType<List<User>>() {`

`};`

`userList = target.request(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON).get(data);`

`} catch (Exception e) {`

`System.out.println("findAll()" + e.getLocalizedMessage());`

`}`

`return userList;`

`}`

`// </editor-fold>`

`// <editor-fold defaultstate="collapsed" desc="Boolean add(User user)">`

`public Boolean add(User user) {`

`try {`

`Client client = ClientBuilder.newClient();`

`client.register(authentificationProducer.httpAuthenticationFeature());`

`WebTarget webTarget = client.target("http://localhost:8080" + "/jsfmicroservices/resources/user/add");`

`Invocation.Builder invocationBuilder = webTarget.request(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON);`

`Response response = invocationBuilder.post(Entity.entity(user, MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON));`

`System.out.println(response.getStatus());`

`if (response.getStatus() == 400) {`

`return false;`

`}`

`System.out.println(response.readEntity(String.class));`

`return true;`

`} catch (Exception e) {`

`System.out.println("error" + e.getLocalizedMessage());`

`}`

`return false;`

`}`

`// </editor-fold>`

`// <editor-fold defaultstate="collapsed" desc="Boolean update(User user)">`

`public Boolean update(User user) {`

`try {`

`Client client = ClientBuilder.newClient();`

`client.register(authentificationProducer.httpAuthenticationFeature());`

`WebTarget webTarget = client.target("http://localhost:8080" + "/jsfmicroservices/resources/user/update");`

`Invocation.Builder invocationBuilder = webTarget.request(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON);`

`Response response = invocationBuilder.post(Entity.entity(user, MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON));`

`if (response.getStatus() == 400) {`

`return false;`

`}`

`System.out.println(response.readEntity(String.class));`

`return true;`

`} catch (Exception e) {`

`System.out.println("error" + e.getLocalizedMessage());`

`}`

`return false;`

`}`

`// </editor-fold>`

`// <editor-fold defaultstate="collapsed" desc="User findByUsername(String username) ">`

`public User findByUsername(String username) {`

`User user= new User();`

`try {`

`Client client = ClientBuilder.newClient();`

`client.register(authentificationProducer.httpAuthenticationFeature());`

`user= client.target("http://localhost:8080"+ "/jsfmicroservices/resources/user/search/")`

`.path("/{username}")`

`.resolveTemplate("username", username)`

`.request(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)`

`.get(User.class);`

`//String result = FAIL;`

`} catch (Exception e) {`

`System.out.println("findBUsername() " + e.getLocalizedMessage());`

`}`

`return user;`

`}`

`}`

`// </editor-fold>`

# [Converter](#toc.xhtml_s123a)

The converter has the function of converting `String` to objects and vice versa. Java Server Faces does not have direct converters for this reason you must make your own implementations. You can use the `@FacesConverter("name")` notation and the class must implement the `Converter`. They will be used for some components such as autocomplete, and `selectOneMenu`. For example:

`@FacesConverter("userConverter")`

`public class UserConverter implements Converter {`

`…`

`}`

Or through the class declaration with the following lines of code:

`@Named`

`@RequestScoped`

`public class UserConverter implements Converter {`

`}`

Below is the code for `UserConverter.java`, with the `getAsObject()` method returning the object that has been selected and the `getAsString()` method returning a `String` from the key attribute:

`@Named`

`@Named`

`@RequestScoped`

`public class UserConverter implements Converter {`

`@Inject`

`UserServices userServices;`

`@Override`

`public Object getAsObject(FacesContext facesContext, UIComponent uiComponent, String s) {`

`User user = new User();`

`try{`

`if(!s.equals("null")){`

`user = userServices.findByUsername(s);`

`}`

`} catch (Exception e) {`

`System.out.println("getAsObject() "+e.getLocalizedMessage());`

`}`

`return user;`

`}`

`@Override`

`public String getAsString(FacesContext facesContext, UIComponent uiComponent, Object o) {`

`String r = "";`

`try {`

`if (o instanceof User) {`

`User user = (User) o;`

`r = String.valueOf(user.getUsername());`

`}else if (o instanceof String) {`

`r = (String) o;`

`}`

`} catch (Exception e) {`

`System.out.println("getAsString() "+e.getLocalizedMessage());`

`}`

`return r;`

`}`

`}`

# [Datamodel](#toc.xhtml_s124a)

When working with `<p:datatable>` you can use in several ways, a `List<>` directly, or through `LazyDataModel` or in the example we use `DataModel`, to manage more efficiently the list of objects.

`DataModel` allows us to make a binding with the data and allows us to handle the selected row.

Let's define the `getRowData(String rowkey)` method that will return the object selected from the object list. The public Object `getRowKey(Role role)` method, returns the primary key. As shown in the code below:

`public class UserDataModel extends ListDataModel<User> implements SelectableDataModel<User> {`

`public User Data Model() {`

`}`

`public UserDataModel(List<User> data) {`

`super(data);`

`}`

`@Override`

`public User getRowData(String rowKey) {`

`List<User> list = (List<User>) getWrappedData();`

`for (User user : list) {`

`if (user.getUsername().equals(rowKey)) {`

`return user;`

`}`

`}`

`return null;`

`}`

`@Override`

`public Object getRowKey(User user) {`

`return user.getUsername();`

`}`

`}`

Create a JSF CDI Beans, and inject `UserServices` and define a `List<User>` and in the `init()` method invoke the `findAll()` method, as shown in the following code:

`@Named`

`@ViewScoped`

`@Getter`

`@Setter`

`public class UserController implements Serializable{`

`private static final long serialVersionUID = 1L;`

`@Inject`

`UserServices userServices;`

`List<User> userList = new ArrayList<>();`

`public UserController() {`

`}`

`@PostConstruct`

`public void init() {`

`userList = userServices.findAll();`

`for(User u:userList){`

`System.out.println("---> "+u.getName() + " profile "+u.getProfile().get(0));`

`}`

`}`

`}`

Edit the `index.xhtml` file and add a panel and `datatable` associated with `userList`. Remember that `Profiles` is defined inside the User as a `List<>`, so a repeat will be used to go through each one of the records and show the value of the `profile` attribute:

`<p:panel header="User" >`

`<p:dataTable value="#{userController.userList}" var="item">`

`<p:column headerText="Username">`

`<p:outputLabel value="#{item.username}"/>`

`</p:column>`

`<p:column headerText="Name">`

`<p:outputLabel value="#{item.name}"/>`

`</p:column>`

`<p:column headerText="Profile">`

`<p:repeat value="#{item.profile}" var="var">`

`<p:outputLabel value="#{var.profile}"/>`

`</p:repeat>`

`</p:column>`

`</p:dataTable>`

`</p:panel>`

Execute the project and the `datatable` with the User data is displayed. That is being consumed from the microservices:

![](images/6-37.jpg)

**Figure 6.37**

# [Internationalization](#toc.xhtml_s125a)

Multi-language support is essential for applications. Jmoordbutils contains properties files with the most common tags to be used in projects, such as messages, buttons, and warnings.

Jmoordbjsf has been optimized to use the `messages.properties` file that is stored in the `com.properties` package. Create the packages `com.properties` and within it generate the `messages.properties` file as shown in the following screenshot:

![](images/6-38.jpg)

**Figure 6.38**

For your convenience group the labels by categories, this way it will be easier to use them, for example:

*   label
*   field
*   button
*   warning
*   info
*   error
*   menubar
*   menu
*   dialog

The properties files you can add support for new languages.

![](images/6-39.jpg)

**Figure 6.39**

Select the language you want to add support as shown in the following screenshot:

![](images/6-40.jpg)

**Figure 6.40**

In the project in the repository, you can see the properties file. Then select or open the file and choose the option **`Add properties`** and add the attributes you want:

![](images/6-41.jpg)

**Figure 6.41**

In the `xhtml` pages in the `h:head` section, add the following lines:

`<h:head>`

`<f:facet name="first">`

`<f:view locale="#{jmoordbLanguajes.locale !=null?jmoordbLanguajes.locale:'es'}"></f:view>`

`<f:loadBundle basename="com.properties.messages" var="msg" />`

`<f:loadBundle basename="com.jmoordbutils.properties.application" var="app" />`

`</f:facet>`

`<title>#{msg['application.shorttitle']}</title>`

`</h:head>`

`JmoordbLanguages` is a class within `JmoordbUtils` that allows us to support multiple languages. With `<f:loadBundle>` the property files containing the tags are loaded. By default, the `application.properties` file containing the most frequently used tags was created inside `jmoordUtils`.

To use it for example in values `use #{msg['field.username']}` for tags in your local project, for tags that are stored inside `application.properties` use `#{app['field.username']}`. Now modify the properties of the `datatable` tags:

`<p:dataTable value="#{userController.userList}" var="item">`

`<p:column headerText="#{msg['field.username']}">`

`<p:outputLabel value="#{item.username}"/>`

`</p:column>`

`<p:column headerText="#{msg['field.name']}">`

`<p:outputLabel value="#{item.name}"/>`

`</p:column>`

`<p:column headerText="#{msg['field.profile']}">`

`<p:repeat value="#{item.profile}" var="var">`

`<p:outputLabel value="#{var.profile}"/>`

`</p:repeat>`

`</p:column>`

`</p:dataTable>`

Example:

`@Inject`

`JmoordbResourcesFiles rf;`

`private void show(){`

`JsfUtil.successMessage(rf.getAppMessage(“login.welcome”));`

`}`

would show the following result:

![](images/6-42.jpg)

**Figure 6.42**

# [Login](#toc.xhtml_s126a)

In `jmoordbjsf` create a component for the users login, just add to the namespaces `xmlns:jmoordbjsf="http://jmoordbjsf.com/taglib"`, and include the component `<jmoordbjsf:login/>` defining the required attributes:

`<jmoordbjsf:login`

`id="login"`

`fieldusername="#{userController.username}"`

`labelusername="#{app['login.username']}"`

`fieldpassword="#{userController.password}"`

`dologin="#{userController.login()}"`

`showone="false"`

`idone="rol"`

`/>`

In the `UserController.java` class add the `login()`, where the user will validate by making a request to the microservice:

`public String login(){`

`try {`

`JsfUtil.successMessage(rf.getAppMessage("login.welcome"));`

`User user= userServices.findByUsername(username);`

`if (user== null || user.getUsername() == null) {`

`JsfUtil.warningMessage(rf.getAppMessage("login.usernamenotvalid"));`

`return "";`

`} else {`

`JsfUtil.infoDialog("Welcome ", "user "+user.getName());`

`}`

`} catch (Exception e) {`

`JsfUtil.errorDialog("login()", e.getLocalizedMessage());`

`}`

`return "";`

`}`

When the project is executed you can see the component drawn on the screen where a text box appears to request the username, a password box and a button to validate the access. As shown in the following screenshot:

![](images/6-43.jpg)

**Figure 6.43**

# [Autocomplete](#toc.xhtml_s127a)

Many common functionalities are implemented through the services. In particular, they are used in conjunction with the `jmoordbjsf` components, so that regex searches can be made within the MongoDB database. This way in all the ways in which it is necessary to use an autocomplete role for example, it is only necessary to inject the corresponding services. In the image, we describe the general scheme:

![](images/6-44.jpg)

**Figure 6.44**

In the following segment, the code of the complete method that is associated with the component `< autocomplete>` is shown, and it summons the `complete()` method of the repository:

`public List<Profile> complete(String query) {`

`return repository.complete(query);`

`}`

Open the `jsfmicroservices` project and search for `ProfileResources` and add the following method that allows you to do Regex-based searches in a MongoDB database using `jmoordb`:

`@GET`

`@Path("/autocomplete/{query}")`

`@RolesAllowed({"admin"})`

`@Produces({MediaType.APPLICATION_XML, MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON})`

`public List<Profile> complete(@PathParam("query") String query) {`

`List<Profile> suggestions = new ArrayList<>();`

`try {`

`query = query.trim();`

`suggestions = profileRepository.findRegex("profile", query, true, new Document("profile", 1));`

`} catch (Exception e) {`

`System.out.println("complete() " + e.getLocalizedMessage());`

`}`

`return suggestions;`

`}`

When running the project the endpoint that will be available is `GET /jsfmicroservices/resources/profile/autocomplete/{query}`. Now edit the `ProfileServices.java` file in the `Jsfclient` project and add the complete method:

`public List<Profile> complete(String query) {`

`List<Profile> suggestions = new ArrayList<>();`

`try {`

`Client client = ClientBuilder.newClient();`

`client.register(authentificationProducer.httpAuthenticationFeature());`

`suggestions = client`

`.target("http://localhost:8080"+ "/jsfmicroservices/resources/profile/autocomplete/")`

`.path("/{query}")`

`.resolveTemplate("query", query)`

`.request(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)`

`.get(new GenericType<List<Profile>>() {});`

`} catch (Exception e) {`

`System.out.println("complete() " + e.getLocalizedMessage());`

`}`

`return suggestions;`

`}`

When defining an autocomplete in our `.xhtml` page use the component `<jmoordbjsf:autocomplete>`, you can see that the property `completeMethod` refers to the complete method that is defined within the `Service`, the field by which the search will be performed. Indicate the converter, the complete method, the values for the `labeltips`, a listener and other attributes:

`<jmoordbjsf:autocomplete converter="#{profileConverter}"`

`completeMethod="#{userController.profileServices.complete}"`

`labeltip1="#{msg['field.idprofile']} #{p.idprofile}"`

`labeltip2="#{msg['field.profile']} #{p.profile}"`

`listener="#{userController.handleAutocompleteOfListXhtml}"`

`value="#{userController.profileSearch}"`

`itemLabel="#{p.idprofile}"`

`field="idprofile"`

`dropdown="true"`

`minQueryLength="0"`

`update=":form"/>`

Executing it and we would have a view like the following figure where the user writes a term and the list of Profiles that fulfill that condition is displayed:

![](images/6-45.jpg)

**Figure 6.45**

# [Conclusion](#toc.xhtml_s129a)

This chapter was a very basic introduction to Java Server Faces by way of very simple examples. We splitted projects, based on Jmoordb Stack, Primefaces or BootsFaces as well, into Microservices architecture,. PrimeFaces is a wonderful tool to create Web applications from Java in a friendly and fast way. We recommend visiting the site official (**[http://www.primefaces.org](http://www.primefaces.org)**) web site where you will find many examples of the use of components.

PrimeFaces is a wonderful tool to create web application. It is one of the elaborated implementation of the JSF specification. We have also demonstrate the use of one of the next generation of JSF framework Jmoorb jsf. It the combination of the power of the two major JSF implementations (PrimeFaces and OmniFaces) and has the flexibility to communicate with NoSQL databases.

# [CHAPTER 7](#toc.xhtml_c07)

# [Vaadin](#toc.xhtml_c07)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml_s131a)

Vaadin is an open-source web framework that helps Java developers build great user experiences with minimal effort. This chapter will introduce the framework with its complete set of user interface components designed for web applications.

# [Structure](#toc.xhtml_s132a)

*   Introduction to Vaadin
*   Architecture to Vaadin
*   Web components
*   Form inputs
    *   Label
    *   Button
    *   CheckBox
    *   ComboBox
    *   Text Field
    *   Text Area
    *   Date Picker
    *   Email Field
    *   Select
    *   Number Field
    *   Password Field
    *   Radio Button
    *   Dialog
    *   Grid
    *   Form Layout
    *   App Layout
    *   Accordion
    *   Notification
    *   Login
    *   Upload
    *   Tabs
    *   Lumo

# [Objective](#toc.xhtml_s133a)

This chapter covers an introduction to one of the most innovative, modern, and cohesive Java frameworks with high performance and adaptability for modern web applications.

# [Introduction to Vaadin](#toc.xhtml_s134a)

What is Vaadin? It is a Finnish company with more than thirteen years of experience doing free software, although the development center of Vaadin is in Finland also has offices in Germany and the United States, traditionally the star product has been the framework for making web applications in Java.

Today we are talking about Vaadin 14 which is much more than a framework, it is really a complete platform designed to simplify the development of web applications. Web applications based on combining blocks that fit perfectly between them.

We can create complex and modern Enterprise applications. All these components are designed to be accessible to adapt to any available device, which is called responsive, Vaadin is also prepared for new technologies such as **PWA (Progressive Web Application)**, HTTP2, etc.

What are Progressive Web Applications? They are web applications that can be installed directly on the home screen of your device so when you open them they appear as if they were a native application without the decorations of the browser.

Therefore the new trend will possibly replace the development of native applications making web applications.

# [Architecture to Vaadin](#toc.xhtml_s135a)

Vaadin architecture has been focused on improving communication between the user interface and client-server, making it easier for the Java developer to use web technologies such as HTML, CSS, and JavaScript, facilitating the use of web components in a secure server-side manner. Giving Java developers full access to all modern web enhancements.

On the server-side for frontend developers in this case working with JavaScript, they can create a server-side Java application and have full access to the backend more easily where they can implement client-side TypeScript views.

Another feature that I find a great advantage when developing an application, is the ease of connecting the user interface to the data through a robust Java backend, instead of using the traditional REST based communication. According to my work experience when I have worked with subtraction based services is always complexity maintenance or a way to recover the general state of the application and use the network communication protocols when it is the case.

These architectural approaches and programming models are being used and how the framework leverages development productivity much more than in the past. The Vaadin idea now provides a modern solution for developing business applications. This makes development easier than ever before in terms of both time and learning.

Some main features of Vaadin architecture, as you can see in *[Figure 7.1](#c07.xhtml_fig7_1):*

*   **Web components:** The web components that are the visual blocks, with which the application will be composed and that are developed using a new browser standard called custom elements. These two components will be able to be used not only in our framework but also with any other framework like polymer, angular, that is to say, anyone that knows how to handle the browser's Dom.
*   **Theme:** These are default theme options that can be defined via CSS or HTML template.
*   **Application Server:** In this section, you can integrate the same themes, HTML template, components, and backend services generating in the user interface logic a great capacity of response in the communication.
*   **Back-End:** It allows you to use the components from the virtual machine that is to say from the backend, we have the web components that are independent by themselves, but from the backend, we are able to use those same components, this makes Vaadin a framework that is unique in this architecture, that is to say in other words that Vaadin refers to the server of the virtual machine, therefore, in theory, you will be able to make web applications using any language that is executed on the JVM such as Scala, groovy, etc.

On the other hand, the business logic can be executed in the same servlet as the code of the UI and the data storage in some cases is distributed in a database management system or flat files, in the case of the database it is accessed by means of a persistence solution such as JPA which is a technique for converting data from the type system of an object-oriented language and the relational model of the database, this is known as **Object Relational Mapping (ORM)**. Therefore you can deploy a Vaadin application like any other java web application. For example, you can package this class as part of a standard Java web application in a WAR file and deploy it to a web server (or more precisely, a Servlet container) such as Tomcat:

![](images/7-1.jpg)

**Figure 7.1**

# [Form Inputs](#toc.xhtml_s136a)

*   **Label:** Component is the easiest component related to Vaadin, with Label you can display text in different formats or scenarios depending on the mode of the content, such as non-editable text, plain text, and HTML text.
