So basically creating the button is quite easy, its main function is initiating some action, such as finalizing input in forms. Button has a method that allows you to add click listeners, which can be handled by adding a click listener using either the `addClickListener()` or the `onClick()` or method.

*   **CheckBox:** The verification boxes the only difference between option buttons (Option Button) and checkboxes (Checkbox) is that more than one option can be selected within the same container. When a checkbox is activated, the verified value to a Boolean property can be determined with the `setValue()` method and obtained with the `getValue()` method. To modify the value of a checkbox produces a `ValueChangeEvent`, which can be used by a `ValueChangeListener`.

*   **ComboBox:** Is a selection component that allows you to select an item from a drop-down list. But the Combobox also hides the list of items and is only shown when you click on the down arrow button containing the control.

    **A drop-down:** This is the most common value and corresponds to the case where the selected record is displayed, which is editable by the user, where you can type text to find the matching items and select one of them. The rest of the elements will remain hidden until the user displays the complete list by clicking on the down arrow button.

*   **Text Field:** A TextField control is one that allows the user to enter data at run time. They are also widely used to display data contained in a database or text file. Whenever you need to request data from the user use the text boxes, for example, registering an employee, registering a customer, registering a product in a warehouse, entering student grades, registering a form, etc.

It is used when it is required to display or request a large amount of data in the text box. For example, to show the content of a file or to request a lot of user data such as comment, note, explanation, details, etc. Word processors (Word, WordPad, Notepad, etc.) are examples of applications that handle text boxes with multiple lines.

*   **Grid:** The Web Component for the Grid can lazily load data from any data source, display it, and allow manipulation of data from a series of rows and columns corresponding to records and fields. This component may be capable of maintaining text and/or graphics. On the other hand, they can handle large data that is present and easily scroll through 100k lines of data in a single component of the user interface.

    As can be seen in the following screenshot is an example of a Grid:

    ![](images/7-31.jpg)

    **Figure 7.31**

    Now, we can see the result of Grid example as shown in the following screenshot:

    ![](images/7-32.jpg)

    **Figure 7.32**

*   **Form Layout:** The Web Component for Form Layout provides a configurable and responsive design for form elements. One of its main features is that it secures the form content so that it remains easily readable for all devices. On the other hand, it helps the programmer to develop an application in the least possible time.

    As can be seen in the following screenshot is an example of a **Form Layout**:

    ![](images/7-33.jpg)

    **Figure 7.33**

    Now, we can see the result of **Form Layout** example as shown in the following screenshot:

    ![](images/7-34.jpg)

    **Figure 7.34**

    In the following screenshot, we can see how the **Form Layout** is shown in a mobile device:

    ![](images/7-35.jpg)

    **Figure 7.35**

*   **App Layout:** The App Layout provides a quick and easy way to achieve a common application design structure. It allows the design of an application such as a menu that can be configured horizontally at the top, and in turn, can have a vertical drawer on the side. Both components can be combined to have a side menu with an application header bar. On the other hand, you can also configure the page content, icons, logos, etc.

    One of the advantages is that the menus are adjusted to the size of the screen so that it is intuitive to use on all devices as if it were a native application.

    As can be seen in the following screenshot is an example of an **App Layout**:

    ![](images/7-36.jpg)

    **Figure 7.36**

    Now, we can see the result of **App Layout** example as shown in the following screenshot:

    ![](images/7-37.jpg)

    **Figure 7.37**

*   **Menu Bar:** The set of options presented to the user for selection in a certain area of the screen, these are called menus. Most of the applications developed in the market have menus that contain all the necessary tools to make possible the utility of the application.

    As can be seen in *[Figure 7.39](#c07.xhtml_fig7_39)* it is similar to programs such as Word, Excel, PowerPoint, which include menu bars, and within these bars are the menus and within the menus the options for each menu.

    For example, as shown in *[Figure 7.39](#c07.xhtml_fig7_39)* the **`Product`** menu of the demo includes options or commands such as Sales, Billing, and submenus Find, Add, Delete, etc.

    The advantage of the menus over the other controls is that they take up less space on the screen, but are limited in that the options or commands on the menus are not visible in some cases until the menu is fully displayed.

    As can be seen in the following screenshot an example of a Menu Bar:

    ![](images/7-38.jpg)

    **Figure 7.38**

    Now, we can see the result of Menu Bar example as shown in the following screenshot:

    ![](images/7-39.jpg)

    **Figure 7.39**

*   **Accordion:** The accordion web component is a set of expandable panels to show and hide the content to the user so that the user interface is less saturated. This component is composed in other words by elements called nodes and subnodes (secondary nodes). You can show primary nodes or nodes that contain secondary nodes, as expanded or contracted. It is similar to another component of the Vaadin family with the name TreeGrid.

    On the other hand, the Component Accordion one of the advantages can be displayed well in both mobile and desktop environments. Therefore it supports keyboard and screen readers. It also supports Lumo and Material themes that will be explained at the end of the chapter.

    As can be seen in the following screenshot is an example of an Accordion:

    ![](images/7-40.jpg)

    **Figure 7.40**

    Now, we can see the result of the Accordion example as shown in the following screenshot we can see the Accordion component for this example contains a user's personal information and health information. In this image, you can see the name and document:

    ![](images/7-41.jpg)

    **Figure 7.41**

    Continuing with the same example in the following screenshot we expand the user's health information and show us the data such as the EPS and city where the services are provided:

    ![](images/7-42.jpg)

    **Figure 7.42**

*   **Notification:** The Web Component of the notifications, serves to show a small panel that appears on the screen, therefore you can customize the location and time that the notification will be shown. Usually, it is always set for a short time and a message is displayed.

    This example uses the `setDuration`, which indicates how long it should take for the notification to close by itself. As you can see in the following screenshot you must specify in milliseconds, for example, if you want the notification to be open for 5 seconds, you pass the value of `5000`:

    ![](images/7-43.jpg)

    **Figure 7.43**

    Now, we can see the result of the Notification example as shown in the following screenshot, as we have been able to observe the figure, we click on the button **`Example Notification`** and automatically it emits us a message that has a duration of 5 seconds:

    ![](images/7-44.jpg)

    **Figure 7.44**

*   **Login:** Has a set of Web Components for the login form with password manager support. It consists of the verification of an entity such as a person, vehicle, computer, etc. requesting access to a resource. This component can add an event listener so you can provide password recovery instructions for your users. On the other hand, it can be adapted to different languages and regions without the need to modify the code, in other words, you can internationalize an application with i18n.

    As can be seen in the following screenshot is an example of Login:

    ![](images/7-45.jpg)

    **Figure 7.45**

    Now, we can see the result of the Login example as shown in the following screenshot. Here you can see two scenarios, the first is that the person who wants to enter the application enters the username and password correctly.

    In the second scenario in case the data is not entered correctly the component is in charge of showing and hiding an error message to provide information to the user when an unsuccessful login attempt occurs:

    ![](images/7-46.jpg)

    **Figure 7.46**

*   **Upload:** The Web Upload Component provides a number of specialized features, such as uploading multiple files by indicating their progress, drag and drop uploads the files with the drag and drop method. Allows you to display any drive you have in your computer, this control includes hard drives, floppy disks, Cd-ROM, etc. On the other hand, its most interesting property is `setAcceptedFileTypes` that allows us to specify what kind of files are shown in that component. If you set the property `setAcceptedFileTypes` with the string `*.txt`, you are indicating that only files with this extension are shown. More than one file type can be shown by separating them with a forward slash (/).

    As you can see in the following screenshot is an example of Upload. We will try uploading a JPG file format:

    ![](images/67-47.jpg)

    **Figure 7.47**

    In the following, for example, as shown in the following screenshot the upload component is used, we click on the **`Upload File…`** button to browse the system drives and display the image format files:

    ![](images/7-48.jpg)

    **Figure 7.48**

    As you can see in the following screenshot we added an image file of type `JPG`:

    ![](images/7-49.jpg)

    **Figure 7.49**

*   **Tabs:** The tabbed Web Component is important for you to know about a feature of Tab components that often confuses programmers. That is, the tabs organize content into separate views where you can only see one view at a time. It's not a container, that is, it doesn't contain sections where you can place the elements for each option, but rather, it's a set of views that must be associated with components such as grid and form layout. So the tabs can be configured both horizontally and vertically, finally, the label of each tab is shown in the header of the same and can be moved if necessary.

    As you can see in the following screenshot is an example of Tabs:

    ![](images/7-50.jpg)

    **Figure 7.50**

    Now, we can see the result of **`Tabs`** example as shown in the following screenshot, in the example, 3 tabs are created with 3 pages with country names and their respective page names:

    ![](images/7-51.jpg)

    **Figure 7.51**

    In the following screenshot, it shows the country of Colombia:

    ![](images/7-52.jpg)

    **Figure 7.52**

    In the following screenshot, it shows the country of Brazil:

    ![](images/7-53.jpg)

    **Figure 7.53**

*   **Lumo:** Lumo is a design that has evolved the modern web applications, where the developers of Vaadin in a single line of code can change the appearance of the theme of the application, have the advantage that they can create their custom configuration of the theme. Vaadin Components implement lumo as their default theme.

    Let's look at a basic example of how Lumo is implemented in a demo.

    In this example we will make a copy of the Login example, the only observation that will be made is to change the theme to dark configuring in the @Theme annotation the `Lumo.DARK` variant, by default the demo contains the light theme as you can see in the following screenshot:

# [Objective](#toc.xhtml_s142a)

In this chapter, we will develop a modern web application, using everything learned from previous chapter, connected to a MongoDB database performing a CRUD operation, that is adaptable for mobile devices and progressive web applications.

# [Building a modern web application](#toc.xhtml_s143a)

We built a basic application to manage user data and their respective roles.

To keep things simple, the application will integrate with the Java EE framework and connect to a relational (MySQL or PostgreSQL) or Non-Relational (MongoDB) database, applying the Design Patterns, POO, and SOLID principles.

The end result of chapter is to develop a web page with a dynamic menu, where you can easily create new custom views, add tables (called a Grid in Vaadin) that have functionality capabilities to add, delete and update user data.

The following are the features:

*   Customizable styles
*   Desktop and mobile installation
*   Templates for CRUD.
*   100% Java-based templates.

![](images/8-1.jpg)

**Figure 8.1**

You can use the application as a project started with *Vaadin*. For example, you could add new views, create a custom theme, use plug-ins, and optimize for mobile devices.

Here are the tools to be used.

# [Requirements](#toc.xhtml_s144a)

*   **Programming language:** Java SE 8, Node.js 10
*   Intellij, NetBeans, or Eclipse
*   **Framework:** Vaadin 14
*   **Database:** MongoDB (**[https://www.mongodb.com/download-center](https://www.mongodb.com/download-center)**, **[https://mongodb.github.io/mongo-java-driver/](https://mongodb.github.io/mongo-java-driver/)**)
*   **Component Vaadin:** Crud UI Add-on

# [What is Maven?](#toc.xhtml_s146a)

Apache Maven is a software project management tool. The basis of Maven is the object model of the `pom` project. The goal of Maven is to facilitate and unify the software development process.

The minimum version of the pom.xml file must contain the project root, `modelVersion`, `groupId`, `artifactId`, and version.

Now we will visualize the structure of the pom.xml file, which dependency we must add for this project, Jmoordbutils, Lombok, the configuration of the JDK, and the version of the Vaadin framework, we can see the `pom.xml` file.

The following code is from the `pom.xml` file:

# [Models](#toc.xhtml_s149a)

A summary explaining the Classes or interfaces that will be used in the project or demo. This type of models represents a Role. It contains an `idRole`, a name, two boolean fields the first for module authorization, the second that has access to the application.

For the Class user this contains the field `idUser`, for the identification of the user, `firstName` and `lastName` the complete name of the user, additionally other basic information like his email, password, country, etc.

# [Class Role](#toc.xhtml_s150a)

The following code is the Class Role:

`import com.avbravo.jmoordb.anotations.Embedded;`

`import com.avbravo.jmoordb.anotations.Id;`

`import com.avbravo.jmoordb.anotations.Secondary;`

`import com.avbravo.jmoordb.pojos.UserInfo;`

`import java.util.List;`

`public class Role {`

`@Id`

`private String idRole;`

`@Secondary`

`private String name;`

`private Boolean authorizedModule1;`

`private Boolean accessModule2;`

`public Role(){}`

`public Role(String idRole, String name, Boolean authorizedModule1, Boolean accessModule2) {`

`this.idRole = idRole;`

`this.name = name;`

`this.authorizedModule1 = authorizedModule1;`

`this.accessModule2 = accessModule2;`

`}`

`public String getIdRole() {`

`return idRole;`

`}`

`public void setIdRole(String idRole) {`

`this.idRole = idRole;`

`}`

`public String getName() {`

`return name;`

`}`

`public void setName(String name) {`

`this.name = name;`

`}`

`public Boolean getAuthorizedModule1() {`

`return authorizedModule1;`

`}`

`public void setAuthorizedModule1(Boolean authorizedModule1) {`

`this.authorizedModule1 = authorizedModule1;`

`}`

`public Boolean getAccessModule2() {`

`return accessModule2;`

`}`

`public void setAccessModule2(Boolean accessModule2) {`

`this.accessModule2 = accessModule2;`

`}`

`@Override`

`public String toString() {`

`return "Role{" +`

`"idRole=" + idRole +`

`", name='" + name + '\'' +`

`", authorizedModule1=" + authorizedModule1 +`

`", accessModule2=" + accessModule2 +`

`'}';`

`}`

# [Class User](#toc.xhtml_s151a)

The following illustrates the Class User:

`import com.avbravo.jmoordb.anotations.Embedded;`

`import com.avbravo.jmoordb.anotations.Id;`

`import com.avbravo.jmoordb.anotations.Secondary;`

`import com.avbravo.jmoordb.pojos.UserInfo;`

`import javax.validation.constraints.Email;`

`import java.util.List;`

`public class User {`

`@Id`

`private String idUser;`

`@Secondary`

`private String firstName;`

`private String lastName;`

`@Email`

`private String contactEmail;`

`private String password;`

`private Boolean blocked;`

`private String country;`

`private Role mainRole;`

`public User(){}`

`public User(String idUser, String firstName, String lastName, @Email String contactEmail, String password, Boolean blocked, String country, Role mainRole) {`

`this.idUser = idUser;`

`this.firstName = firstName;`

`this.lastName = lastName;`

`this.contactEmail = contactEmail;`

`this.password = password;`

`this.blocked = blocked;`

`this.country = country;`

`this.mainRole = mainRole;`

`}`

`public String getIdUser() {`

`return idUser;`

`}`

`public void setIdUser(String idUser) {`

`this.idUser = idUser;`

`}`

`public String getFirstName() {`

`return firstName;`

`}`

`public void setFirstName(String firstName) {`

`this.firstName = firstName;`

`}`

`public String getLastName() {`

`return lastName;`

`}`

`public void setLastName(String lastName) {`

`this.lastName = lastName;`

`}`

`public String getContactEmail() {`

`return contactEmail;`

`}`

`public void setContactEmail(String contactEmail) {`

`this.contactEmail = contactEmail;`

`}`

`public String getPassword() {`

`return password;`

`}`

`public void setPassword(String password) {`

`this.password = password;`

`}`

`public Boolean getBlocked() {`

`return blocked;`

`}`

`public void setBlocked(Boolean blocked) {`

`this.blocked = blocked;`

`}`

`public String getCountry() {`

`return country;`

`}`

`public void setCountry(String country) {`

`this.country = country;`

`}`

`public Role getMainRole() {`

`return mainRole;`

`}`

`public void setMainRole(Role mainRole) {`

`this.mainRole = mainRole;`

`}`

`@Override`

`public String toString() {`

`return "User{" +`

`"idUser=" + idUser +`

`", firstName='" + firstName + '\'' +`

`", lastName='" + lastName + '\'' +`

`", contactEmail='" + contactEmail + '\'' +`

`", password='" + password + '\'' +`

`", blocked=" + blocked +`

`", country='" + country + '\'' +`

`", mainRole=" + mainRole +`

`'}';`

`}`

`}`

`}`

# [Resources](#toc.xhtml_s155a)

Create API resources endpoint for the user, web services are applications that communicate over the Internet using the HTTP protocol. There are many different types of web service architectures, but the main idea in all designs is the same. In this article, we are creating a RESTful web service from what is a design, in order to see a person's information. Creating an endpoint allows you to create a new record of the person in the system. There is also the option of an endpoint, where it returns a JSON answer where the information of all the users registered in the application is displayed, on the other hand, the endpoint allows to search for the person's information by the user's name.

Currently, every basic API resource contains two parts:

*   The actual resource that takes care of the CRUD operations.
*   A controller that handles the different request types (`POST`, `GET`, `PUT`, `DELETE`).

# [UserResource Class](#toc.xhtml_s156a)

The `UserResource` class facilitates repository communication with save, update, delete, list by `findByName` and `findAll` User operations, as can be seen in the `UserResource` class code:

`import com.avbravo.jmoordbutils.JsfUtil;`

`import com.book.bpb.backend.dao.UserManagement;`

`import com.book.bpb.backend.entity.User;`

`import org.bson.Document;`

`import java.util.List;`

`import javax.inject.Inject;`

`import javax.ws.rs.Consumes;`

`import javax.ws.rs.DELETE;`

`import javax.ws.rs.GET;`

`import javax.ws.rs.POST;`

`import javax.ws.rs.PUT;`

`import javax.ws.rs.Path;`

`import javax.ws.rs.PathParam;`

`import javax.ws.rs.core.MediaType;`

`import javax.ws.rs.core.Response;`

`@Path("users")`

`public class UserResource {`

`String directoryLogger = JsfUtil.isLinux() ? JsfUtil.userHome() + JsfUtil.fileSeparator() + "demo" + JsfUtil.fileSeparator() + "logs" + JsfUtil.fileSeparator() + "logger.json" : "C:\\demo\\logs\\logger.json";`

`@Inject`

`UserManagement userManagement;`

`@GET`

`public List<User> findAll() {`

`return this.userManagement.findAll();`

`}`

`@GET`

`@Path("{name}")`

`public List<User> findByName(@PathParam("name") String name) {`

`return this.userManagement.findRegex("name", name, true, new Document("name", 1));`

`}`

`@POST`

`public Response createUser(User user) {`

`try {`

`if (userManagement.update(user)) {`

`return Response.status(201).entity("User saved").build();`

`}`

`return Response.status(400).entity("User could not be saved").build();`

`} catch (Exception e) {`

`JsfUtil.appendTextToLogErrorFile(this.directoryLogger, JsfUtil.nameOfClass(), JsfUtil.nameOfMethod(), e.getLocalizedMessage(), e);`

`return Response.status(400).entity("Error!!" + e.getLocalizedMessage()).build();`

`}`

`}`

`@PUT`

`@Path("/update")`

`@Consumes(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)`

`public Response update(User user) {`

`try {`

`if (userManagement.update(user)) {`

`return Response.status(201).entity("Updated User").build();`

`}`

`return Response.status(400).entity("User was not updated").build();`

`} catch (Exception e) {`

`JsfUtil.appendTextToLogErrorFile(this.directoryLogger, JsfUtil.nameOfClass(), JsfUtil.nameOfMethod(), e.getLocalizedMessage(), e);`

`return Response.status(400).entity("Error!!" + e.getLocalizedMessage()).build();`

`}`

`}`

`@DELETE`

`@Path("/delete")`

`@Consumes(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)`

`public Response delete(User user) {`

`try {`

`if (userManagement.delete(user)) {`

`return Response.status(201).entity("delete user").build();`

`}`

`return Response.status(400).entity("Not update of user").build();`

`} catch (Exception e) {`

`JsfUtil.appendTextToLogErrorFile(this.directoryLogger, JsfUtil.nameOfClass(), JsfUtil.nameOfMethod(), e.getLocalizedMessage(), e);`

`return Response.status(400).entity("error!!" + e.getLocalizedMessage()).build();`

`}`

`}`

`}`

# [UserList Class](#toc.xhtml_s159a)

`@Route(value = "users",layout = MainView.Class)`

`@PageTitle("Users List")`

`public class userList extends Composite<VerticalLayout> {`

`private Button refresh = new Button("", VaadinIcon.REFRESH.create());`

`private Button add = new Button("", VaadinIcon.PLUS.create());`

`private Button edit = new Button("", VaadinIcon.PENCIL.create());`

`private Grid<User> grid = new Grid<>(User.Class);`

`private final IUserService repo;`

`private final IRoleService role;`

We create the form that extends from a dialog box, which will serve to enter or update the user's registration:

`private TextField firstName = new TextField("First name");`

`private TextField lastName = new TextField("Last name");`

`private EmailField contactEmail = new EmailField("Contact Email");`

`private PasswordField password = new PasswordField("Password");`

`private CheckboxGroup<Role> roles = new CheckboxGroup<>();`

`private ComboBox<Role> mainRole = new ComboBox<>("Main Role", role.listar());`

`private Checkbox blocked = new Checkbox("Blocked");`

`private ComboBox<String> country = new ComboBox<>("Country");`

`private Button cancel = new Button("Cancel");`

`private Button save = new Button("Save", VaadinIcon.CHECK.create());`

When any of the buttons are clicked, a `UserFormDialog` is displayed. For the add button, we pass a new user instance. For the update button, we pass the `User` instance selected in the GRID. We can implement `UserFormDialog` as an internal Class within Crud:

`public UserFormDialog(String caption, User user) {`

`initLayout(caption);`

`initBehavior(user);`

`}`

All input fields in the form are members of the `UserFormWindow` Class and are added to some kind of design in the `initLayout` method.

The `initBehaviour` method must set up the data link between the user instance and the input fields. The `initBehaviour` method should set up the data link between the user instance and the input fields.

We need data-binding. In the Vaadin framework, that usually means using a binder:

`private void initLayout(String caption) {`

`roles.setItems(role.listar());`

`save.getElement().setAttribute("theme", "primary");`

`HorizontalLayout buttons = new HorizontalLayout(cancel, save);`

`buttons.setSpacing(true);`

`FormLayout formLayout = new FormLayout(new H2(caption), firstName, lastName, contactEmail, password, country, roles, mainRole, blocked);`

`VerticalLayout layout = new VerticalLayout(formLayout, buttons);`

`layout.setAlignSelf(FlexComponent.Alignment.END, buttons);`

`add(layout);`

`}`

`private List<String> getCountriesList()`

`{`

`return Stream.of(Locale.getISOCountries())`

`.map(iso -> new Locale("",iso).getDisplayCountry()).sorted()`

`.collect(Collectors.toList());`

`}`

Something important happens in the `initBehavior` method:

*   All Java fields that are also input fields in the `UserFormWindow` Class are linked to the Java fields in the `Student` Class (with the `bindIntanceFields` invocation).
*   All the values in the Java fields of the User Class are set in the corresponding input fields in the `UserFormWindow` Class (with the `readBean`).

    `private void initBehavior(User user) {`

    `BeanValidationBinder<User> binder = new BeanValidationBinder<>(User.Class);`

    `binder.bindInstanceFields(this);`

    `binder.readBean(user);`

    `cancel.addClickListener(e -> close());`

    `save.addClickListener(e -> {`

    `try {`

    `binder.validate();`

    `binder.writeBean(user);`

    `repo.registrar(user);`

    `close();`

    `refresh();`

    `Notification.show(“User saved”);`

    `} catch (ValidationException ex) {`

    `Notification.show(“Please fix the errors and try again”);`

    `}`

    `});`

    `}`

    `}`

    When you hear the cancel button, just call `Window.close()` (legacy). Listening on the save button calls `writeBean` to write the values in the input fields on the `User` instance:

    `cancel.addClickListener(e -> close());`

    **Note: The writeBean launches a ValidationException. Therefore, there are no validations at this time. Adding the JavaBean Validation restrictions we have in the User Class is as simple as changing the Binder implementation:**

    `BeanValidationBinder<User> binder = new BeanValidationBinder<>(User.Class);`

# [Implement the Delete operation](#toc.xhtml_s160a)

Let's implement the CRUD operation off the delete button, using a different approach. Instead of simply adding a single button for the operation, we will add a delete button in each row of the GRID. The easiest way to add a UI component within a GRID is by using the `addComponentColumn` method:

`private Class RemoveDialog extends Dialog {`

`private Button cancel = new Button("Cancel");`

`private Button delete = new Button("Delete", VaadinIcon.TRASH.create());`

`public RemoveDialog(User user) {`

`initLayout(user);`

`initBehavior(user);`

`}`

We create the `initLayout()` method where the whole part of the page design is configured, adding the column names to configure the Grid using the `setColumns` method to display the `idUser`, `firstName`, and `lastName` properties. Finally, the dimension and position:

`private void initLayout(User user) {`

`Span span = new Span("Do you really want to delete the user " + user.getFirstName() + " " + user.getLastName() + "?");`

`delete.getElement().setAttribute("theme", "error");`

`HorizontalLayout buttons = new HorizontalLayout(cancel, delete);`

`VerticalLayout layout = new VerticalLayout(new H2("Confirm"), span, buttons);`

`layout.setAlignSelf(FlexComponent.Alignment.END, buttons);`

`add(layout);`

`}`

In the `initBehavior()` method we will create the CRUD operations to create and update just by clicking on the button:

`private void initBehavior(User user) {`

`cancel.addClickListener(e -> close());`

`delete.addClickListener(e -> {`

`repo.eliminar(user.getIdUser());`

`refresh();`

`close();`

`});`

`}`

`}`

# [UserList Class constructor](#toc.xhtml_s161a)

`public userList(IUserService repo, IRoleService role) {`

`this.repo = repo;`

`this.role = role;`

`initLayout();`

`initBehavior();`

`refresh();`

`}`

`private void initLayout() {`

`HorizontalLayout header = new HorizontalLayout(refresh, add, edit);`

`grid.setColumns("firstName", "lastName", "contactEmail", "mainRole");`

`grid.addComponentColumn(user -> new Button("Delete", e -> deleteClicked(user)));`

`grid.setSizeFull();`

`getContent().add(header, grid);`

`getContent().expand(grid);`

`getContent().setSizeFull();`

`getContent().setMargin(false);`

`getContent().setPadding(false);`

`}`

`private void initBehavior() {`

`grid.asSingleSelect().addValueChangeListener(e -> updateHeader());`

`refresh.addClickListener(e -> refresh());`

`add.addClickListener(e -> showAddDialog());`

`edit.addClickListener(e -> showEditDialog());`

`}`

`In the refresh() method as its name indicates is to refresh the grid:`

`public void refresh() {`

`grid.setItems(repo.listar());`

`updateHeader();`

`}`

`private void deleteClicked(User user) {`

`showRemoveDialog(user);`

`refresh();`

`}`

We create a new `updateHeader` method we configure to enable or disable the edit button depending on the selection state in the Grid. It makes sense to have the edit button enabled only when there is a row selected. We need to call this method when we update the list and when the value selected in the Grid changes:

`private void updateHeader() {`

`boolean selected = !grid.asSingleSelect().isEmpty();`

`edit.setEnabled(selected);`

`}`

`private void showAddDialog() {`

`UserFormDialog dialog = new UserFormDialog("New User", new User());`

`dialog.open();`

`}`

`private void showEditDialog() {`

`UserFormDialog dialog = new UserFormDialog("Update User", grid.asSingleSelect().getValue());`

`dialog.open();`

`}`

`private void showRemoveDialog(User user) {`

`RemoveDialog dialog = new RemoveDialog(user);`

`dialog.open();`

`}`

# [RoleList Class](#toc.xhtml_s162a)

`import com.book.bpb.backend.dao.RoleManagement;`

`import com.book.bpb.backend.entity.Role;`

`import com.mongodb.client.result.DeleteResult;`

`import javax.ejb.Stateless;`

`import javax.inject.Inject;`

`import javax.ws.rs.*;`

`import javax.ws.rs.core.MediaType;`

`import javax.ws.rs.core.Response;`

`import java.util.List;`

`@Stateless`

`@Path("roles")`

`@Produces({MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON})`

`@Consumes({MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON})`

`public class RoleResource {`

`@Inject`

`RoleManagement roleManagement;`

`@GET`

`public List<Role> findAll() {`

`return this.roleManagement.findAllRole();`

`}`

`@GET`

`@Path("{name}")`

`public Role findByName(@PathParam("name") String name) {`

`return this.roleManagement.findBYName(name);`

`}`

`@POST`

`public void createRole(Role role) {`

`this.roleManagement.createRole(role);`

`}`

`@PUT`

`@Path("{name}")`

`public void updateAccount(@PathParam("name") String name, Role role) {`

`this.roleManagement.updateRoleByName(name, role);`

`}`

`@DELETE`

`@Path("{name}")`

`public Response deleteRole(@PathParam("name") String name) {`

`DeleteResult result = this.roleManagement.removeByName(name);`

`System.out.println(result.getDeletedCount());`

`return Response.ok().build();`

`}`

`}`

# [Running with PWA](#toc.xhtml_s164a)

As mentioned in the previous chapter on **Progressive Web Application (PWA)** which is application software, which combines web pages and native mobile applications. Offering a similar experience to the native one, therefore Vaadin provides a very easy configuration of a PWA application, it is just adding a `@PWA` annotation to the main Class of the project, in this case in the `MainView` Class, as you can see in the following code:

`@PWA(name = "Project Base for Vaadin Flow with CDI", shortName = "Project Base")`

This will provide an `offline.html` page, therefore it can look like traditional desktop applications, or like modern native mobile applications by combining the features of modern browsers.

When running the application you can see in the bar at the bottom of the page, where it is indicated that it is a PWA application, as shown in the following screenshot:

![](images/8-16.jpg)

**Figure 8.16**

By pressing click the application is installed on a mobile device, tablet, or desktop.

# [Responsive Design](#toc.xhtml_s165a)

As you can see in the following screenshot, we are going to simulate the application from a mobile or desktop device. Vaadin Responsive is an extension that can be added to any component or UI. It is a web design approach aimed at creating sites that provide an optimal, easy to read and navigate viewing experience with minimal resizing and scrolling on a wide range of devices, also from desktop computer monitors to mobile phones.

# [Objective](#toc.xhtml_s171a)

Learn some basic concepts of Eclipse Krazo and how to implement security using IdentityStore and JWT.

# [Introduction to Eclipse Krazo](#toc.xhtml_s172a)

The inclusion of an action-oriented **MVC (Model View Controller)** framework on the Java EE / Jakarta EE platform has been expected for years. The first attempt was made within the Java EE 8 platform through the Ozark project (JSR Specification 371), which can be found at the following link **[https://jcp.org/en/jsr/detail?id=371](https://jcp.org/en/jsr/detail?id=371)**.

This specification was excluded from the Java EE 8 platform, so it was thought that it would not be further developed. This project is currently under the umbrella of the Eclipse Foundation and its name was changed to Eclipse Krazo to avoid the name being associated with copyright issues. The specification is available on the site **[https://www.mvc-spec.org/news/2019/10/28/eclipse-krazo-beta1.html](https://www.mvc-spec.org/news/2019/10/28/eclipse-krazo-beta1.html)**.

Java Web frameworks are divided into two categories:

*   Based on components such as Java Server Faces
*   Based on MVC actions.

Component-oriented frameworks handle HTTP requests in the framework so little interaction is required in the application code. With action-oriented frameworks, all HTTP requests are routed to the application and it is the responsibility of the developer to make the respective implementations in the controller.

Eclipse Krazo is built on top of JAX-RS API and supports Jakarta EE platform APIs such as CDI and Bean Validation. An example of Eclipse Krazo with JNoSQL is shown in *[Chapter 5: Exploring Eclipse MicroProfile](#c05.xhtml)*. In this chapter, we will focus on general aspects.

Now a project called `krazosguide` will be created using the maven archetype. Enter the console and execute the command:

`mvn archetype:generate -DarchetypeGroupId=de.erdlet.archetypes -DarchetypeArtifactId=jakartaee8-mvc-archetype -DarchetypeVersion=1.0.1-Beta1 -DgroupId=com.bpb.book -DartifactId=krazosguide`

The archetype generates several components of the application, the view that will be the `hello.jsp` file, the main class `App.java`, the controller called `AppController.java`, which will be described in the following sections:

![](images/9-1.jpg)

**Figure 9.1**

The `App.java` class defines the `ApplicationPath ("/mvc")` as shown in the code below.

`@ApplicationPath("/mvc")`

`public class App extends Application {`

`}`

`AppController.java` has the `@Controller` annotation and the `sayHello`() method has the `@GET` annotation that will be used as a path by the client through /`mvc/app` where the `hello.jsp` page will be invoked. Below is the code for `AppController.java:`

`@Path("app")`

`@Controller`

`public class AppController {`

`@GET`

`public String sayHello() {`

`return "hello.jsp";`

`}`

`}`

The `hello.jsp` represents the view of the application and displays a `Hello MVC` message. As you can see in the following code:

`<%@ page contentType="text/html;charset=UTF-8" language="java" %>`

`<html lang="en">`

`<head>`

`<meta charset="UTF-8">`

`<title>Hello!</title>`

`</head>`

`<body>`

`<h1>Hello MVC!</h1>`

`</body>`

`</html>`

When you run the application and enter the web browser you will get a view like the one shown in the image below. `http://localhost:8080/krazosguide/mvc/app:`

![](images/9-2.jpg)

**Figure 9.2**

# [MVCContext](#toc.xhtml_s173a)

MvcContext is defined by the specification as an object that allows us to access the application's configuration, security, and paths. It is used in controllers by injection and in views through Expression Language (EL). For example

`<form action="${mvc.basePath}/mvc/update" method="post">`

# [Controller](#toc.xhtml_s174a)

A controller is a java class to which we add the annotation `@Controller` which is a JAX-RS API decorator. Remember that the `Controller` according to the MVC pattern is an interface that allows processing the business logic and the requests that occur between the model and the view.

The types of return values that are defined in the Controller's methods are:

*   `String`: This is interpreted as a Path.
*   `void`: used in conjunction with the @View decorator.
*   `Response`: It is a JAX-RS style Response.
*   `Viewable`: Includes references to viewengine models or objects.

`Models`

Models allow us to pass data to the view through a map. For example,

`@Inject`

`private Models models;`

`@GET`

`public String sayHello() {`

`models.put("message","Welcome !");`

`return "hello.jsp";`

`}`

# [CDI](#toc.xhtml_s175a)

Eclipse Krazo supports the use of the CDI implementation through the `@Named` annotation that defines the beans and models which is an interface that defines a map between the view and the controller. These are defined as `@RequestScoped`.

Proceed to create the `Greeting.java` class as shown in the code below:

`@Named("greeting")`

`@RequestScoped`

`public class Greeting {`

`private String message;`

`public String getMessage() {`

`return message;`

`}`

`public void setMessage(String message) {`

`this.message = message;`

`}`

`}`

Then we inject it into the `AppController.java` class and create the `Cdi()` method that assigns the `Welcome from CDI message:`

`@Inject`

`Greeting greeting;`

`@GET`

`@Path("cdi")`

`public String Cdi() {`

`greeting.setMessage("Welcome from CDI");`

`return "hello.jsp";`

`}`

# [View](#toc.xhtml_s176a)

It is responsible for the display of data, at the time of writing this book Eclipse Krazo supports 12 view engines:

*   JSP
*   Facelets
*   Asciidoc
*   Freemarker
*   Handlebars
*   Jade
*   Jetbrick
*   JSR-223
*   Mustache
*   Pebble
*   Stringtemplate
*   Thymeleaf
*   Velocity
*   Jtwig

To show the data assigned through Models and CDI we edit the page `hello.jsp` and add the following lines of code:

`<h2>${message}</h2>`

`<h3>${greeting.message}</h3>`

The `hello.jsp` page would look similar to the following:

`<%@ page contentType="text/html;charset=UTF-8" language="java" %>`

`<html lang="en">`

`<head>`

`<meta charset="UTF-8">`

`<title>Hello!</title>`

`</head>`

`<body>`

`<h1>Hello MVC!</h1>`

`<h2>${message}</h2>`

`<h3>${greeting.message}</h3>`

`</body>`

`</html>`

Below is the complete code of the `AppController` class with the implementations of Models, CDI, `etc`:

`@Path("app")`

`@Controller`

`public class AppController {`

`@Inject`

`Models models;`

`@Inject`

`Greeting greeting;`

`@GET`

`public String sayHello() {`

`return "hello.jsp";`

`}`

`@GET`

`@Path("view")`

`@View("hello.jsp")`

`public void view() {`

`this.models.put("message", "Welcome from view");`

`}`

`@GET`

`@Path("response")`

`public Response response() {`

`this.models.put("message", "Welcome from response");`

`return Response.status(Response.Status.OK)`

`.entity("hello.jsp")`

`.build();`

`}`

`@GET`

`@Path("viewable")`

`public Viewable viewAble() {`

`this.models.put("message", "Welcome from viewable");`

`return new Viewable("hello.jsp");`

`}`

`@GET`

`@Path("cdi")`

`public String Cdi() {`

`greeting.setMessage("Welcome from CDI");`

`return "hello.jsp";`

`}`

`}`

`GET /krazosguide/mvc/app/view`

`GET /krazosguide/mvc/app/viewable`

`GET /krazosguide/mvc/application.wadl`

# [DataBinding](#toc.xhtml_s177a)

The JAX-RS `@FormParm` annotations offer the possibility of binding to the objects defined in the views.

In the code segment below is a simple example:

`@Controller`

`@Path("form")`

`public class PersonController {`

`@MvcBinding`

`@FormParam("id")`

`@Min(35)`

`private String name;`

`}`

# Validation

Data validations can be simplified using `BindingResult`, which we inject into the controller, and make use of the `isFailed()` method:

`@Inject`

`private BindingResult bindingResult;`

`@POST`

`@Path("update")`

`public String update(@Valid @BeanParam Person person) {`

`if (bindingResult.isFailed()) {`

`this.models.put("message", "Error");`

`return "update";`

`}`

`repository.save(person);`

`this.models.put("message", "Update");`

`return "redirect:mvc/update";`

`}`

# [Security](#toc.xhtml_s178a)

To establish restrictions on security some protections are implemented for example for **cross-site request forgery (CSRF)** we use in the JSP pages a hidden field that is sent with the form and allows us to verify the token:

`<input type="hidden" name="${mvc.csrf.name}" value="${mvc.csrf.token}"/>`

Validation can be applied at the method level in the controllers using `@CsrfProtected`. To prevent **cross-site scripting (XSS)** attacks, `javax.mvc.security.encoders` is used similarly through the `MvcContext` object.

# [Jakarta EE](#toc.xhtml_s179a)

The security of applications and microservices is a crucial issue to ensure the integrity of the operations performed.

The Jakarta Security specification is the equivalent of the specification defined by the JSR-375 Java EE Security API. The base document for the specification can be found at **[https://projects.eclipse.org/projects/ee4j.es/governance](https://projects.eclipse.org/projects/ee4j.es/governance)**. Some concepts that define the specification:

*   **Authentication Mechanism**: This is the mechanism by which the server interacts with the caller to obtain the credentials and invoke the identity store to verify the identity.
*   **Caller, Caller Principal**: It is the user who requests the application. The `MainCaller` is the object that represents the user.
*   **Identity Store**: It is a component that can access the specified security of the application such as users, groups, roles, and permissions. An identity store can be through Data Access Object, LDAP directory, file system, etc.

`IdentityStore` is defined by the specification as an abstraction of an identity store which is a database or directory with the information of the identities of the users who are application callers. It can be used for implementations with `HttpAuthenticationMechanism` and other types of authentication mechanisms

The `IdentityStoreHandler` interface allows you to invoke an Identity Store and validate a user's credentials.

It has two methods:

*   `validate(Credential):` Validates the credential and returns information about the user.
*   `getCallerGroups(CredentialValidationResult):` It returns all the groups associated with the caller.

Create a project using NetBeans IDE where a microservice that will return a list of countries will be executed in PayaraMicro. (You can use another server that supports Microprofile). The roles we will use our web, and mobile which will be specified in the web.xml file. The web.xml file with the security restrictions set for the example:

`<security-constraint>`

`<web-resource-collection>`

`<web-resource-name>Application pages</web-resource-name>`

`<url-pattern>/faces/pages/*</url-pattern>`

`</web-resource-collection>`

`<auth-constraint>`

`<role-name>web</role-name>`

`<role-name>mobile</role-name>`

`</auth-constraint>`

`</security-constraint>`

`<security-role>`

`<role-name>web</role-name>`

`</security-role>`

`<security-role>`

`<role-name>mobile</role-name>`

`</security-role>`

In the `JAXRSConfiguration` class, we establish the permissions for the web and mobile roles through `@DeclaredRoles`. Ensuring that only users who have them can have access. The following code of `JAXRSConfiguration.java:`

`@ApplicationScoped`

`@ApplicationPath("resources")`

`@DeclareRoles({"web", "mobile"})`

`@BasicAuthenticationMechanismDefinition(realmName = "jnosqlrealm")`

`public class JAXRSConfiguration extends Application {`

`@Override`

`public Set<Class<?>> getClasses() {`

`Set<Class<?>> resources = new java.util.HashSet<>();`

`resources.add(com.bpb.jakarta.nosql.securityidentitystore.resources.CountryResources.class);`

`return resources;`

`}`

`}`

The entity Country represents the information of the countries:

`public Class Country {`

`private String id;`

`private String name;`

`//get/set`

`}`

The `CountryResources` class contains the `getCountry()` method that will return the list of some countries. Using `@RolesAllowed({"web"})` restricts access only to users with web roles:

`@Stateless`

`@Path("/country")`

`public class CountryResources {`

`@GET`

`@Produces(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)`

`@RolesAllowed({"web"})`

`public List<Country> getCountry() {`

`List<Country> country = new ArrayList<Country>();`

`country.add(new Country("pa", "Panama"));`

`country.add(new Country("co", "Colombia"));`

`country.add(new Country("br", "Brasil"));`

`return country;`

`}`

`}`

The `CountrySecurity` class that implements `IdentityStore`, contains the `CredentialValidationResult` validate method (`UsernamePasswordCredential` `usernamePasswordCredential`), in which the validation of the user credentials (username, password) that are provided by the caller is performed. These credentials to be verified can be stored in a repository, files, databases, etc.

The method will return the user and the roles it has, and these will be used to validate the accesses in the classes that implement `@DeclareRoles({"web", "mobile"}), and @RolesAllowed({"web"}).`

Code of the `CountrySecurity` class where the validation of credentials is shown, is similar to this one, remember that it is only as an example that the data for the user and password are placed in your application, use the sources of the `IdentityStore`:

`@ApplicationScoped`

`public class ContrySecurity implements IdentityStore {`

`public CredentialValidationResult validate(UsernamePasswordCredential usernamePasswordCredential) {`

`if (usernamePasswordCredential.compareTo("userjnosql", "userjnosql")) {`

`return new CredentialValidationResult("userjnosql", new HashSet<>(asList("web", "mobile")));`

`}`

`return INVALID_RESULT;`

`}`

`}`

Run the application from the IDE or via docker or convert it to an Uber jar depending on your considerations and enter `http://localhost:8080/resources/country` in the web browser, a dialogue is displayed where the user's credentials are requested:

![](images/figure_9.5.jpg)

**Figure 9.5**

Enter the credentials for the username: `userjnosql` and password: `userjnosql`. Remember that they are used only in an educational way, never use passwords similar to the username, and do not expose them in your code directly. It generates the list of countries in JSON format:

![](images/9-5.jpg)

**Figure 9.6**

Postman is an excellent tool to interact with microservices. To establish the authentication you must set the parameters for the `BasicAuth` as are the username and password.

The following figure shows how microservices are consumed through `IdentityStore` authentication:

![](images/9-6.jpg)

**Figure 9.7**

# [Eclipse MicroProfile Interoperable JWT RBAC](#toc.xhtml_s180a)

It is a Microprofile specification that establishes how to implement **JSON Web Token (JWT)** with microservices. It uses **OpenID Connect (OIDC)** and **JWT (JSON Web Tokens)** for role-based access control (RBAC). The specification can be consulted at the following link:

**[https://github.com/eclipse/microprofile-jwt-auth/releases/download/1.1/microprofile-jwt-auth-spec-1.1.pdf](https://github.com/eclipse/microprofile-jwt-auth/releases/download/1.1/microprofile-jwt-auth-spec-1.1.pdf)**

The specification defines some characteristics such as:

*   RESTFUL Security is the architecture to guarantee the security of microservices.
*   Microprofile Microprofile is built on standard APIs such as JAX-RS, CDI, and JSON-P.
*   One of the ways to spread security from customers to services and from services to services is through the use of tokens.
*   The services do not store the status of the client.
*   Services can identify the caller by introspection on the token.

The specification also defines Token-Based Authentication as a mechanism to enable systems to authenticate, authorize, and verify token-based identities. It also defines the entities involved:

*   **Issuer**: Responsible for issuing the token.
*   **Client**: This is the application for which the token was issued. It also acts as an intermediary between a subject and the target service.
*   **Subject**: Entity to which the token information refers.
*   **Resources Server**: Application that consumes the token.

# [JSON Web Tokens (JWT)](#toc.xhtml_s181a)

The RFC-7519 standard defines JSON Web Tokens as a mechanism for securely representing claims between two parties. A JWT consists of:

*   **Header**: It contains metadata that identifies the type of algorithm used to sign the token, the type of token.
*   **Claims**: Information stored on the token.
*   **Signature**: It's the firm.

For the examples developed below, the Payara Server will be used. You can use your preferred server. The basic steps that will be performed are:

*   Create a Realm on Payara Server
*   Generate two users for testing
*   Generate applications using `mplemonbuilder`
*   Deploy both applications
*   Testing from console and PostMan.

# [MP Lemon Builder](#toc.xhtml_s183a)

MP Lemon Builder is a tool developed by Diego Enrique Silva Límaco `(@apuntesdejava)` that allows us to generate projects with JWT authentication for Microprofile. It is very easy to use and automates the whole process of generating a project that generates the token and the application that consumes it.

The first thing to do is to download the .jar file from the following site:

**[https://github.com/apuntesdejava/mp-lemon-builder](https://github.com/apuntesdejava/mp-lemon-builder)**

Once downloaded, it is executed from the console with the --help parameter to obtain help on the different parameters that must be indicated to generate the projects:

**`java -jar mplemonbuilder.jar --help`**

To create a project run `mplemonbuilder` with the following parameters:

`java -jar mplemonbuilder.jar --group-id=com.jnosqlmvc --project-name=jnosqlmvc-jwt --realm-name=jnosql-realm --header-key=my-header-key --issuer=http://www.jnosql.org/spec/ --roles=web,mobile`

Provide the version number, web application name, application name to generate `jwt` and the path where the projects are generated will be requested. In the following image, we see the MP Lemon Builder console:

![](images/9-13.jpg)

**Figure 9.14**

The directory `jnosqlmvc-jwt` is created and within it the two projects: `jnosqljwt` and `jnosqlweb`.

# [Analyze the project: jnosqljwt](#toc.xhtml_s184a)

Open the project from our favorite editor, the example uses NetBeans IDE:

![](images/9-14.jpg)

**Figure 9.15**

This project has two classes `CypherService.java` that is used to create the token. The following is the code for the class `CypherService.java:`

`@javax.enterprise.context.ApplicationScoped`

`public class CypherService {`

`private static final Logger LOG = Logger.getLogger(CypherService.class.getName());`

`private String issuer;`

`private int tokenValid;`

`private String audience;`

`private String headerKey;`

`private final Collection<String> roles = new LinkedHashSet<>();`

`@javax.annotation.PostConstruct`

`public void init() {`

`Security.addProvider(new BouncyCastleProvider());`

`LOG.info("Credentials loaded");`

`}`

`public String createToken(PrivateKey key, String username, Collection<String> groups) {`

`LocalDateTime now = LocalDateTime.now();`

`LocalDateTime expiration = now.plus(tokenValid, ChronoUnit.SECONDS);`

`return Jwts.builder()`

`.setHeaderParam("typ", "JWT")`

`.setIssuedAt(Date.from(now.atZone(ZoneId.systemDefault())`

`.toInstant()))`

`.setExpiration(Date.from(expiration.atZone(ZoneId.systemDefault())`

`.toInstant()))`

`.signWith(SignatureAlgorithm.RS256, key)`

`.setSubject(username)`

`.setAudience(audience)`

`.setHeaderParam("kid", headerKey)`

`.claim("groups", groups)`

`.setId(UUID.randomUUID().toString())`

`.setIssuer(issuer).compact();`

`}`

`public PrivateKey readPrivateKey() throws IOException, NoSuchAlgorithmException, InvalidKeySpecException {`

`try (InputStream is = CypherService.class.getResourceAsStream("/jwt-config.json")) {`

`JsonObject json = Json.createReader(is).readObject();`

`String keyString = json.getString("private-key");`

`this.issuer = json.getString("issuer");`

`this.audience = json.getString("audience", "audience");`

`json.getJsonArray("roles").stream()`

`.map((v) -> (JsonString) v).forEachOrdered((s)`

`-> roles.add(s.getString()));`

`this.tokenValid = json.getInt("token-valid");`

`this.headerKey = json.getString("header-key");`

`byte[] encoded = Base64.getDecoder().decode(keyString);`

`return KeyFactory.getInstance("RSA").generatePrivate(new PKCS8EncodedKeySpec(encoded));`

`}`

`}`

`public Collection<String> getRoles() {`

`return roles;`

`}`

`}`

`TokenProviderResource.java` extends JAX-RS Application and the path /`auth` is indicated, it also has the Response `login`() method that receives the user's credentials and invokes `CypherService` to generate the token and return it to the client.

Next, code from the TokenProviderResource.java class:

`@ApplicationScoped`

`@Path("/auth")`

`@ApplicationPath("/")`

`public class TokenProviderResource extends Application {`

`private static final Logger LOG = Logger.getLogger(TokenProviderResource.class.getName());`

`@Inject`

`private CypherService cypherService;`

`private PrivateKey key;`

`@PostConstruct`

`public void init() {`

`try {`

`key = cypherService.readPrivateKey();`

`} catch (IOException | NoSuchAlgorithmException | InvalidKeySpecException ex) {`

`LOG.log(Level.SEVERE, null, ex);`

`}`

`}`

`@POST`

`@Produces(MediaType.TEXT_PLAIN)`

`@Consumes(MediaType.APPLICATION_FORM_URLENCODED)`

`public Response login(`

`@FormParam("username") String username,`

`@FormParam("password") String password,`

`@Context HttpServletRequest request) {`

`if (key == null) {`

`LOG.severe("Credentials not loaded");`

`return Response.status(Response.Status.INTERNAL_SERVER_ERROR).build();`

`}`

`List<String> target = new ArrayList<>();`

`try {`

`request.login(username, password);`

`cypherService.getRoles()`

`.stream()`

`.filter((role) -> (request.isUserInRole(role)))`

`.forEachOrdered((role)`

`-> target.add(role)`

`);`

`} catch (ServletException ex) {`

`LOG.log(Level.SEVERE, ex.getMessage(), ex);`

`return Response.status(Response.Status.UNAUTHORIZED)`

`.build();`

`}`

`String token = cypherService.createToken(key, username, target);`

`return Response.status(Response.Status.OK)`

`.header(AUTHORIZATION, "Bearer ".concat(token))`

`.entity(token)`

`.build();`

`}`

`}`

The `jwt-config.json` file contains information about the token such as issuer, token-valid. `header`-key. roles, public-key, private-key.

`{`

`"issuer": "http://www.jnosql.org/spec/",`

`"token-valid": 1000000,`

`"header-key": "my-header-key",`

`"roles": ["web","mobile"],`

`"public-key": "",`

`"pivate-key": ""`

`}`

The file `payara-web.xml c`ontains the roles defined in the Payara realm configuration:

`<?xml version="1.0" encoding="UTF-8"?><!DOCTYPE payara-web-app PUBLIC "-//Payara.fish//DTD Payara Server 4 Servlet 3.0//EN" "https://docs.payara.fish/schemas/payara-web-app_4.dtd">`

`<payara-web-app>`

`<security-role-mapping>`

`<role-name>web</role-name>`

`<group-name>web</group-name>`

`</security-role-mapping>`

`<security-role-mapping>`

`<role-name>mobile</role-name>`

`<group-name>mobile</group-name>`

`</security-role-mapping>`

`</payara-web-app>`

Build the project from NetBeans, right-click on the project and build or via console, enter the project directory `jnosqljwt` and generate the application's .war file using the

**`mvn clean install`**

Now make the deployed in Payara Server for it we go to the **`Applications`** section, click on **`Deploy`**, select the file .**`war`** and press the **`OK`** button:

**`sudo apt-get install httpie`**

**`http --form :8080/jnosqljwt/auth username=userjnosql password=userjnosql`**

# [Project jnosqlweb](#toc.xhtml_s185a)

Open the project from NetBeans IDE or your favorite editor as shown in the screenshot below:

![](images/9-18.jpg)

**Figure 9.19**

The project contains following files:

*   `JAXRSConfiguration.java:` Define the path for the microservices and establish that the authentication would be via MP-JWT. The MicroProfile JWT RBAC specification defines the `LoginConFig` notation to provide the same information as the `login-config e`lement of the `web.xml` file, this requires a class that extends from JAX-RS Application:

    `@ApplicationPath("resources")`

    `@LoginConfig(authMethod = "MP-JWT")`

    `@DeclareRoles({"web","mobile"})`

    `public class JAXRSConfiguration extends Application {`

    `}`

*   The specification defines:
    *   `java.security.Main` identifies the subject of the JWT (`Main`).
    *   An MP-JWT implementation to support caller injection authenticated as a `JsonWebToken` and requires `@RequestScoped` scope.

The `PingResource.java` class is a microservice that can be accessed via path /`ping`. The path `all` has the annotation `@PermitAll` allows access to all roles and the path secure only to users with roles `{"web", "mobile"}`. The following code is the c lass `PingResource.java`:

`@Path("ping")`

`@Produces(TEXT_PLAIN)`

`public class PingResource {`

`@Inject`

`private Principal principal;`

`@Inject`

`private JsonWebToken jwt;`

`@GET`

`@PermitAll`

`@Path("all")`

`public String ping() {`

`return principal.getName() + jwt.getGroups();`

`}`

`@GET`

`@RolesAllowed({"web","mobile"})`

`@Path("secure")`

`public String pingSecure() {`

`return principal.getName() + jwt.getGroups();`

`}`

`}`

Then generate the `.war` file of the application from the IDE or via console with the command:

**`cd jnosqlweb/`**

**`mvn clean install`**

Perform the process of deploying the application on Payara Server, so that you have both applications running:

![](images/9-20.jpg)

**Figure 9.20**

Execute from console to access the path all using following command:

**`http :8080/jnosqlweb/resources/ping/all`**

![](images/9-21.jpg)

**Figure 9.21**

Now try to access secure using the following command:

**http :8080/jnosqlweb/resources/ping/secure**

As you can see in the image, there is no authorized access. You have to indicate the token that was generated for that user:

![](images/9-22.jpg)

**Figure 9.22**

Use the previous token created for `userjnosql` or create one for the other `userjmoordb`. Using the following command:

**http --form :8080/jnosqljwt/auth username=userjmoordb password=userjmoordb**

Copy the generated token:

![](images/9-23.jpg)

**Figure 9.23**

Run it again by copying the token at the end and adding quotes at the beginning and end. As shown in the figure below:

![](images/9-24.jpg)

**Figure 9.24**

An output similar to this one is generated showing the user and the role that token has:

`HTTP/1.1 200 OK`

`Server: Payara Server 5.194 #badassfish`

# [Conclusion](#toc.xhtml_s186a)

This chapter shows some features of Eclipse Krazo and authentication mechanisms using the `IdentityStore` implementation of Microprofile JWT. We include the use of the MP Lemon Builder application that automates the whole process of token creation and authentication. These are essential features when building web applications and especially microservices.

In the next chapter, we will observe how to perform application testing using tools optimized for use with containers such as Docker.

# [CHAPTER 10](#toc.xhtml_c10)

# [Testing and Continuous Integration](#toc.xhtml_c10)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml_s188a)

Nowadays the quality of application delivery needs testing processes. In the context of Cloud applications and working with the team, it is essential to have methodologies and tools that guarantee the quality of the code of our applications.

*   TestContainers

*   MicroShed

*   Continuous Integration and Continuous Delivery
