# [Objective](#toc.xhtml_s190a)

The objective of this chapters to use tools to test applications by using containers.

# [Introduction to Test](#toc.xhtml_s191a)

In this chapter, we will show some tools that allow developers to test their applications shipped in a container. Several tools exist to automate the application test in the world of Java: JUnit, Mockito, TestNG, Arquillian, and Selenium among others.

Dockers and Kubernetes have undoubtedly offered facilities for the development of microservices and other types of applications. Additionally, containers can be used to perform tests in a more simplified way. The next lines will show you some of them and how to use them.

Continuos Integration, Continuos Delivery, Containers, Devops are terms that are currently used by development teams to achieve efficiency in the construction of applications and in the delivery of products.

In this book we do not delve into these technologies, we focus on showing through simple examples some solutions that can be useful for development teams.

# [TestContaniers](#toc.xhtml_s192a)

Testcontainers is a Java library that supports JUnit tests, providing lightweight, throwaway instances of common databases, Selenium web browsers, or anything else that can run in a Docker container. You can find more information about it from here: **[https://www.testcontainers.org/](https://www.testcontainers.org/)**

# [Testing Eclipse Krazo](#toc.xhtml_s193a)

To test the view of our applications we can use Selenium. TestContainers offers us integrated support with containers that we will describe below.

# [WebDriverContainers](#toc.xhtml_s194a)

Testcontainers defines as one of its main functions the instantiation and management of containers that include web browsers, making it easy to test these containers.

Many of our applications need to test the view and interact with various components such as buttons, tables, forms, and know-how our application behaves. Among the test frameworks for Java application Selenium is an appropriate alternative to consider. You can check out on their website **[https://selenium.dev/](https://selenium.dev/)**.

Testcontainers offers support for Webdriver containers, which allow us to instantiate containers that include a web browser with projects like SeleniumHQ.

The main site **[https://www.testcontainers.org/modules/webdriver_containers/](https://www.testcontainers.org/modules/webdriver_containers/)** describes some benefits such as:

*   Compatibility with Selenium 2/Webdriver test.
*   The test container unit works with the Docker installation. You do not need to specify any web browser.
*   Browsers are always launched from a clean image.
*   Compatibility between the browser and Selenium is guaranteed.

# [Selenium](#toc.xhtml_s195a)

It provides a set of tools to automate the testing of web browsers, where the iteration of users with applications can be simulated.

Selenium provides us:

*   **Web Driver:** It's an API that allows us to control web browsers. Each web browser has its implementation of WebDriver.
*   **WebElement:** Represents a DOM tree element within the page. (labels, buttons, text boxes, etc.)

To locate the elements we can do it in several ways:

*   name
*   id
*   tag name
*   xpath
*   xss selector
*   class name

The following are the examples of element selector:

*   `driver.findElement(By.name("name"))`
*   `driver.findElement(By.id("name"))`
*   `driver.findElement(By.cssSelector("input[type='submit']")).click();`

# ChromeDriver

To run the tests we will need ChromeDriver installed on our computer. You can download it from **https://sites.google.com/a/chromium.org/chromedriver/**downloads.

Follow the steps below:

*   Unzip the `chromedriver_linux64 file.zip`
*   Copy the chromedriver file to the `usr/bin` directory
    *   `cp chromedriver /usr /bin`
*   Give you execution permissions
    *   `sudo chmod + x/usr/bin / chromedriver`
*   If you are using another browser please review the documentation for the required requirements.

As you can see we can access any element of the website, even emulate events on the buttons and other elements. The example is based on the project `krazosguide` developed in the previous chapter. Edit the `AppController.java` class and add the model resource the full code is shown below:

`@Path("app")`

`@Controller`

`public class AppController {`

`@Inject`

`Models models;`

`@Inject`

`Greeting greeting;`

`@GET`

`public String sayHello() {`

`return "hello.jsp";`

`}`

`@GET`

`@Path("view")`

`@View("hello.jsp")`

`public void view() {`

`this.models.put("message", "Welcome from view");`

`this.models.put("resource", "view");`

`}`

`@GET`

`@Path("response")`

`public Response response() {`

`this.models.put("message", "Welcome from response");`

`this.models.put("resource", "response");`

`return Response.status(Response.Status.OK)`

`.entity("hello.jsp")`

`.build();`

`}`

`@GET`

`@Path("viewable")`

`public Viewable viewable() {`

`this.models.put("message", "Welcome from viewable");`

`this.models.put("resource", "viewable");`

`return new Viewable("hello.jsp");`

`}`

`@GET`

`@Path("cdi")`

`public String cdi() {`

`this.models.put("message", "Welcome from CDI");`

`this.models.put("resource", "cdi");`

`greeting.setMessage("Welcome from CDI/Greeting");`

`return "hello.jsp";`

`}`

`}`

Modify the `hello.jsp` page and add an input element to display a text box on the website:

`<%@ page contentType="text/html;charset=UTF-8" language="java" %>`

`<html lang="en">`

`<head>`

`<title>Hello!</title>`

`</head>`

`<body>`

`<form>`

`<h2 id="message">${message}</h2>`

`<input name="textfield" size="45" type="text" value=${resource} />`

`</form>`

`</body>`

`</html>`

You want to verify that when you enter from the browser to the URL **[http://localhost:8080/krazosguide/mvc/app/view](http://localhost:8080/krazosguide/mvc/app/view)**, an output similar to the one shown in the following page is produced:

![](images/10-1.jpg)

**Figure 10.1**

Let's create a test where we can check the values of `<h2>` and the text box to make sure they show the expected results. Remember that the method is invoked `view()` and assigns the values to the `Models` that will be shown in the `hello.jsp` page:

`@GET`

`@Path("view")`

`@View("hello.jsp")`

`public void view() {`

`this.models.put("message", "Welcome from view");`

`this.models.put("resource", "view");`

`}`

With Google Chrome or Firefox web browsers we can install the Xpath Finder extension, which generates the `xpath` of the components:

With the active extension, we place ourselves on the text and it tells us the path of the element `<h2>` that would be `/html/body/h2`, as shown in the screenshot below:

![](images/10-2.jpg)

**Figure 10.2**

Edit the `pom.xml` file and add the following dependencies:

`<properties>`

`<testcontainers.version>1.12.4</testcontainers.version>`

`<selenium-remote-driver.version>3.141.59</selenium-remote-driver.version>`

`</properties>`

`<!--Test Containers -->`

`<dependency>`

`<groupId>org.testcontainers</groupId>`

`<artifactId>testcontainers</artifactId>`

`<version>${testcontainers.version}</version>`

`</dependency>`

`<dependency>`

`<groupId>org.testcontainers</groupId>`

`<artifactId>selenium</artifactId>`

`<version>${testcontainers.version}</version>`

`</dependency>`

`<dependency>`

`<groupId>org.seleniumhq.selenium</groupId>`

`<artifactId>selenium-remote-driver</artifactId>`

`<version>${selenium-remote-driver.version}</version>`

`</dependency>`

`<dependency>`

`<groupId>org.seleniumhq.selenium</groupId>`

`<artifactId>selenium-chrome-driver</artifactId>`

`<version>${selenium-remote-driver.version}</version>`

`</dependency>`

`<dependency>`

`<groupId>org.seleniumhq.selenium</groupId>`

`<artifactId>selenium-support</artifactId>`

`<version>3.141.59</version>`

`</dependency>`

`<dependency>`

`<groupId>org.slf4j</groupId>`

`<artifactId>slf4j-jdk14</artifactId>`

`<version>1.7.29</version>`

`</dependency>`

In the plugins section add the following lines of code:

`<plugin>`

`<groupId>org.apache.maven.plugins</groupId>`

`<artifactId>maven-surefire-plugin</artifactId>`

`<version>2.22.2</version>`

`</plugin>`

You will create the class for the test called `AppControllerTest` that will be used to validate the `hello.jsp page`. The components we will use are:

*   `BrowserWebDriverContainer`: It represents the web browser, in this case Google Chrome and Firefox are supported.
*   `RemoteWebDriver:` It implements `WebDriver` interfaces and allows you to `control` the browser.
*   `WebElement`: It represents html elements.

Below is the test code:

`public class AppControllerTest {`

`String url = "http://192.168.0.5:8080/krazosguide";`

`@Rule`

`public BrowserWebDriverContainer chrome = new BrowserWebDriverContainer()`

`.withCapabilities(new ChromeOptions());`

`@Test`

`public void viewTest() {`

`RemoteWebDriver driver = chrome.getWebDriver();`

`driver.get(url + "/mvc/app/view");`

`WebElement element = driver.findElement(By.name("textfield"));`

`String resource = element.getAttribute("value");`

`assertEquals("view1", resource);`

`driver.close();`

`}`

`@Test`

`public void cdiTest() {`

`RemoteWebDriver driver = chrome.getWebDriver();`

`driver.get(url + "/mvc/app/cdi");`

`WebElement element = driver.findElement(By.id("message"));`

`String message = element.getAttribute("innerHTML");`

`assertEquals("Welcome from CDI", message);`

`driver.close();`

`}`

`@Test`

`public void viewable() {`

`RemoteWebDriver driver = chrome.getWebDriver();`

`driver.get(url + "/mvc/app/viewable");`

`WebElement element = driver.findElement(By.id("message"));`

`//String heading = driver.findElement(By.xpath("/html/body/div/h1")).getText();`

`String message = element.getAttribute("innerHTML");`

`assertEquals("Welcome from viewable", message);`

`driver.close();`

`}`

`}`

The steps we will follow will be:

1.  Execute the Web project.
2.  Running the test from NetBeans IDE
3.  Select the `AppControllerTest` file `.java` and right-click and click the **`Test File`** option. The following screenshots shows the procedure for running the test.

![](images/10-3.jpg)

**Figure 10.3**

The following screenshots how the test is run on the specified containers:

![](images/10-4.jpg)

**Figure 10.4**

At the end of the execution an error message is sent, one of the tests failed. Specifically, `viewTest`() since the value of `view1` was expected and it returns a view. As can be seen in the figure below:

![](images/10-5.jpg)

**Figure 10.5**

A more detailed message like this is displayed:

`viewTest(com.bpb.book.AppControllerTest) Time elapsed: 10.097 s <<< FAILURE!`

`org.junit.ComparisonFailure: expected:<view[1]> but was:<view[]>`

`at com.bpb.book.AppControllerTest.viewTest(AppControllerTest.java:37)`

The following will be the results:

`Failures:`

`AppControllerTest.viewTest:37 expected:<view[1]> but was:<view[]>`

`Tests run: 3, Failures: 1, Errors: 0, Skipped: 0`

`------------------------------------------------------------------------`

`BUILD FAILURE`

What we are doing automatically is as if we open the browser and enter the address and get the message. The text box contains the value view and not view1 as indicated in the test, so this is the reason why the error is generated:

![](images/10-6.jpg)

**Figure 10.6**

We modified the test to indicate that we should return the value of view to the content in the text box. It would look like this:

`@Test`

`public void viewTest() {`

`RemoteWebDriver driver = chrome.getWebDriver();`

`driver.get(url + "/mvc/app/view");`

`WebElement element = driver.findElement(By.name("textfield"));`

`String resource = element.getAttribute("value");`

`assertEquals("view", resource);`

`driver.close();`

`}`

Run the test again and at the end you will get the following results:

`Tests run: 3, Failures: 0, Errors: 0, Skipped: 0, Time elapsed: 74.31 s - in com.bpb.book.AppControllerTest`

`Results:`

`Tests run: 3, Failures: 0, Errors: 0, Skipped: 0`

Since NetBeans IDE is displayed in green indicating that all tests were passed successfully:

![](images/10-7.jpg)

**Figure 10.7**

Run the tests through Maven using the command:

**`mvn test`**

In the Selenium portal there are complete examples of how to test other components, this is a small introduction to their use. For example, we can assign values to the input texts:

`WebElement element = driver.findElement(By.name("textfield"));`

`element.sendKeys("From test");`

To control the events of the elements of the page is always done in a way, for example an event click:

`WebElement button = driver.findElement(By.name("buttonHello"));`

`button.click();`

# [Testing a container](#toc.xhtml_s196a)

`TestContainer` API offers a class `GenericContainers` that helps to run a container without a special setup. An annotation `@ClassRule` ignites the annotated method before the test.

This method can be used to initialize the connection to NoSQL databases such as MongoDB, OrientDB, and many others. With `GenericContainers` it is possible to establish:

*   `withExposedPorts`: exposing ports;
*   `withEnv`: establish environment variables;
*   `withCommand`: allows you to execute commands;
*   `withLogConsumer`: register the API for logs.
*   `withNetwork`: allows you to customize the network.

It also offers us methods like:

*   `getMappedPort():` mapped port.
*   `getFirstMappedPort():` first port exposed.
*   `getContainerIpAddress():` IP address.
*   `execInContainer:` execute commands similar to docker exec.

In the following example, we use a MongoDB database and expose port 27017:

`@ClassRules`

`public GenericContainer container = new GenericContainer("mongodb:latest")`

`.withExposedPorts(27017,27017);`

`Integer firstMappedPort = container.getFirstMappedPort();`

You can find a further reference at the following link: **[https://www.testcontainers.org/features/creating_container](https://www.testcontainers.org/features/creating_container)**

# [MicroShed Testing](#toc.xhtml_s197a)

MicroShed is a useful tool for us to create and execute tests using Docker images in a simple and faster way. Its particularity is that it can help to test a ready for production container. MicroShed allows you to write and test Microprofile and Jakarta EE applications using containers.

It's been tested on:

*   OpenLiberty
*   Wildfly
*   Payara Micro
*   Apache TomEE

Microshed facilitates the creation of tests for endpoints created with JAX-RS. The annotations you can use to create the tests are:

*   `@MicroShedTest`: Creates an integration test, allows the injection of JAX-RS
*   `@Container`: Annotation looks for a Dockerfile in the repository and starts the container and the test will be run once the container is loaded. If the label on the image of the Docker container is not specified, you can indicate the specific adapter you want to use. You can use `ApplicationContainer` or through `MicroprofileApplication`, if the dependency of a container, for example, PayaraMicro, was specified.

    `@Container`

    `public static ApplicationContainer app = new ApplicationContainer()`

    `.withAppContextRoot("/myservice");`

    `or`

    `@Container`

    `public static MicroProfileApplication app = new MicroProfileApplication();`

*   @RESTClient: It allows to inject the Rest client and to invoke the HTTP requests.

# [Supported runtimes](#toc.xhtml_s198a)

*   Open Liberty

    **Maven dependency:**

    `<dependency>`

    `<groupId>org.microshed</groupId>`

    `<artifactId>microshed-testing-liberty</artifactId>`

    `<version>0.6.1.1</version>`

    `</dependency>`

    **Example Dockerfile:**

    **`FROM openliberty/open-liberty:full-java8-openj9-ubi`**

    **`COPY src/main/liberty/config /config/`**

    **`ADD build/libs/myservice.war /config/dropins`**

*   Payara Micro

    **Maven dependency:**

    `<dependency>`

    `<groupId>org.microshed</groupId>`

    `<artifactId>microshed-testing-payara-micro</artifactId>`

    `<version>0.6.1.1</version>`

    `</dependency>`

    **Example Dockerfile:**

    **`FROM payara/micro:5.193`**

    **`CMD ["--deploymentDir", "/opt/payara/deployments", "--noCluster"]`**

    **`ADD build/libs/myservice.war /opt/payara/deployments`**

*   Payara Server

    **Maven dependency:**

    `<dependency>`

    `<groupId>org.microshed</groupId>`

    `<artifactId>microshed-testing-payara-server</artifactId>`

    `<version>0.6.1.1</version>`

    `</dependency>`

    **Example Dockerfile:**

    **`FROM payara/server-full:5.193`**

    **`ADD target/myservice.war /opt/payara/deployments`**

*   Wildfly

    **Generic Maven dependency:**

    `<dependency>`

    `<groupId>org.microshed</groupId>`

    `<artifactId>microshed-testing-testcontainers</artifactId>`

    `<version>0.6.1.1</version>`

    `</dependency>`

    **Example Dockerfile:**

    **`FROM jboss/wildfly`**

    **`ADD build/libs/myservice.war /opt/jboss/wildfly/standalone/deployments/`**

# [Sharing containers](#toc.xhtml_s199a)

We usually perform multiple tests of the applications and to avoid overloading the containers at the beginning of each test, it can be avoided by using Sharing containers.

Use the `@SharedContainerConfig` notation that starts the container before the first test is run. As you can see in the next code segment:

`public class AppContainerConfig implements SharedContainerConfiguration {`

`@Container`

`public static ApplicationContainer app = new ApplicationContainer()`

`.withAppContextRoot("/openlibertytest");`

`}`

`@MicroShedTest`

`@SharedContainerConfig(AppContainerConfig.class)`

`public class TestPeople {`

`// …`

`}`

`@MicroShedTest`

`@SharedContainerConfig(AppContainerConfig.class)`

`public class TestCountry{`

`// …`

`}`

# [Example with OpenLiberty](#toc.xhtml_s200a)

We will create a sample of container tests using OpenLiberty. For that we need a set of requirements:

*   Docker
*   Apache Maven or Gradle
*   Apache NetBeans IDE or any other

    Create a Maven (in this example) or Gradle based application. From Apache NetBeans ignite the following steps: **`File | New Project | Categories,`** select **`Java With Maven`** and **`Web Application.`**

    Fill the wizard dialog with those information:

    *   **`Project Name:`** `openlibertytest`
    *   **`Group id`**`: com.bpb.jakarta.nosql`
    *   **`Version`**`: 1.0-SNAPSHOT`
    *   **`Package`**`: com.bpb.jakarta.nosql.openlibertytest`

    Do not provide any server, but for in dropbox **`JavaEE Version`** select **`JavaEE 8`** web and click **`Finish`**.

1.  Add dependencies for test. In the project you created, go into the `pom.xml` file and add Microshed and JUnit dependencies just below:

    `<dependency>`

    `<groupId>org.microshed</groupId>`

    `<artifactId>microshed-testing-testcontainers</artifactId>`

    `<version>0.6.1.1</version>`

    `<scope>test</scope>`

    `</dependency>`

    `<dependency>`

    `<groupId>org.junit.jupiter</groupId>`

    `<artifactId>junit-jupiter</artifactId>`

    `<version>5.4.2</version>`

    `<scope>test</scope>`

    `</dependency>`

2.  Configure the project to discover OpenLiberty. Create the `server.xml` file in the folder /`src/main/liberty/`config of your project and add the following content:

    `<server>`

    `<featureManager>`

    `<feature>jaxrs-2.1</feature>`

    `<feature>jsonb-1.0</feature>`

    `<feature>mpHealth-2.0</feature>`

    `<feature>mpConfig-1.3</feature>`

    `<feature>mpRestClient-1.1</feature>`

    `<feature>beanValidation-2.0</feature>`

    `<feature>cdi-2.0</feature>`

    `</featureManager>`

    `</server>`

3.  Create and configure the docker file. Creating the `DockerFile` from menu **`File | New File | DockerFile.`**

    Here the content of the DockerFile:

    **`FROM`** `openliberty/open-liberty:full-java8-openj9-ubi`

    **`COPY`** `src/main/liberty/config /config/`

    **`ADD`** `target/openlibertytest.war /config/dropins`

4.  Enrich the project with logs and execution plugin. Add additional dependencies in `pom.xml:`
    *   **For handling logs through SFL4J:**

        `<dependency>`

        `<groupId>org.slf4j</groupId>`

        `<artifactId>slf4j-log4j12</artifactId>`

        `<version>1.7.29</version>`

        `<scope>test</scope>`

        `</dependency>`

    *   **For OpenLiberty container test:**

        `<dependency>`

        `<groupId>org.microshed</groupId>`

        `<artifactId>microshed-testing-liberty</artifactId>`

        `<version>0.6.1.1</version>`

        `</dependency>`

Modify the build section by including the final name of the file after the build. The section `finalName` will look like this:

`<finalName>openlibertytest</finalName>`

In the section plugin, configure `maven-failsafe-plugin` like this:

`<plugin>`

`<groupId>org.apache.maven.plugins</groupId>`

`<artifactId>maven-failsafe-plugin</artifactId>`

`<version>3.0.0-M3</version>`

`<executions>`

`<execution>`

`<phase>integration-test</phase>`

`<id>integration-test</id>`

`<goals>`

`<goal>integration-test</goal>`

`</goals>`

`<configuration>`

`<trimStackTrace>false</trimStackTrace>`

`</configuration>`

`</execution>`

`</executions>`

`</plugin>`

Create the file `log4j.properties,` with the basic configuration, create it in the /`src/test/resources` folder. With content similar to the following:

`log4j.rootLogger=INFO, stdout`

`log4j.appender=org.apache.log4j.ConsoleAppender`

`log4j.appender.layout=org.apache.log4j.PatternLayout`

`log4j.appender.stdout=org.apache.log4j.ConsoleAppender`

`log4j.appender.stdout.layout=org.apache.log4j.PatternLayout`

`log4j.appender.stdout.layout.ConversionPattern=%r %p %c %x - %m%n`

`log4j.logger.org.microshed.testing=DEBUG`

We used as a basis the example offered by the official portal microshed and adapted to our previous example in which we used the class Country:

`public class Country {`

`@NotNull`

`private String id;`

`@NotNull`

`@Size(min = 5, max = 50)`

`private String name;`

`@JsonbCreator`

`public Country(@JsonbProperty("name") String name,`

`@JsonbProperty("id") String id) {`

`this.name = name;`

`this.id = id;`

`}`

`@Override`

`public boolean equals(Object obj) {`

`if (obj == null || !(obj instanceof Country))`

`return false;`

`Country other = (Country) obj;`

`return Objects.equals(id, other.id) && Objects.equals(name, other.name) ;`

`}`

`@Override`

`public int hashCode() {`

`return Objects.hash(id, name);`

`}`

`//Getters /Setters`

`}`

A class will be created to simulate the repository which can be a source of data e.g. MongoDB. It is a simple class that simulates the operations with the database:

`@ApplicationScoped`

`public class CountryRepository {`

`private final Map<String, Country> countryRepo = new HashMap<>();`

`public String save(String name, String id) {`

`Country country = new Country(name, id);`

`countryRepo.put(country.getId(), country);`

`return country.getId();`

`}`

`public Collection<Country> findAll() {`

`return countryRepo.values();`

`}`

`public Country getCountry(String id) {`

`Country foundCountry = countryRepo.get(id);`

`if (foundCountry == null) {`

`countryNotFound(id);`

`}`

`return foundCountry;`

`}`

`public void removeCountry(String id) {`

`Country toDelete = countryRepo.get(id);`

`if (toDelete == null) {`

`countryNotFound(id);`

`}`

`countryRepo.remove(id);`

`}`

`private void countryNotFound(String id) {`

`throw new NotFoundException("Country with id " + id + " not found.");`

`}`

`public void updateCountry(String id, Country p) {`

`Country toUpdate = getCountry(id);`

`if (toUpdate == null) {`

`countryNotFound(id);`

`}`

`countryRepo.put(id, p);`

`}`

`}`

The `CountryService.java` class shown below contains the `Rest` services we will consume from our test:

`@Path("/country")`

`@ApplicationScoped`

`@Produces(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)`

`@Consumes(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)`

`public class CountryService {`

`@Inject`

`CountryRepository countryRepository;`

`@PostConstruct`

`public void initPeople() {`

`}`

`@GET`

`public Collection<Country> getAllPeople() {`

`return countryRepository.findAll();`

`}`

`@GET`

`@Path("/{countryId}")`

`public Country getCountry(@PathParam("countryId") String id) {`

`return countryRepository.getCountry(id);`

`}`

`@POST`

`public String createCountry(@QueryParam("name") @NotEmpty @Size(min = 2, max = 50) String name, @QueryParam("id") String id) {`

`return countryRepository.save(name,id);`

`}`

`@POST`

`@Path("/{countryId}")`

`public void updateCountry(@PathParam("countryId") String id, @Valid Country p) {`

`countryRepository.updateCountry(id, p);`

`}`

`@DELETE`

`@Path("/{countryId}")`

`public void removeCountry(@PathParam("countryId") String id) {`

`countryRepository.removeCountry(id);`

`}`

`}`

Create the Testing class `JaxrsJsonIT.java` using the example name from the `MicroShed` portal you use the name you find most appropriate. Some important considerations:

*   `@MicroShedTest` notation to create the test.
*   `@Container`: Searches for containers specified in the Dockerfile.
*   `@RestClient`: Inject the Rest service we are using into the application.

Below is the source code for the class `JaxrsJsonIT.java:`

`@MicroShedTest`

`public class JaxrsJsonIT {`

`@Container`

`public static ApplicationContainer app = new ApplicationContainer()`

`.withAppContextRoot("/openlibertytest")`

`.withReadinessPath("/health/ready");`

`@RESTClient`

`public static CountryService countryService;`

`@Test`

`public void testCreateCountry() {`

`String id = countryService.createCountry("Brasil", "br");`

`assertNotNull(id);`

`}`

`@Test`

`public void testGetCountry() {`

`String id = countryService.createCountry("Colombia", "co");`

`Country country = countryService.getCountry(id);`

`assertEquals("Colombia", country.getName());`

`assertNotNull(country.getId());`

`}`

`@Test`

`public void testGetAll() {`

`String country1Id = countryService.createCountry("Panama", "pa");`

`String country2Id = countryService.createCountry("Colombia", "co");`

`String country3Id = countryService.createCountry("Brasil", "br");`

`Collection<Country> allCountry = countryService.getAll();`

`assertTrue(allCountry.size() >= 2);`

`}`

`@Test`

`public void testUpdate() {`

`String countryId = countryService.createCountry("Argentina", "ar");`

`Country originalCountry = countryService.getCountry(countryId);`

`assertEquals("Argentina", originalCountry.getName());`

`countryService.updateCountry(countryId, new Country("Spain", originalCountry.getId()));`

`Country updatedCountry = countryService.getCountry(countryId);`

`assertEquals("Spain", updatedCountry.getName());`

`}`

`}`

To run it, from the terminal enter the following command maven:

`mvn clean install.`

# [GenericContainer](#toc.xhtml_s201a)

Using the public static GenericContainer <?> is possible to define generic containers, such as MongoDB. With the method dependOn() you indicate that the main container does not start until the generic containers are started first. As shown in the following code segment:

`@Container`

`public static GenericContainer<?> mongo = new GenericContainer<>("mongo:3.4")`

`.withExposedPorts(27017)`

`.withNetworkAliases("testmongo");`

`@Container`

`public static ApplicationContainer app = new ApplicationContainer()`

`.withAppContextRoot("/openlibertytest")`

`.withReadinessPath("/health/ready")`

`.dependsOn(mongo);`

Some container methods allow obtaining information such as IP, port, etc:

`@Test`

`public void testMongoDB() {`

`mongo.start();`

`String connection = "mongodb://" + mongo.getContainerIpAddress() + ":" + mongo.getMappedPort(27017);`

`MongoClient mongoClient = MongoClients.create(connection);`

`MongoDatabase database = mongoClient.getDatabase("test");`

`MongoCollection<Document> collection = database.getCollection("country");`

`Document document = new Document("name", "Panama").append("id", "pa");`

`collection.insertOne(document);`

`Country country = convert(collection.find(eq("name", "Panama")));`

`assertEquals("Panama", country.getName());`

`}`

You can consult the examples of this book in the GitHub repository. It can be seen that Microservices tests are implemented in a very simple way. `Microshed` offers us a series of additional facilities such as JWT testing, `ApplicationEnvironment` that can be extended at the main Microshed site.

# [Continuous integration and Continuous Delivery (CI/CD)](#toc.xhtml_s202a)

A few years ago Martin Fowler defined Continuous Integration **[https://martinfowler.com/articles/continuousIntegration.html](https://martinfowler.com/articles/continuousIntegration.html)** *as Continuous Integration is a software development practice where members of a team integrate their work frequently, usually, each person integrates at least daily - leading to multiple integrations per day. Each integration is verified by an automated build (including test) to detect integration errors as quickly as possible. Many teams find that this approach leads to significantly reduced integration problems and allows a team to develop cohesive software more rapidly. This article is a quick overview of Continuous Integration summarizing the technique and its current usage.*

# [Jenkins](#toc.xhtml_s203a)

For years Jenkins **([https://www.jenkins.io/](https://www.jenkins.io/))**has become one of the main automation servers used for Continuous Integration. As explained on their website Jenkins allows you to automate the tasks of *building, testing and delivering or deployed software.*

It can be installed natively on the operating system, run it using a war file or in the way most is currently used using a Docker image. Many platforms incorporate Jenkins pipelines such as Cloudbeess, RedHat OpenShift among many others.

We will focus on a small application that uses pipeline. Jenkins pipeline is composed of a set of plugins that allow Jenkins to integrate continuous Pipeline within Jenkins. It is done using a simple text file called Jenkins file. You can find the official information on the site (**[https://www.jenkins.io/doc/book/pipeline/](https://www.jenkins.io/doc/book/pipeline/)),**

# [Continuous Delivery](#toc.xhtml_s205a)

It is a practice that has evolved a lot with the help of many tools to ensure the development of projects using mainly test. The **[https://continuousdelivery.com/](https://continuousdelivery.com/)** portal gives us the following definition: Continuous Delivery is the ability to get changes of all types—including new features, configuration changes, bug fixes, and experiments—into production, or into the hands of *users, safely,* and *quickly* in a sustainable way.

CI/CD is an important part of DevOps, the orchestration of containers is one of the current practices with more use in the development of applications today. Many cloud platforms offer us CI/CD Pipeline environments. For example Microsoft Azure allows us to use Azure Pipeline very easily:

Visit the main page **[https://github.com/marketplace/azure-pipelines](https://github.com/marketplace/azure-pipelines) and use the options available:**

# [Tekton Framework](#toc.xhtml_s206a)

Tekton is a framework based on Kubernetes that makes the creation of CI/CD systems more flexible. With it, it is possible to test various cloud providers by abstracting the details of the implementation. (**https://cloud.google.com/tekton/).**

The components are:

*   **Task**: These are the jobs that must be executed. They are like the Pod of a Kubernetes cluster.
*   **TaskRunner:** Executes the defined tasks.
*   **PipelineResources:** It defines the artifices that are used by a task.
*   **PipelineRun**: Defines the list of tasks to be executed.

The requirements are very basic:

*   **Install Kubernetes:**

    **`snap install kubectl --classic`**

    **`kubectl version`**

    To install it we follow the following steps:

    **`kubectl apply --filename https://storage.googleapis.com/tekton-releases/pipeline/latest/release.yaml`**

    The execution is done by:

    **`kubectl get pods --namespace tekton-pipelines`**

    The following examples are part of the official documentation that you can find at **[https://github.com/tektoncd/pipeline/blob/master/docs/tutorial.md](https://github.com/tektoncd/pipeline/blob/master/docs/tutorial.md)**

*   **Create a task:**

    `apiVersion: tekton.dev/v1alpha1`

    `kind: Task`

    `metadata:`

    `name: echo-hello-world`

    `spec:`

    `steps:`

    `- name: echo`

    `image: ubuntu`

    `command:`

    `- echo`

    `args:`

    `- "hello world"`

*   **Create a TaskRunner:**

    `apiVersion: tekton.dev/v1alpha1`

    `kind: TaskRun`

    `metadata:`

    `name: echo-hello-world-task-run`

    `spec:`

    `taskRef:`

    `name: echo-hello-world`

*   **Now create a PipelineResources:**

    `apiVersion: tekton.dev/v1alpha1`

    `kind: PipelineResource`

    `metadata:`

    `name: skaffold-git`

    `spec:`

    `type: git`

    `params:`

    `- name: revision`

    `value: master`

    `- name: url`

    `value: https://github.com/GoogleContainerTools/skaffold`

*   **Creating the Pipeline:**

    `apiVersion: tekton.dev/v1alpha1`

    `kind: Pipeline`

    `metadata:`

    `name: tutorial-pipeline`

    `spec:`

    `resources:`

    `- name: source-repo`

    `type: git`

    `- name: web-image`

    `type: image`

    `tasks:`

    `- name: build-skaffold-web`

    `taskRef:`

    `name: build-docker-image-from-git-source`

    `params:`

    `- name: pathToDockerFile`

    `value: Dockerfile`

    `- name: pathToContext`

    `value: /workspace/docker-source/examples/microservices/leeroy-web #configure: may change according to your source`

    `resources:`

    `inputs:`

    `- name: docker-source`

    `resource: source-repo`

    `outputs:`

    `- name: builtImage`

    `resource: web-image`

    `- name: deploy-web`

    `taskRef:`

    `name: deploy-using-kubectl`

    `resources:`

    `inputs:`

    `- name: source`

    `resource: source-repo`

    `- name: image`

    `resource: web-image`

    `from:`

    `- build-skaffold-web`

    `params:`

    `- name: path`

    `value: /workspace/source/examples/microservices/leeroy-web/kubernetes/deployment.yaml #configure: may change according to your source`

    `- name: yamlPathToImage`

    `value: "spec.template.spec.containers[0].image"`

As you can see from the examples, it is very simple to use Tekton.

# [Conclusion](#toc.xhtml_s207a)

This chapter shows how to create tests using `TestContainers` and `Microshed` and shows an example of CI/CD with Azure Pipeline. In addition to a brief introduction to the Tekton framework for creating CI/CD Pipeline.

Throughout the book there are introductions to various technologies, APIs and Frameworks used for the development of Jakarta Enterpise applications.
